---
title: Type system
description: The precise static-typing semantics contract for ry's R type checker
---

import { Tabs, TabItem } from '@astrojs/starlight/components';

Two principles explain most of the rules on this page. First, the checker prefers skipping a check
to giving a wrong answer: a construct it cannot describe becomes `Unknown`, which is compatible with
everything, so a gap means a skipped check rather than a false error, and [strict mode](#strict-mode)
shows where the gaps are. Second, every rule must be cheap enough to check on every keystroke, which
is why inference is Hindley–Milner, why your own functions cannot be overloaded, and why there is no
subtyping between nominal types. The [guide](/guide#step-2-type-your-code) introduces the same ideas
through examples.

`ry check` reports the type errors on this page only when typing is on, through `[check] typing = true`
in `ry.toml` or a `# typing: on` comment at the top of the file (see
[configuration](/reference/configuration)). Every output block here was produced that way. With
typing off, the checker still reports unresolved names, such as a misspelled column in a data.table
query's `i`, but no type errors.

## Typing comment syntax

Annotations are written in two ways that share one type grammar: in `#:` comments, described here,
and [inline](#inline-annotation-syntax), in the code itself. An example that has both spellings shows
them in tabs, and the tab you choose is remembered on every page of this site. A form that exists in
only one spelling shows a single block and says so. A `#:` comment sits directly above the
binding or expression it describes. Consecutive `#:` lines with no blank line between them form one
*annotation block*. Each annotation takes one of these forms:

- `#: TYPE` is a [checked annotation](#checked-annotations).
- `#: @trust TYPE` is a [trusted coercion](#trusted-coercions).
- `#: @if-unknown TYPE` is an [unknown-only coercion](#unknown-only-coercions).
- `#: @new NOMINAL_TYPE` is a [nominal introduction](#nominal-introduction).

After an annotation, a `#` starts a plain comment, so `#: double # amount in euros` is the type
`double` with a note. A second `#:` on the same line is an error, because it would otherwise be
read as that note and its type dropped: write each annotation on its own line.

A block is one of three kinds, which cannot be mixed: a single line in one of those forms, an
[expanded function annotation](#expanded-function-annotations) written as `@param` and `@return`
lines, or a definition block of `@type`, `@alias` and `@opaque` lines.

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
value: integer <- 1L

double_count <- function(count: integer) -> integer count + count
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: integer
value <- 1L

#: fn(count: integer) -> integer
double_count <- function(count) count + count
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

A block can attach at any statement depth. Inside a function body it annotates the assignment or the
block-final expression that follows it, which is how a function that builds a value of a named type
is written:

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
type Person = list{name: character}

make_person <- function(name: character) -> Person {
  @Person(list(name = name))
}
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: @type Person {list{name: character}}

#: fn(name: character) -> Person
make_person <- function(name) {
  #: @new Person
  list(name = name)
}
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

### Attachment

A block attaches to the expression that starts on the line directly after it. A braceless function
body, a braceless `if` branch, a parenthesised expression, and an argument are valid targets. In an
argument list the block types the argument's value, so a lambda gets its signature where it is passed:

```r
#: fn(m: matrix) -> double[]
row_sums <- function(m) apply(
  m, 1,
  #: fn(row: double[]) -> double
  function(row) sum(row)
)
```

When a block needs a target and has none, it is an error and the annotation does not apply. That
happens when:

- a blank line separates the block from the expression;
- a plain `#` comment separates them, or no expression follows at all. A roxygen2 `#'` line is a
  plain comment here, so the `#:` block goes below the roxygen block, directly above the definition;
- the block follows code on its own line, as in `x <- 1 #: integer` or `x <- 1; #: integer`,
  which would otherwise read as an annotation on the next line's statement;
- the block has no content beyond the `#:` marker.

A definition block, one that contains only `@type`, `@alias` and `@opaque` lines, attaches to nothing, and
neither does a `@strict` toggle, so the adjacency rules do not apply to them.

### Refused blocks

A block is refused as a whole when it mixes forms, repeats a directive other than `@param`, orders its directives wrongly, declares a
duplicate or unknown type parameter, or gives `@new` a payload that is not nominal. A refused block
reports its error and carries no typing payload, so a broken annotation does not cause follow-on
findings. An annotation that names an undeclared type, or writes `X[]` over an element that is not
atomic (`Rec[]` with `Rec` a record alias), is refused the same way, so the code it covers is
typed as if it had no annotation. A block the annotation grammar could not read at all, such as
the bare optional parameter in `fn(integer, [character])`, is refused silently, because the parse
error has already said what was wrong.

## Inline annotation syntax

An inline annotation writes a type into the code instead of a comment above it:

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
count: integer <- 0L

add <- function(x: integer, y: integer = 1L) -> integer {
  x + y
}

first <- function<T>(items: list[T]) -> T items[[1L]]
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: integer
count <- 0L

#: fn(x: integer, [y]: integer) -> integer
add <- function(x, y = 1L) {
  x + y
}

#: <T> fn(items: list[T]) -> T
first <- function(items) items[[1L]]
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

- `name: TYPE <- value` declares a binding's type. The `=` spelling, `name: TYPE = value`, works too.
- `name: TYPE` in a parameter list declares a parameter's type, before its default if it has one.
  `...: TYPE` declares the rest parameter.
- `-> TYPE` between the parameter list and the body declares a function's return type.
- `<T, U: numeric>` directly after `function` or `\` declares the function's type parameters, with
  the same names and constraints as the compact `<...>` binder.
- `type Name<T> = TYPE` and `alias Name<T> = TYPE` at the top level of a file define a
  [named type](#named-types-and-generic-application), like `#: @type Name<T> {TYPE}` and
  `#: @alias Name<T> {TYPE}`.
- `name: @trust TYPE <- value`, `name: @if-unknown TYPE <- value` and `name: @new Name <- value`
  write a binding's [coercion](#unknown-only-coercions) inline.
- `@Name(value)` and `@Name<ARGS>(value)` introduce a nominal type on a value, like `#: @new Name`
  on that value. The `(` must follow the type directly, and `value` may be any expression, including
  a record or tuple: `@Point((x = 1, y = 2))`.
- `name <- @pure function(...)` and `name <- @pure \(x) ...` declare the function pure, like a
  `#: @pure` line above the binding (see [purity](#purity)). Followed by `(`, `@pure(...)` is a
  nominal introduction instead.

Every `TYPE` is the type grammar of `#:` comments, so anything a `#:` type can say an inline type can
say, and a type reads the same written either way.

### Meaning

An inline annotation means exactly what the equivalent `#:` block means, because both lower to the
same annotation:

- A binding's type is a [checked annotation](#checked-annotations): `count: integer <- 0L` is
  `#: integer` above `count <- 0L`.
- A function's inline types form a [compact annotation](#compact-function-annotations) naming the
  annotated parameters, so `function(x: integer, y)` is `#: fn(x: integer)` over `function(x, y)`.
  [The formal list](#annotations-and-the-formal-list) rules apply unchanged, and an unannotated
  parameter keeps its inferred type.
- A parameter with a default is optional, so the inline form never needs `[name]`.
- `type`, `alias` and `opaque` definitions are the `@type`, `@alias` and `@opaque` definitions of
  the same name, in the same project-global namespace.
- A binding's directive, as in `x: @trust integer <- v`, is the same directive in a `#:` block above
  the binding. `@Name(value)` is `(value)` with `#: @new Name` on it.
- A function without `-> TYPE` infers its return type from its body, as an
  [elided return](#elided-return-types) on a definition does. A function type inside an inline
  annotation, such as `f: fn(integer)` or `callback: fn(integer)`, follows the `#:` rule for that
  position: `NULL` when nested, and inferred from the body when it is a binding's whole type over a
  function literal.

Because the signature sits on the function itself, a function that is not a statement can be
annotated too. `lapply(ids, function(id: integer) -> character format(id))` checks the lambda's
body against its signature and the call against its parameter, where a `#:` comment could not reach
it. When a binding's type and its function's own signature are both written, as in
`step: fn(x: integer) -> integer <- function(x: integer) -> integer x + 1L`, the signature types the
function and the binding's type is then checked against it like any other value.

### Where inline annotations parse

A parameter's type and a function's return type and type parameters can be written on any function
literal. A binding's type is recognized only where a statement starts, at the top level of a file or
directly inside `{ }`. Elsewhere, such as in a braceless `if` branch, `y: integer <- 1L` is R's
reading of `y:integer` as an assignment target, which is reported as a syntax error. A binding's type
continues onto the next line inside an open bracket, after a `|`, and onto a line that starts with
`|`.

`type`, `alias` and `opaque` start a definition only at the start of a statement and only when a
name follows them on the same line, so `type <- "x"`, `alias(y)` and every other use of them as ordinary names
keep their R meaning. A definition inside a function body or any other block parses, but is an
`annotation` error: definitions belong at the top level of a file. A definition ends at the end of
its line, and nothing but a comment may follow it there, so `type Id = integer; x <- 1` is a syntax
error. Its type continues onto the next line after `=`, after a `|`, inside an open bracket, and
onto a line that starts with `|`. No R statement starts with `|`, so a union may line
up its members:

```r
alias Shape =
  | :Circle(radius: double)
  | :Rect(width: double, height: double)
```

A leading `|` is allowed in any union, in a `#:` comment too.

A return type is read as far as the type grammar reaches, and the body starts after it. A list or
function type must hug its bracket, as the formatter writes it: `-> list{name: character}` is a
record type, while in `-> list {` and `-> fn (` the bracket starts the body.

A mistake in an inline type's grammar is a `syntax-error`, because the statement cannot be read
until it is fixed. A type that parses but means nothing, such as an unknown type name or a record
that mixes named and positional items, is an `annotation` error, as it is in a comment.

### Erasure to plain R

R's own parser rejects every inline annotation except a binding's, which R reads as an assignment to
`name:TYPE` through a `` `:<-` `` replacement function that base R does not define. So no working R
program contains one, and existing R code parses and behaves exactly as before.

`type`, `alias` and `opaque` definitions and `@Name(value)` are not R either: a definition is two names in a
row, and `@` cannot start an R expression. At the start of a line, `@Name` reads as a constructor
only when `(` or `<` follows the name, so a slot access broken across lines, `obj` then `@slot`,
keeps R's own syntax error.

Removing each inline annotation turns an annotated program into the plain R program it means:
delete a binding's or parameter's `: TYPE` (with its directive), a function's `<...>`, its
`-> TYPE`, and the `@Name` of `@Name(value)`. A type definition becomes its `#:` comment, line for
line, so the erased program still declares the type, and a definition paired with a `#:` block
directly above it is removed, because that block already declares it. Comments and `#:` blocks
stay. `count: integer <- 0L`
erases to `count <- 0L`, `function<T>(x: T) -> T x` to `function(x) x`, `@UserId(5L)` to `(5L)`,
and `type UserId = integer` to `#: @type UserId {integer}`.

`ry build --plain-r` keeps the types instead of deleting them: each statement's inline annotations
become a `#:` block above it, so `count: integer <- 0L` is written as `#: integer` over
`count <- 0L`, a function's signature as `#: <T> fn(x: T, [y]: double) -> T`, `@pure` as a
`#: @pure` line, and a statement's value `@Name(value)` as `#: @new Name`. An annotation with no
statement to sit above, such as the signature of a function passed as an argument, is erased
([the CLI reference](/reference/cli) lists the cases).

### Inline and comment annotations together

A statement can carry both a `#:` block and inline annotations. They combine one *slot* at a time,
where a slot is the binding's value, one parameter, the rest parameter, or the return:

- A slot only one of them types takes that type.
- A slot both type takes the inline type.
- A coercion (`@trust`, `@if-unknown`, `@new`) says how the value gets its type, so it governs the
  value wherever it is written, and the other side's type is only compared against it. When both
  sides are coercions, the inline one governs.

A slot typed both ways with different types is reported as `annotation-mismatch`, a warning at the
inline type that points at the comment's type, so the reader always learns which one ry uses.
Types are compared as types: an alias agrees with its expansion, and `integer | character` agrees
with `character | integer`. Type parameters pair by position, so `<T>` in the comment and `<U>` in
the code agree, and a binder whose constraint differs is reported the same way, with the inline
constraint winning. A positional parameter type in a compact `#:` annotation is compared with
the formal it pairs with.

A type definition pairs the same way. A `#:` definition directly above an inline definition of the
same name, with nothing but a line break between them, is one definition written twice. It counts
once, so it is not a duplicate, and the inline definition is the one that applies. When the two
differ in kind (`@type` against `alias` or `opaque`), in the number of type parameters, in a constraint, or in
the type itself, ry reports `annotation-mismatch` at the inline definition, pointing at the comment.
Type parameters pair by position, so `#: @alias Pair<T> {Tuple{T, T}}` and
`alias Pair<U> = Tuple{U, U}` agree. A `#:` definition anywhere else in the project with the same
name is a duplicate, as two `#:` definitions are.

This example deliberately uses both spellings on one function:

```r
#: fn(price: double, rate: double) -> double
apply_discount <- function(price: integer, rate) price * (1 - rate)
```

```text ry check typing
! the inline annotation gives parameter `price` the type `integer`, but the `#:` comment gives it
  `double`. ry uses the inline type; make the two agree, or delete one
```

### Turning inline annotations off

`[check] inline-annotations = false` in `ry.toml` turns inline annotations off, for code that must
also run under R. An annotation that is off types nothing, and each one is reported once as an
`inline-annotation` warning, because R cannot run that code. ry also warns once per run, and the
language server once per load, for as long as the setting stays off. A `#:` block on the same
statement then applies alone, and so does a paired `#:` definition. An inline type definition with
no `#:` partner still declares its name, so uses of it are not also reported as unknown types. A
`type` stays nominal, so a plain value is still not accepted where it is expected, but its
representation is `Unknown`. The setting also governs running code: `ry run` and `ry R` run
annotated code either way, and ry's runtime takes a function's signature from its `#:` block alone.

### Annotations at run time

`ry run` runs annotated code on ry's runtime and `ry R` on GNU R, which reads the erasure. ry's runtime
reads the annotations themselves and checks a function's parameter and return types at its
boundary, so a function called with the wrong kind of value fails where it was called instead of
somewhere inside it:

```r
scale <- function(values: double, by: double) -> double values * by
scale("a", 1)
```

```text ry run runtime=ry
Error in scale("a", 1) :
  argument `values` must be `double`, not `character`
```

**A signature counts the same wherever it is written.** The runtime resolves a function's
signature by the rules the checker uses ([together](#inline-and-comment-annotations-together)): a
type written on the function wins, then the function type on the name it is assigned to, then the
`#:` block [attached](#attachment) to its statement. The two functions below are checked exactly
like `scale` above, and a signature written entirely in ry types
[compiles to native code](#native-functions) in any of the three forms.

```r
#: fn(values: double, by: double) -> double
scale <- function(values, by) values * by

halve: fn(value: double) -> double <- function(value) value / 2

halve("a")
```

```text ry run runtime=ry
Error in halve("a") : argument `value` must be `double`, not `character`
```

GNU R reads a `#:` block as a comment, so a file annotated only in `#:` blocks runs under
`Rscript` without checks.

**`print` shows the annotations; `deparse` does not.** A function that keeps no source references,
such as one from an installed package or, by default, any function under `ry run`, prints from its
code, and `print` writes the signature's types into it. A generic function prints without its
type parameters (`function (y: T) -> T`). `deparse` and `dput` write the erasure, because they
produce R code that another R may read:

```r
scale <- function(values: double, by: double = 1) -> double values * by
scale
deparse(scale)
```

```text ry run runtime=ry
function (values: double, by: double = 1) -> double 
values * by
[1] "function (values, by = 1) " "values * by"
```

Two kinds of signature are not checked at run time:

- **A coercion.** `@trust`, `@if-unknown` and `@new` say how the value gets its type instead of
  declaring one to check, and a coercion governs the value wherever it is written. A function under
  one has no run-time checks, whatever types its literal writes, and it is not compiled.
- **A block the checker rejects.** A `#:` line that does not parse is a comment, and a block whose
  directives are out of order declares nothing. A block with an error that only the checker can
  see, such as an unknown type name, declares nothing to the checker, and the runtime checks the
  types it recognizes in it.

A signature that is only the name of a type alias, as in `#: Scaler` with
`#: @alias Scaler {fn(values: double, by: double) -> double}`, is checked and compiled like the
function type it names, because `ry run`, the console, `ry load`, `ry install`, `ry build` and a
parse of a file on ry's runtime write that type out in place of the name before the code runs. Code
parsed from text, such as `parse(text = )` or `eval(str2lang())`, is not rewritten, so there such a
function has no run-time checks and runs in the interpreter.

The console attaches a `#:` block the same way. A command that ends in a `#:` block is unfinished,
so the console reads the next line into the same command, and a blank line ends the wait and leaves
the block attached to nothing.

A check never changes what code that satisfies its annotations does, which the oracle pins by
comparing annotated cases on ry's runtime against GNU R running their erasure. The checks follow
these rules:

- **Parameters are checked when their value is computed.** An argument from R code stays a promise
  and is checked when it is forced, so laziness, `missing()`, and `substitute()` behave as in R, and
  an argument that is never used is never checked. An argument that is already a value, such as a
  constant, is checked when the function is entered.
- **Every `...` argument is checked against the rest parameter's type**, under the same rule. An
  argument forwarded through `...` to another typed function is checked against both functions'
  types when it is forced.
- **The return value is checked when the function returns**, including through `return()`, and
  before `on.exit` code runs.
- **Bindings are not checked.** `x: integer <- value` types `x` for the checker and is erased at
  run time. A literal in the bound value still runs as the number type the checker gave it (see
  [number literals](#number-literals)).
- **Type definitions and `@Name(value)` do nothing at run time.** A definition is skipped, and
  `@Name(value)` runs as `(value)`.
- **A check tests the value's storage type only.** `logical`, `integer`, `double`, `complex`,
  `character`, `raw`, and `NULL` admit values of that type, and each numeric type also admits the
  types below it in the promotion order, as the checker does. A parameter whose type admits
  `integer` also admits a `double` scalar holding a whole number, because the checker accepts a
  whole-number literal there (`f(10)` for `n: integer`) and storage cannot tell a literal from a
  computed value; a return value gets no such allowance. A list type admits any R list, a
  `Record{...}` or `Tuple{...}` type any ry record or tuple, a function type any function, and a
  union any of its members. Vector shape, element types, and
  attributes are not checked, and a type the storage type cannot decide (a class, a type
  parameter, `Any`, `data.frame`) is not checked at all. Under `ry run`, the console and an
  installed package, an alias is checked as the type it stands for, because ry's runtime reads
  each one-line annotation there with its aliases expanded. A check therefore fails only on a value that really does not
  have the annotated type.
- **A logical vector holding only `NA` arrives as `character` where the type is `character`.** A
  bare `NA` and a vector computed from no data, such as `ifelse()` over an empty test, are `logical`
  in R, but the checker lets them stand for any atomic type. `integer`, `double` and `complex`
  admit `logical` storage as it is, while a `character` parameter or return value converts such a
  value, keeping its attributes, so `typeof()` on it says `"character"` where GNU R running the
  erasure says `"logical"`. Every other check passes the value through unchanged, except the `I64`
  conversion described under [number literals](#number-literals).

  ```r
  label <- function(x: double[]) -> character[] ifelse(x > 0, "positive", "other")
  label(double())
  ```

  ```text ry run runtime=ry
  character(0)
  ```
- **An installed typed package's signatures name the types, not their aliases.** `ry install` and
  `ry build` on ry's runtime write each one-line parameter and return annotation of a typed package
  as the type it stands for ([CLI reference](/reference/cli#build-and-install)), so an installed
  function with a parameter `p: Point`, where `alias Point = Record{x: F64, y: F64}`, checks `p` as
  a record. An annotation that spans lines stays as written.
- **A function with no parameters whose body is a constant or a bare name is not checked**, because
  it has no language object to carry its signature.

Typed ry is strict, so a call from one typed function to another computes every argument the
caller wrote, left to right, before the callee runs, even an argument the callee never uses. This
is the one place where ry's runtime and GNU R running the erasure differ: GNU R computes such an
argument only when the callee uses it. A missing argument the caller passes on stays missing, so
`missing()` in the callee is `TRUE`, as in GNU R. An argument written in R code stays lazy, also
when a typed function passes it on as `...` (one it names, such as `..1`, is computed first): R
computes it where the body first reads the parameter, so an R call the body makes before that read can change what it is. With `y <- 1` and
`rebind <- function() y <<- 100`, `twice <- function(x: F64) -> F64 { rebind(); x * 2 }` gives 200
for `twice(y)`, compiled or not. Native code computes an R call argument, such as `step(as.double(i))`,
at entry when the body reads the parameter before it runs any other code, because R would compute
it at that point too. A read reaches into the arguments of a call of a builtin or a typed function,
which take their arguments left to right, but the call itself ends the reads: `x + sqrt(y)` reads
`x` and then `y`, and `sqrt(x) + y` reads only `x`. An `if`, `&&`, `||`, `<-`, `=` or `match` ends
the reads after its condition, value or scrutinee, and a call of an R closure, a name that is not
a parameter, a named or empty argument and `...` end them at once. Every parameter up to the last
of these arguments must be passed, since a default runs code. A body that is nothing but reads, such as
`function(x: F64) -> F64 x` or `return(x)`, reads nothing this way, so that
`ident(invisible(1))` keeps R's visibility. The argument's code runs in a frame for the call, after
the entry checks, and `swapped <- function(a: F64, b: F64) -> F64 b - a` computes `b`'s argument
before `a`'s. Otherwise the call runs in the interpreter, which computes the argument at the read.
Argument code that rebinds a function the body calls differs from R in one case: when the body
looked the function up before it read that argument, as `h(a)` does, R calls the function it found
and ry calls the new one.

```r
first <- function(a: double, b: double) -> double a
typed_caller <- function(x: double) -> double first(x, { cat("computed b\n"); 2 })
r_caller <- function(x) first(x, { cat("computed b\n"); 2 })
typed_caller(1)
r_caller(1)
```

```text ry run runtime=ry
computed b
[1] 1
[1] 1
```

A failed check is an ordinary R error, so `tryCatch()` and `try()` handle it. A parameter whose
check failed stays unevaluated, as it would after an error while computing it, so code that catches
the error and uses the parameter again computes it again and checks it again.

The checks belong to the function definition that carried the annotations. A closure built from
another function's `formals()` with a body of its own, as `rlang::new_function()` builds one, is a
new definition and is not checked.

Before running anything, `ry run` and `ry R` check the script and their console checks each command, with the
project's `ry.toml`. They print every `annotation-mismatch` and `inline-annotation` warning. In
[strict mode](/guide#strict-mode) they also print every error and refuse to run code that
has one: a script exits with status 1, and the console drops the command. A paste from a terminal
with bracketed paste is one command, so an error anywhere in it drops all of it. Other findings are left
to `ry check`, so they never change whether R code runs.

Each console command is checked against the commands that ran before it. It sees the names they
bound, the types they defined and the last `# typing:` directive among them, so a directive governs
the commands after it. A function body reads a global when the function is called, so it sees the
latest binding of the name. In strict mode, a command that rebinds a name in a way that breaks a
function an earlier command defined is refused, with the error shown in that function's command:

```r
> x: integer <- 1L
> f <- function() x + 1L
> x <- "a"
type-mismatch

  x `+` is not defined on `Str`. It takes a number
   --[<console>:1:17]
 1 | f <- function() x + 1L
   |                 ^^^^^^

  x strict mode found 1 error, so this code does not run
```

Line numbers in a console finding count from the start of its command. Ctrl-C while a paste runs
cancels the rest of the paste, and the cancelled lines are checked as if they were never typed.

## Types

### Atomic names

Types use R's own names: `logical`, `integer`, `double`, `complex`, `character`, `raw`, and `NULL`.
The names from other languages, such as `bool`, `int`, `float`, and `string`, are not accepted.

### Reserved constants

- `TRUE` and `FALSE` are `logical`.
- `NA` is `logical`, while `NA_integer_`, `NA_real_`, `NA_complex_`, and `NA_character_` are
  `integer`, `double`, `complex`, and `character`.
- `Inf` and `NaN` are `double`.
- An imaginary literal such as `1i` is `complex`.
- `NULL` is `NULL`.

### Vector shapes

Every atomic vector type comes in three shapes, written with a suffix on the atomic name:

```r
1L                  # integer: scalar-like, a vector of length one
c(1L, 2L)           # integer[]: array-like, a vector of unknown length
c(a = 1L, b = 2L)   # integer[named]: map-like, keyed by names
```

#### Vector coercions

- A scalar-like `T` coerces to an array-like `T[]`.
- A map-like `T[named]` coerces to an array-like `T[]`.
- An atomic type coerces up R's promotion order, `logical` < `integer` < `double` < `complex`, in
  every shape. That covers `logical` to `integer`, `integer[]` to `double[]`, `double[named]` to
  `complex[named]`, and compositions such as a scalar `integer` to `double[]`. The reverse never
  holds. `character` and `raw` are not part of the order, because R reaches `character` only through
  an explicit conversion.
- No other coercion between vector types is allowed unless a rule elsewhere on this page states it
  explicitly.

### List shapes

R has one list type, but programs use it in four roles, and the type system gives each role its own
type, because each role allows different checks:

- **tuple-like**, `list{T1, T2, ...}`: no names, a fixed number of positions, each with its own type.
- **list-like**, `list[T]`: no names, any length, one element type.
- **record-like**, `list{name: T, ...}`: named fields, a fixed set of them, each with its own type.
- **dict-like**, `list[named: T]`: names, any length, one element type.

The tuple-like and record-like shapes have a *fixed shape*: their positions or field names are part
of the type. The list-like and dict-like shapes are homogeneous collections, so no particular
position or name is part of the type. They mostly come from annotations and from coercing a
fixed-shape list.

A `list(...)` expression infers a fixed shape whenever its elements allow one:

- When all elements are unnamed, it is tuple-like, even when they share a type: `list()` is
  `list{}`, `list(1L, 2L, 3L)` is `list{integer, integer, integer}`, and `list(1L, "foo")` is
  `list{integer, character}`.
- When all elements are named and the names are known statically, it is record-like:
  `list(foo = 1L, bar = "foo")` is `list{foo: integer, bar: character}`.
- When named and unnamed elements are mixed, the names are dropped and it is list-like, so
  `list(1L, bar = "foo")` is `list[integer | character]`.
- When an argument is the enclosing function's `...`, the number and names of the elements are not
  known, so `list(...)` is `list[named: Unknown]`. Any field may be present, so reading or writing
  `dots$environment` is not reported. A list whose elements are not known at all, such as
  `as.list(environment())`, likewise says nothing about its names: `$` on a `list[Any]` or
  `list[Unknown]` reads `Any` or `Unknown` without an error.

R lets a list name be any string. A field name that is not a syntactic R name is written, and
rendered, in backticks, so `list(\`max size\` = 10L)` has the type `` list{`max size`: integer} ``.

#### List coercions

- Any list coerces to a list-like `list[T]` when every element is compatible with `T`.
- A record-like or dict-like list coerces to a dict-like `list[named: T]` when every value is
  compatible with `T`. So does a `list[Any]` or `list[Unknown]`, whose names are not known, such
  as `as.list(x)` on an untyped `x`.
- When `T` is still open, it becomes the union of the element types, so `lapply(list(1L, "a"), f)`
  passes `integer | character`.
- Otherwise the reverse is not allowed: a `list[T]` does not coerce back into a tuple-like, record-like, or
  dict-like list, and a `list[named: T]` does not coerce back into a record-like one.

Two closed records are compatible when they declare the same field names and each field's type is
compatible with the field of the same name on the other side. Fields pair by name, so their order
does not matter: `list(label = "a", id = 1L)` satisfies `list{id: integer, label: character}`.

When a record is rejected, the finding names the one field that failed rather than printing both
whole types: a field both sides declare whose types do not fit, a field the expected type declares
that the value lacks, or a field the value has that the expected type does not declare. For a nested
record, the finding names the path, outermost field first, as in `retry.count`.

#### Open records

A record type ending in `..` is open: `list{tick: fn() -> NULL, ..}` asks for a `tick` field and
accepts a record with more fields. It types a dependency by what the code uses from it, so a fake
built for a test may carry extra fields:

```r
#: fn(t: list{tick: fn() -> NULL, ..}) -> NULL
run <- function(t) t$tick()
run(list(tick = function() NULL, finished = TRUE))   # accepted
run(list(tock = function() NULL))                    # error: expected a field `tick` here, and this list has `tock` instead
```

A closed record never takes `..` implicitly, so `list{id: integer}` still reports a surplus or
misspelled field. Through an open record, `$` and `[[` read the listed fields by name. A name it
does not list reads as `Unknown` (the field may or may not be there), except a near miss of a
listed field, which is reported as a typo. A positional `[[` and a `[` subset read `Unknown`,
because the listed fields need not be the first ones. An open record does not pass its unlisted
fields through: `fn(t: list{tick: fn() -> NULL, ..}) -> list{tick: fn() -> NULL, ..}` returns
only what it lists. Every item of an open record is named. A `data.frame{...}` or
`data.table{...}` type lists every column, and only ry's own `Record{...}` and `DataFrame{...}`
types pass unlisted fields or columns through, with a [row](#rows).

#### The empty list

`list()` is the empty tuple `list{}`. It has no element whose type or name could conflict, so it is
compatible with `list[T]` and `list[named: T]` alike, which makes `function(options = list())` a
usable default for a `list[named: T]` parameter. A record type with required fields still rejects
it, because those fields really are missing.

Code starts from `list()` and fills it later far more often than it reads from a list it knows is
empty, so any element read from `list{}` is `Unknown`: `out <- list(); out[[1]]` and
`opts <- list(); opts$force` are not reported. Inside a union, an empty-list member contributes
`NULL` to a field read instead, as [`$` on a union](#indexing) describes.

### Environments

`new.env()`, `environment()` and `globalenv()` have the type `environment`, whose bindings are not
typed: `$` on it reads `Unknown`. An `environment{name: TYPE, ...}` type lists the bindings an
environment may hold, which types the mutable state R code keeps in one, such as a counter or a
cache:

```r
count <- function() -> integer {
  #: environment{count: integer}
  calls <- new.env()
  calls$count <- 0L
  calls$count <- calls$count + 1L
  calls$count            # integer
}
```

`new.env()` fits any `environment{...}` type, and a typed environment passes wherever an
`environment` is expected. An environment is mutable, so a write never changes its type:
`calls$count <- "many"` is an error against `integer`, and a binding the type does not list can be
neither written nor read (`calls$total <- 1L` is an error that asks to add `total: TYPE`). `$` and
`[[` with a literal name read the listed bindings. Two typed environments fit each other only when
they list the same bindings with the same types, because a write through either one changes both.
The type lists every binding, so it cannot end in `..`.

### `NULL`

The literal `NULL` has the type `NULL`, which is the unit type of this type system and is
incompatible with every other type. An empty block is also `NULL`.

A variable whose every visible write is `NULL` is a placeholder for a value written where the
checker cannot see, such as lifecycle's `msg <- NULL # trick R CMD check` before `msg %<~% ...`,
or a top-level flag that `assign()` fills in later. Passing such a variable as an argument, or
testing it as a condition, is not reported. A parameter whose type is `NULL` and the literal
`NULL` stay checked, so `report(NULL)` against a `character[]` parameter is an error.

A local whose every visible write is a bare `NA`, and that a closure fills with `<<-`, is the same
placeholder spelled as a missing value, as in knitr's `pattern.end <- NA` before a nested function
assigns the pattern. Its reads are not checked either.

### `Any` and `Unknown`

`Any` and `Unknown` are both compatible with every type, in both directions. They differ in where
they come from, and therefore in what they mean.

`Any` is an explicit opt-out from type checking. It comes from an annotation you wrote or from a
standard-library declaration. The shipped declarations use it where any precise type would reject
calls R accepts, so seeing `Any` in a hover is not by itself a sign of a problem.

`Unknown` means the checker could not work out a more specific type, because of an unsupported or
partly supported construct, an unresolved name, or too little information. It is not an opt-out: it
records a gap in the checker's knowledge, and a value of that type flows into a `double` parameter
without complaint.

The difference in intent shows in two places. [`@if-unknown`](#unknown-only-coercions) supplies a
type where one is missing, so it applies to an `Unknown` and is refused on an `Any`, which already
says not to check the value. [Strict mode](#strict-mode) reports where an `Unknown` arose, and treats
an `Any` as an intentional opt-out.

### Type parameters, aliases, and nominal types

A type expression can bind type parameters with a leading binder, written `<T> TYPE` or
`<T, U> TYPE`:

- `<T> list[T]`
- `<T> list{ value: T }`
- `<T, U> fn(T) -> U`
- `<T> fn(T) -> T | NULL`

A type parameter may appear inside a structural type, a function type, and the vector suffix forms:
`list[T]`, `list{ value: T }`, `fn(T) -> T`, `T | NULL`, `T[]`, and `T[named]`.

Binders are rank-1: a binder is allowed only at the outermost level of a type expression, never
nested inside another type, and never inside parentheses. A directive's `{...}` payload does not
count as the outermost level, so the expanded form declares its type parameters with `@forall`, and
a named type declares them on its name, as in `@type Pair<T>`. All three of these are refused:

- `fn(f: <T> fn(T) -> T) -> integer`
- `list{ value: <T> list[T] }`
- `@param f {<T> fn(T) -> T}`

A refused binder is reported once. The type is then read as though the binder had not been written,
and the block carries no typing payload.

A function signature's type parameters are in scope in the function's body, so an annotation there
can name them. The body sees each parameter as one fixed type, the one the caller chose, so a value
of another type is an error even when it would fit some other choice:

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
twice <- function<T: numeric>(y: T) -> T {
  doubled: T <- y + y
  doubled
}
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: <T: numeric> fn(y: T) -> T
twice <- function(y) {
  #: T
  doubled <- y + y
  doubled
}
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

In `#: <T, U> fn(x: T, y: U) -> T`, the body annotation `#: T` on `kept <- y` reports "expected
`T`, found `U`". A nested function reads the type parameters of every signature around it, and a
signature only ry inferred binds no name, so `#: T` in an unannotated function is an unknown type.

#### Constraints

A binder name may carry a constraint, written `NAME: CONSTRAINT`, as in
`<T: numeric> fn(values: T) -> T`. These are the constraints:

- `numeric` admits `integer` and `double`, scalar or vector, and any class whose arithmetic takes
  two of its values, which an [operator method](#operator-methods-on-a-class) such as
  `"+.Money" <- function(e1: Money, e2: Money)` declares.
- `arithmetic` admits everything `numeric` does and any class that declares an arithmetic operator
  method at all, such as `Date`, whose `+.Date` adds a number to a date. `add_them(day, day)` under
  `<T: numeric> fn(a: T, b: T) -> T` reports "expected a numeric value (`integer` or `double`) or a
  class with arithmetic between two of its values, found `Date`", as R stops on `day + day`.
- `num` admits everything `numeric` does and the ry numbers `I64` and `F64`.
- `arithmetic or number` admits everything `arithmetic` does and `I64` and `F64`.
- `arithmetic or F64` admits everything `arithmetic` does and `F64`.
- `numeric or F64` admits everything `numeric` does and `F64`.
- `atomic` admits one of the six atomic scalar types: `logical`, `integer`, `double`, `complex`,
  `character`, and `raw`.
- `key` admits a scalar `logical`, `integer`, `double` or `character`, the types a dictionary key
  or set element may have. `complex` and `raw` are left out.
- `scalar numeric` admits a scalar `integer` or `double`, which is both bounds at once. It is what an
  endpoint of `:` infers, so `function(n) 1:n` is `<T: scalar numeric> fn(n: T) -> double[]`.
- `item` admits what a [`List`](#lists) can hold: a number, a `Bool`, a `Str`, or another ry value.
  `List.from_r` uses it to type a `list[T]` argument.
- `eq` admits every value but a function or an environment: the values `==` compares.
- `comparable` admits the R values a comparison takes: an atomic value other than `complex` or
  `raw` in any vector shape, a list, which R compares element by element, and a class.
- `equatable` admits what `comparable` does and `complex` and `raw`, which R tests for equality but
  does not order.
- `ordered` admits the ry values with an order: `I64`, `F64` and `Str`. A number literal passed for
  an `ordered` parameter is a ry number, and a string literal a `Str`.
- `comparable or number` admits everything `comparable` does and `I64` and `F64`.
- `comparable or Str` admits everything `comparable` does and `Str`.
- `comparable or ordered` admits everything `comparable` does and what `ordered` does.
- `equatable or number` and `equatable or Str` admit everything `equatable` does and the ry numbers
  or `Str`.

The constraints named `... or ...` join an R constraint and a ry one, and they are what
[unannotated arithmetic](#unannotated-values-in-arithmetic) and comparisons infer, with a result
written `Promoted<T>`, `Subtracted<T>` or `Compared<T>` when R and ry give different results:

| Type | `T` a ry value | `T` a class | `T` another R value |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Promoted<T>` | `T` | `T`, for a class with arithmetic | `double` |
| `Subtracted<T>` | `T` | `T`, for a class with arithmetic between two of its values | `double` |
| `Compared<T>` | `Bool`, or `List<Bool>` for a `List` | `logical` | `logical` |

So `function(x) x * 2` is `<T: arithmetic or number> fn(x: T) -> Promoted<T>`: it gives an `I64`
for an `I64`, as `x * 2` on an `I64` does in ry, a `double` for an `integer`, as R does, and a
`matrix` for a `matrix`, because R keeps the class through its arithmetic methods.
`Subtracted<T>` is the difference of two values of one type, so it gives a `double` for two dates,
whose difference R makes a `difftime`. The three may be written in an annotation too.

A class keeps its type under `Promoted<T>` when it declares any arithmetic method, whichever
operator the method covers. This is a known gap: R refuses some of these operations (`x * 2` on a
`Date`, `/` between two `difftime`s), and ry types them as the class instead of reporting them.

Any other constraint name is an annotation error, and the error lists the ones that exist. An
argument whose type violates a constraint is a type error at the call, and the message names the
constraint: `middle("a")` under `<N: scalar numeric>` reports "expected a scalar numeric value
(`integer` or `double`), found `character`". A printed signature names a declared type parameter
as the annotation spells it, and an inferred one `T`, `U` and so on in order of appearance. A
call's message prints the parameter as the fresh variable the call instantiated, so `wrap(gh)`
under `<R> fn(f: fn(...: double) -> R) -> R` expects `fn(...: double) -> T`. A union satisfies a
constraint when every member does, so `double | integer` satisfies `numeric`, and an undetermined
member takes the constraint: a `NULL` default replaced by a number, `T | double`, passed where a
number is needed makes `T` a number. A vector `U[]`
satisfies `numeric` when its element is a number, and an undetermined element becomes a
`scalar numeric` one. A constrained value that meets a union takes the union whole when the
constraint admits every member, so a number meeting `integer | double` stays `integer | double`.
Otherwise it takes the first member it fits, so
`function(n) if (length(n) == 1L) half(n) else lapply(n, half)`, where `half` needs a number, gives
`n` the type `T[]` from `lapply`'s `list[T] | T[] | NULL`, with `T: scalar numeric`.

#### Type bounds

A binder may carry a type instead of a constraint, written `NAME: TYPE`. The binder then stands for
one type that fits its bound, and the signature can name that type again:

```r
#: <T, U: list[T] | T[]> fn(path: U) -> U
normalize <- function(path) {
  vapply(path, nchar, 1L)
  path
}
one <- normalize("a")              # character
many <- normalize(list("a", "b"))  # list{character, character}
```

Written `fn(path: list[T] | T[]) -> list[T] | T[]`, the signature would return the whole union to
every caller. The body may use the parameter wherever its bound fits, and an argument outside the
bound, such as `normalize(sum)`, is a type error at the call. A bound whose every member meets a
constraint meets it too, so under `<N: integer | double> fn(x: N) -> N` the body `x + x` is
arithmetic and has the type `N`. A bound with a `logical` member does its arithmetic as the bound
itself, because R promotes the `logical` member: under `<N: integer | logical> fn(x: N) -> N`,
`x + x` is `integer`, and returning it as `N` is a type error. `@forall T, U: list[T] | T[]` and
`function<T, U: list[T] | T[]>(path: U) -> U` declare the same binders. A bound that is a single
name reads as a constraint, so a type bound is a union or a compound type such as `list[T]`.

Inference gives an unannotated parameter the same shape when it meets a union and flows to the
return: the `normalize` above infers exactly the declared signature. A parameter that does not
reach the return keeps the union, as in `<T> fn(path: list[T] | T[]) -> integer`. A parameter
that meets two unions keeps the members of the first that fit the second, so `integer | logical`
and then `character | logical` leave `logical`, and two unions with no common member are a type
error at the second use.

A constraint limits what a caller may instantiate `T` with, and the annotated body may rely on it.
Under `<T: numeric> fn(x: T) -> T` the body may use `x` as a number. Arithmetic between two different
constrained parameters, `a / b` under `<T: numeric, U: numeric> fn(a: T, b: U) -> double`, is
`double`, R's widest numeric, because neither parameter's type fixes the other's. The same holds for
any two distinct type parameters, and for an unannotated parameter that a scalar use bounds, such as
`n` in `for (i in 1:n)`: `x + n` is `double`, so a call passing `1L` and `2.5` checks. A body under a plain `<T>` that
does arithmetic is a type error, and `atomic` does not imply `numeric`. Under
`<T: arithmetic> fn(x: T) -> T`, `x + 1` checks and `x + x` is a type error that says to declare
`<T: num>`, because a `T` such as `Date` adds a number and not another `Date`. Unary minus asks
`num` too, as R has no `-` on a single `Date`. Under `<T: num>`, `x - x` is a `T`, because `num`
admits no class, and `x / x` is a type error that says to declare `<T: numeric>`, because an `F64`
divides to an `F64` and not to the `double` R's `/` gives.

Using a type parameter as a vector element, `T[]`, imposes the `atomic` bound, because a vector holds
only atomic elements. This is what makes element-preserving signatures possible. With
`keep : <T> fn(x: T[]) -> T[]`, `keep(c("b", "a"))` is `character[]` and `keep(c(1L))` is
`integer[]`, while a list argument cannot bind `T` at all. Several rules follow from the bound:

- A scalar argument coerces into a generic vector parameter and binds the element, so
  `<T> fn(x: T[])` called with `2.5` binds `T := double`.
- `[[` on a generic vector `T[]` extracts `T`.
- An arithmetic operator on a `T[]` operand also requires the element to be numeric, so the variable
  then holds both bounds. The result keeps the element, so `keep(x) + 1L` is still `T[]`, unless a
  `double` operand promotes the result to `double[]`.
- A comparison on a `T[]` operand yields `logical[]` and constrains nothing beyond the bound.

A bound that can no longer be satisfied is a type error at the expression that imposed it, whether
that means binding an element variable to a type that is not atomic or requiring a `character`
element to be numeric.

Writing `X[]` is an error when `X` is neither an atomic type nor a type parameter. Records,
functions, and nominal types are all such an `X`, and `list[X]` is how to write a list of them. An
alias expands first, so `Id[]` with `@alias Id {integer}` is fine, while an alias of a record is
refused.

#### Named types and generic application

Named types are declared with `@type`, `@alias` and `@opaque`, and each may take parameters, as in
`NAME<T, U>`:

- `type NAME = TYPE`, or `#: @type NAME {TYPE}`, declares a *nominal type* whose representation is
  `TYPE`.
- `alias NAME = TYPE`, or `#: @alias NAME {TYPE}`, declares a *structural alias* for `TYPE`.
- `opaque NAME = TYPE`, or `#: @opaque NAME {TYPE}`, declares an [opaque type](#opaque-types): a
  nominal type whose representation only the file that declares it can see.

The three forms share one namespace, so reusing a name under any of them is an error, except for a
`#:` definition paired with the inline one directly below it
([inline and comment annotations together](#inline-and-comment-annotations-together)). A definition
is allowed only at the top level of a file. Definitions are project-global, so a reference need
not come after the declaration; [type names](#type-names) has the scoping rules.

The declared type parameters are in scope only within `TYPE`, where they shadow any project-global
type of the same name. A type parameter is not itself a generic, so applying type arguments to one,
as in `Wrap<integer>` where `Wrap` is a parameter, is an annotation error rather than a reference to
the shadowed global.

A named generic is applied with angle brackets, as in `Box<integer>`, and the application must match
the declaration's arity:

- Applying the wrong number of type arguments is an error at the applied name. For `Pair<T, U>`,
  `Pair<integer, character>` is valid, while `Pair<integer>` and `Pair<integer, character, double>`
  are errors.
- Applying type arguments to a declaration that is not generic, such as `Meters<integer>`, is an
  error.
- A bare reference to a generic, such as `Box` without arguments, is an error everywhere except after
  [`@new`](#nominal-introduction).
- A wrongly applied name then compares like `Unknown`, so the one arity error never cascades into
  mismatches between values.

A type definition cannot take the name of a built-in type: an atomic or ry scalar name (`double`,
`I64`, `Str`), `Any`, `Unknown`, `NULL`, a table type (`DataFrame`, `LazyFrame`, `GroupBy`,
`LazyGroupBy`), `Record`, `Tuple`, `Promoted`, `Subtracted`, `Compared`, or the standard
library's `List`, `Dict`, `Set` and `Expr`. Annotations and checks read such a name as the built-in type, so a definition
with it would conflict, and ry reports it as an `annotation` error at its name. The standard library's `Result` is
an alias, which a project's own definition of `Result` replaces.

#### Type aliases

An alias is purely structural: writing its name is the same as writing the type it stands for. It
creates no new identity, and may appear anywhere a type may, including inside a larger type. An
alias whose expansion reaches itself, directly or through other aliases, is an error reported at
each declaration on the cycle, whether or not an annotation uses it. An annotation that uses such an
alias checks nothing. A recursive type, such as a logger whose setters return the logger for
chaining, is a nominal type instead: `#: @type Logger {list{set_threshold: fn(level: character) -> Logger}}`
names the type, so `log$set_threshold("info")$set_threshold(3)` is checked at each call.

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
alias Box<T> = list{ value: T }

value: Box<integer> <- list(value = 1L)
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: @alias Box<T> {list{ value: T }}

#: Box<integer>
value <- list(value = 1L)
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

#### Nominal types

A nominal type creates a fresh type identity, even when another nominal type has exactly the same
representation. Its name may appear anywhere a type expression may.

- A nominal type is compatible with itself.
- Two different nominal types are not compatible with each other, even when their representations
  are identical.
- A structural value is not compatible with a nominal type unless [`@new`](#nominal-introduction)
  introduces it.
- A value of a nominal type *is* compatible with its representation type, so it can be passed to any
  function written for the representation, while `@new` is the only place the type's invariant is
  established. An [opaque type](#opaque-types) also restricts where `@new` may appear.
- A nominal type whose representation is another nominal type extends it, the way R's class vector
  `c("TradeDate", "Date")` does. With `@type TradeDate {Date}`, a `TradeDate` passes wherever a
  `Date` is expected, and its operators and `[` dispatch to `TradeDate`'s own methods first and then
  to `Date`'s, so `trade - trade` is a `difftime` through `-.Date`. The stubs declare `data.table`
  over `data.frame` and `ggplot` over `gg` the same way.
- When an operator, an indexing form, or a loop needs a structural shape, a nominal value projects to
  its representation type. The result of the projection is structural, not nominal. A union of
  nominal types projects member by member, so `s$radus` on `Circle | Square` is checked against
  both representations.
- A nominal type carries no runtime class, so `is.list()` cannot tell `Circle` from `Square`. A
  field test can: `is.null(s$radius)` drops, on its false edge, each member whose representation
  has no such field, and keeps the others as the nominal types they are. After
  `if (is.null(s$radius)) stop("not a circle")`, `s` is `Circle`. Tags (`:Circle(radius: double)`)
  are the discriminated form when the representation is yours to choose.

In this example, `Person` and `Pet` are distinct and incompatible. The annotation on `shape` is valid
because a nominal value satisfies its representation, and the final call is an error because a
structural value does not satisfy the nominal type:

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
type Person = list{ name: character, age: double }
type Pet = list{ name: character, age: double }

person <- @Person(list(name = "bob", age = 20))

shape: list{ name: character, age: double } <- person

get_name <- function(value: Person) -> character value$name

get_name(list(name = "bob", age = 20))
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: @type Person {list{ name: character, age: double }}
#: @type Pet {list{ name: character, age: double }}

#: @new Person
person <- list(name = "bob", age = 20)

#: list{ name: character, age: double }
shape <- person

#: fn(value: Person) -> character
get_name <- function(value) value$name

get_name(list(name = "bob", age = 20))
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

Projection is what makes field access and arithmetic work on a nominal value. In `get_name`,
`value$name` is `character` because `$` sees the representation. With `@type Meters {double}`,
`height + height` is `double`: arithmetic projects `Meters` to `double`, and the result does not keep
the nominal identity.

##### Abstract nominal types

An *abstract* nominal type has no representation to project. The standard-library stubs declare
`factor`, `connection`, `Date`, and other types the grammar cannot describe structurally as a bare
`@type NAME` (see [stubs](/development/authoring-stubs)). For these types:

- `$`, `[`, and `[[` are accepted and give `Unknown`, because the R objects behind these classes
  commonly support access that depends on values.
- The access is not checked further: there is no field-existence, index-count, or index-type check.
- Every such access is an unsupported construct under [strict mode](#strict-mode).
- Arithmetic and every other structural requirement remain type errors, unless the class declares
  the matching [operator method](#operator-methods-on-a-class). The nominal identity itself checks
  like any other nominal type.

`data.frame` is not abstract: the stubs declare it as `@type data.frame {list[named: Unknown]}`, the
named list of untyped columns it is at run time. A data frame is therefore accepted wherever a
named list is, as in `lapply(frame, f)` and `vapply(frame, is.numeric, logical(1))`. The stubs also
declare its methods, so `frame[rows, cols]` is `Any` (R keeps the frame or drops it to a column
depending on the indices), arithmetic such as `frame / 100` keeps the `data.frame`, and a comparison
such as `frame > 0.5` is a `matrix`.

`data.frame(...)` and `data.table(...)` know the columns they build, so their result is a
`data.frame{...}` or a `data.table{...}`, whose columns are written as a [DataFrame](#dataframes)'s:

```r
frame <- data.frame(id = 1:3, price = c(1, 2, 3))   # data.frame{id: integer[], price: double[]}
frame$price                                          # double[]
frame$volume                                         # error: the frame `frame` has no column `volume`
```

A column takes its argument's name, or a bare variable's. An argument R spreads over several columns
(a list, a matrix, another frame), a forwarded `...`, a repeated or non-syntactic name, and
`stringsAsFactors = TRUE` or `keep.rownames = TRUE` leave the columns unknown. `frame$x <- v` and
`frame[["x"]] <- v` add or replace a column. Any other write that may rename or retype columns
(`names(frame) <- v`, `frame[, "x"] <- v`, data.table's `set` and `setattr`) forgets them, while
`setDT` and `setDF` change the class and keep them. Everywhere else a `data.frame{...}` is a
`data.frame`: it passes to a `data.frame` parameter, and `[` and arithmetic dispatch on the class.
A data frame built any other way, such as by `read.csv` or `merge`, has unknown columns, so a column
read such as `frame$amount` is `Unknown`, and strict mode reports it where it is read.

#### Opaque types

An *opaque type* is a nominal type whose representation is private to the file that declares it.
Inside that file it is an ordinary [nominal type](#nominal-types). In every other file, test files
included, the type can be named, passed, returned and stored, but not built or taken apart, so the
declaring file's functions are the only way to make or inspect one. Its invariant then holds
everywhere, not only where `@new` is written.

Outside the declaring file, each of these is a `type-mismatch` error that points back at the
declaration:

- `@new` of the type, or `@Name(value)`.
- `$`, `[` and `[[`, read or written, including `x$value <- v`.
- A `for` loop over the value.
- A `match` arm that destructures the value. A wildcard or a bare name still matches it.
- Passing the value where its representation is expected, and arithmetic on it, because the
  representation is not visible to project it to.

The check covers a value whose type is the opaque type itself, a union containing it, or `NULL` or
an optional of it. R functions that take any value still accept it, so `length(x)`,
`attr(x, "unit") <- "m"` and `unclass(x)` are allowed: they leave the type system, as they do for
every other value. The restriction is static only. At run time an opaque value is its
representation, like any nominal value.

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
# R/units.R
opaque Meters = list{value: double}

meters <- function(value: double) -> Meters {
  if (value < 0) stop("negative length")
  @Meters(list(value = value))
}

in_feet <- function(length: Meters) -> double length$value * 3.28

# R/use.R
in_feet(meters(2))                       # fine
@Meters(list(value = -1))                # error: only the declaring file can create one
read_it <- function(length: Meters) -> double length$value   # error: the representation is hidden
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
# R/units.R
#: @opaque Meters {list{value: double}}

#: fn(value: double) -> Meters
meters <- function(value) {
  if (value < 0) stop("negative length")
  #: @new Meters
  result <- list(value = value)
  result
}

#: fn(length: Meters) -> double
in_feet <- function(length) length$value * 3.28

# R/use.R
in_feet(meters(2))                       # fine

#: fn(length: Meters) -> double
read_it <- function(length) length$value # error: the representation is hidden
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

The error on `read_it` reads:

```
  x `Meters` is opaque, so its representation is hidden outside the file that declares it. Use that file's functions instead
   --[R/use.R:2:29]
 1 | #: fn(length: Meters) -> double
 2 | read_it <- function(length) length$value
   |                             ^^^^^^
  `->   > the type is declared opaque here
         --[R/units.R:1:1]
```

A script that declares an opaque type sees it like the declaring file does, because a script's
definitions are its own.

#### Type-argument variance

Two applications of the same generic nominal type, such as `Box<integer>` and `Box<integer | NULL>`,
are compared one type argument at a time. Each parameter's *variance* follows from where it occurs
in the representation:

- **Covariant** positions keep the checking direction. They are a function's return, a direct
  occurrence, and an element of a container or structural type (a `list` item, a `list{...}` field,
  a tuple item, a vector element, or a union member). So `Box<integer>` is accepted where
  `Box<integer | NULL>` is expected.
- A function **parameter** position is contravariant and flips the direction. Given
  `@type Handler<T> {fn(value: T) -> NULL}`, a `Handler<integer | NULL>` is accepted where a
  `Handler<integer>` is expected, but not the other way around, because then a `NULL` could reach a
  function that only accepts `integer`.
- A parameter that occurs in both kinds of position is **invariant**, and its arguments must match
  exactly. Given `@type Cell<T> {list{ get: T, set: fn(value: T) -> NULL }}`, `Cell<integer>` and
  `Cell<integer | NULL>` are incompatible in both directions.
- A parameter that does not occur at all constrains nothing, and accepts any argument.

A type parameter inside a nested generic application, such as the `T` of `Sink<T>` within
`@type Outer<T> {Sink<T>}`, is invariant: the inner type's own variance does not compose with the
outer direction. When a generic nominal has no visible definition, every argument is checked
invariantly, which errs toward rejecting rather than toward accepting an unsound widening. The
standard library's `List<T>`, `Dict<K, V>` and `Set<T>` are the exception: they are immutable
values, so every parameter is covariant, and `[:Cash]`, a `List<:Cash>`, is accepted where a
`List<:Bond | :Cash>` is expected.

R's lists and vectors are mutable, yet their element positions are treated as covariant. A sound
mutable container would have to be invariant, which would break structural coercions such as scalar
to vector and `T` into `T | NULL`.

### Union types

A union type `A | B | ...` describes a value that has one of the member types. It may have any number
of members, any type may be a member, and it can be written anywhere a type can. `T | NULL` is the
nullable form of `T`. A union records the shapes a value can take without merging or coercing its
members:

- `integer | character`
- `character[] | NULL`
- `fn(count: integer | NULL) -> character | logical | NULL`
- `(fn() -> integer) | NULL`, a function returning `integer`, or `NULL`

A type may be parenthesized for grouping, where `(TYPE)` means exactly `TYPE`. In
`fn() -> integer | NULL` the `->` extends over the whole union, so an optional callback has to be
written `(fn() -> integer) | NULL`, which is also how it renders.

#### Union normalization

Unions are kept in one normal form, so equivalent spellings mean the same type and render the same
way. Every union, written or inferred, is normalized:

- A member that is itself a union is flattened, so `(A | B) | C` becomes `A | B | C`.
- Repeated members collapse, keeping the first, so `integer | character | integer` becomes
  `integer | character`.
- Member order does not affect meaning. Rendering keeps first-occurrence order, except that `NULL`
  always renders last.
- A union whose members collapse to one type is that type, so `integer | integer` is `integer`.
- A union with an `Any` member is `Any`, because every value already satisfies `Any`. So an `if`
  whose branch is `Any` is `Any` (`if (is.null(f)) order else f` with `f <- attr(x, "f")`), and
  not the other branch's type. A variable that two paths write is different: it keeps the typed
  path's type, so a later use of it is still checked.
- Otherwise, a union with an `Unknown` member is `Unknown`.

A call's result drops a union member that is a type parameter of the called function when a
parameter the call supplies mentions it and the call left it unconstrained, because no argument then
produces such a value. With `or_else <- function(value, fallback) if (is.null(value)) fallback else value`,
typed `<T, U> fn(value: T | NULL, [fallback]: U) -> U | T`, the call `or_else(NULL, "x")` is
`character`: the `NULL` fills nothing of `T`. `Result.and_then` returns
`Result<B, E | F>`, where `F` is the error type of the step, so a step that never fails leaves `F`
unconstrained and the result keeps the error `E` alone:

```r
a <- Result.and_then(Str.to_integer("1"), function(n) :Ok(n))
# :Ok(integer) | :Err(:InvalidNumStr)
b <- Result.and_then(Str.to_integer("1"), function(n) if (n > 0L) :Ok(n) else :Err(:NotPositive))
# :Ok(integer) | :Err(:InvalidNumStr | :NotPositive)
```

A union made only of such parameters stays as it is. A type parameter that an argument fills
directly, or that appears only in the result (`<T> fn(name: character) -> T | NULL`), is kept,
because there an open `T` means any value. So is one that code outside the call still reaches,
such as an unannotated parameter passed as the callback:
`function(r, step) Result.and_then(r, step)` returns `:Ok(V) | :Err(U | W)`.

### Union compatibility

A value fits an expected union when it fits any one member, with the usual coercions applied per
member. So `integer` fits `integer | character | NULL`, and `NULL` fits any union that contains
`NULL`. Members are tried in order, and a failed attempt leaks no bindings into the next one.

A union *value* has to be acceptable in every shape it can take, so it fits an expected type only
when every one of its members does. A union therefore fits any wider union (`integer | NULL` fits
`integer | character | NULL`), but not a single member type: `integer | character` does not fit
`integer`, and `T | NULL` does not fit plain `T`.

An unannotated value checked against an expected union binds to the whole union at its first use.
Uses commit in program order, so a later use that needs a different type is reported at that later
site. When two union-typed contracts share only some members, such as `integer | character` at one
call and `logical | character` at the next, the checker does not compute their intersection; annotate
the value with the member type you intend.

## Records, tuples and tags

Records, tuples and tags are ry's own values, not R lists. The user guide is
[Records and tags](/guide#records-tags-and-match); this section is their typing contract. They are
inline syntax, so with `[check] inline-annotations = false` each literal, tag or `match` is reported
as a `ry-syntax` warning. Literals are still typed then, but a `match` gets no coverage check. Their types may be written in `#:` comments
either way.

### Record and tuple types

| Literal | Type |
| --- | --- |
| `(x = 1, y = 2)` | `Record{x: double, y: double}` |
| `(1L, "a")` | `Tuple{integer, character}` |
| `(x = 1,)` | `Record{x: double}` |
| `(1L,)` | `Tuple{integer}` |
| `()` | `Tuple{}` |

A one-field record and a one-item tuple need the trailing comma, because `(x = 1)` and `(x)` keep
their R meaning. A literal is all fields or all items, and a repeated field name is an error.

- A record's fields are all required. Field order is part of neither its identity nor its type, so
  `Record{x: double, y: double}` and `Record{y: double, x: double}` are the same type.
- A record and a list are different types: `Record{x: double}` does not fit `list{x: double}`, and
  the reverse does not fit either.
- `point$x` and `point[["x"]]` type as the field's type, and a field the record lacks is a
  `type-mismatch`. `pair[[1L]]` with a literal index types as that item, and an index past the end
  is a `type-mismatch`. On a record, a position counts the fields in their sorted order. A computed
  index types as the union of the item or field types.
- `point$x <- value` and `point[["x"]] <- value` rebind `point` to a copy with that field replaced,
  and the record keeps its type. The value must be compatible with the field's type, and a field the
  record lacks is a `type-mismatch`, because a record's fields are fixed.

### Rows

A `Record{...}`, `DataFrame{...}`, `LazyFrame{...}` or `GroupBy{...}` type may end in a *row* `..R`, where `R` is a
type parameter of the function. The type lists some fields or columns, and `R` stands for all the
others, so a function can take any table that has a `code` column and return it with one more
column:

```r
ids <- DataFrame(code = c("a", "b"), id = c(10L, 20L))

add_id <- function<R>(data: DataFrame{code: character[], ..R}) -> DataFrame{code: character[], id: integer[], ..R} {
  DataFrame.join(data, ids, on = "code")
}

out <- add_id(DataFrame(code = c("a", "b"), price = c(1.5, 2.5)))
print(out$volume)
add_id(DataFrame(name = "a"))
```

`out` is a `DataFrame{code: character[], id: integer[], price: double[]}`, so the checker knows
both which columns it has and which it lacks:

```text ry check typing
type-mismatch

  x the frame `out` has no column `volume`. Its columns are `code`, `id`, `price`

type-mismatch

  x expected `DataFrame{code: character[], ..T}`, found `DataFrame{name: character[]}`
```

- `R` must be declared with `function<R>(...)` or `<R> fn(...)`, and the row comes last. A
  `list{...}` type ends in a bare `..` instead ([open records](#open-records)), and a
  `Tuple{...}`, a tag's payload, a `data.frame{...}` and a `data.table{...}` type list everything,
  so a row there is an `annotation` error.
- At a call, `R` binds to the fields or columns the argument has beyond the listed ones, so the
  result carries them with their types. A listed field takes precedence over a field of the same
  name in the row.
- Inside the function, `R` is unknown. Reading a field or column the type does not list is a
  `type-mismatch`, and so is a positional `[[` such as `x[[1]]`, because the row's fields or
  columns may come before the listed ones. Iterating a table with a row, as `lapply` does, gives
  columns of unknown type. A record pattern needs a trailing `..`, as in `(a = p, ..)`.
- `R` stands for a row wherever it appears: using it also as a type of its own, as in `y: R`, or as
  the row of both a record and a table is an `annotation` error. A generic alias passes a row on:
  with `alias Rec<R> = Record{a: I64, ..R}`, `Rec<S>` is `Record{a: I64, ..S}`, and `Rec<I64>` is an
  `annotation` error.
- A value with a row fits a type with the same row, never a closed type: returning
  `Record{a: I64, ..R}` as `Record{a: I64}` is a `type-mismatch`, and so is building a closed
  `(a = 1L,)` where `Record{a: I64, ..R}` is expected.
- A table verb that keeps every column keeps the row: `filter`, `sort`, `head`, `tail`, `unique`,
  `with_columns`, `drop`, `rename`, `with_row_index`, `group_by`, `lazy` and `collect`, a row
  selection `d[i]`, a `:=` update, and a join whose left table has the row. `select` and `GroupBy.agg` build their result from the columns they name, so it has no
  row, and a join whose right table has a row, or `concat` of a table with a row, types as a plain
  `DataFrame`.

```r ry run runtime=ry
bump <- function<R>(x: Record{a: I64, ..R}) -> Record{a: I64, ..R} (..x, a = x$a + 1L)
y <- bump((a = 1L, b = "x"))
print(y$b)
#> [1] "x"
```

### Tag types

A tag's type is written the way its value is, with types in place of values: `:None` has type
`:None`, `:Some(5L)` has type `:Some(integer)`, and `:Rect(width = 2, height = 3)` has type
`:Rect(width: double, height: double)`. Tags are structural and need no declaration.

A *tag union* is an ordinary [union](#union-types), so the union rules apply unchanged: a tag value
fits a union that lists a tag of the same name with a compatible payload, and an `if` whose branches
build different tags types as their union. Normalization merges two members with the same tag name
and payload shape into one member whose payloads are joined position by position, so joining
`:Some(1L)` with `:Some("a")` gives `:Some(integer | character)`. Two members with the same name and
a different payload shape are a `type-mismatch` where they meet.

A union that lists `..` as a member is *open*: it also holds every tag it does not list. A lone
`..` lists no tag, so it holds every tag.

- A tag value fits an open union when the union lists its name with a compatible payload, or does
  not list its name at all.
- A closed union fits an open one when each of its members does.
- An open union never fits a closed union. It fits another open union only when every tag the
  target lists is also listed by the source, with a compatible payload.

A tag union has no [row](#rows), so a function cannot pass the unlisted tags of an open union through
with their types. Generic tag unions come from aliases, such as
`#: @alias Option<T> {:Some(T) | :None}`. One is built in: the
[standard library](/reference/standard-library/) defines `Result<T, E>` as `{:Ok(T) | :Err(E)}`, and a
project's own `Result` alias shadows it.

### Nominal records, tuples and tags

A record, tuple or tag union may be the representation of a [nominal type](#nominal-types), as in
`type Shape = :Circle(radius: double) | :Square(side: double)`. The nominal rules apply unchanged, so
a structural value with the right shape, such as a bare `:Circle(radius = 1)`, does not fit `Shape`,
and only [`@Shape(...)`](#nominal-introduction) or `#: @new Shape` creates one. Wherever a rule of
this section needs the value's shape, the nominal value projects to its representation:

- `$`, `[[` and field writes follow the record and tuple rules above. A field write keeps the
  nominal type, its value must fit the field's type, and a field the representation lacks is a
  `type-mismatch`.
- A [record update](#record-update) `(..point, y = 3)` on a nominal record follows the same rule:
  the copy keeps the nominal type, so each replaced field keeps its declared type.
- A `match` checks its patterns and its [coverage](#exhaustiveness) against the representation, so a
  nominal tag union, including a recursive generic one such as
  `type Tree<T> = :Leaf | :Node(left: Tree<T>, value: T, right: Tree<T>)`, must be matched
  exhaustively.
- A binding pattern binds the value with its nominal type, and a payload binding has the payload's
  declared type, so `:Node(left = l, ..)` on a `Tree<integer>` binds `l` as a `Tree<integer>`.
- A nominal value whose representation is a record, tuple or tag is still a ry value when it
  [crosses into R](#crossing-into-r).

### Record update

`(..point, y = 3.5)` is a copy of the record `point` with the field `y` replaced. It has `point`'s
type, so every replaced field must exist and keep its type:

```r
p <- (x = 1, y = 2)
q <- (..p, z = 3)
```

```text ry check typing
type-mismatch

  x field `z` does not exist in `Record{x: I64, y: I64}`
```

Only a record, or a nominal type whose representation is a record, can be updated this way, and
`$<-` is the statement form of the same change. A nominal record's copy keeps the nominal type, so
with `type Point = Record{x: F64, y: F64}`, `(..point, y = 3)` is a `Point`, and replacing `y` with a
`Str` is a `type-mismatch` rather than a change of type.

### Equality of records, tuples and tags

`==` and `!=` compare two records, tuples or tags structurally and give a `Bool`. Two values are
equal when they have the same shape (the same fields, or the same tag with the same payload shape)
and every field or payload item is equal, compared the same way. A `List` of them compares item by
item, as the [list operators](#list-operators) do.

```r ry run runtime=ry strict
type Point = Record{x: F64, y: F64}
p <- @Point(x = 1, y = 2)
p == @Point(x = 1, y = 2)
#> [1] TRUE
:Some((1i64, "a")) != :Some((1i64, "b"))
#> [1] TRUE
```

The two sides must have types that can hold one value, so comparing values whose types never meet
is a `type-mismatch`:

```r
isOther <- function(kind: :Bond | :Cash) kind == :Other
```

```text ry check typing
type-mismatch

  x `==` is not defined between `:Bond | :Cash` and `:Other`, because a value of one type never equals a value of the other
```

- A number field is equal when it holds the same number, as `identical` decides, so a field holding
  `NaN` equals one holding `NaN`, unlike `==` on two `F64` values.
- An R value never equals one of these values, so comparing one with an R value is a
  `type-mismatch`.
- `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=` are not defined on records, tuples or tags.

### `match`

`match (EXPR) { ARMS }` is a `match` expression only when the `{` is on the line of the closing `)`,
so every R use of `base::match` keeps its meaning. An arm is `PATTERN => EXPR` or
`PATTERN if (CONDITION) => EXPR`, separated by newlines, `;` or `,`.

| Pattern | Matches |
| --- | --- |
| `_` | anything, binding nothing |
| `name` | anything, binding it to `name` |
| `1L`, `"a"`, `TRUE`, `NA`, `NULL`, `-1` | a length-one value equal to the literal |
| `:Tag`, `:Tag(p1, p2)`, `:Tag(field = p)` | a tag with that name whose payload matches |
| `(p1, p2)`, `(p,)` | a tuple of that length whose items match |
| `(name = p, ..)`, `(name = p,)` | a record with those fields; without `..`, exactly those fields. The `..` takes no name: `(x = a, ..others)` is a syntax error |
| `p1 \| p2` | either pattern; both sides must bind the same names |
