The type of a `match` is the join of its arm types, as for `if`. A pattern that cannot match the
scrutinee's type, such as a tag a closed union does not list or a field the record lacks, is a
`type-mismatch` at the pattern. A scrutinee whose type is not yet known takes its shape from the
patterns: the arms' tags form a closed union. A catch-all arm accepts a value of any type, so with
one the scrutinee is not shaped by the patterns. Pattern
names are bound in the current environment, so after the `match` a name that only some arms bind
types like a variable assigned in only some branches of an `if`.

### Exhaustiveness

When the scrutinee's type is known, the arms must cover every value of it. Coverage is computed over
the cases the type has:

- a closed tag union has one case per tag, and an open union adds an "other tags" case that only a
  catch-all arm covers;
- a tuple or a record is one case, with its items or fields checked in turn;
- a scalar `logical` has three cases, `TRUE`, `FALSE` and `NA`. Coverage trusts the scalar claim,
  so a value typed scalar that holds several elements, such as `v[-1L]` (see
  [`[` on vectors](#-on-vectors-1)), matches no literal arm and stops with an error when the code
  runs;
- `NULL` is one case, and a union has the cases of each member;
- a nominal type has the cases of its representation;
- every other type has unboundedly many values, so only a binding or `_` covers it.

A guarded arm never counts toward coverage, because its guard can be false. A `match` that misses a
case is a `non-exhaustive-match` error naming up to three missing cases as patterns, and an arm that
the arms above it already cover is an `unreachable-pattern` warning.

### List and `as` patterns

`match` takes two more pattern forms. A list pattern matches a `List` by its length and items:
`[]` matches the empty list, `[a, b]` a list of exactly two items, and `[first, ..rest]` a list of at
least one item, binding the remaining items to `rest` as a list. `[.., last]` matches from the end.
`p as name` matches what `p` matches and binds the whole value to `name`:

```r ry run runtime=ry strict
describe <- function(xs: List<I64>) -> Str match (xs) {
  [] => "empty",
  [only] => f"just {only}",
  [first, ..rest] as all => f"{List.len(all)} items, starting with {first}"
}
describe([])
#> [1] "empty"
describe([1i64, 2i64, 3i64])
#> [1] "3 items, starting with 1"
```

### Propagating an error with `try`

`try f(x)`, where `f(x)` returns a result such as `:Ok(value) | :Err(error)`, is the `value` of an
`:Ok`. On an `:Err` the enclosing function returns that `:Err` at once, and the function's result
type gains its error:

```r
parse_both <- function(a: Str, b: Str) {
  x <- try Str.to_i64(a)
  y <- try Str.to_i64(b)
  :Ok(x + y)
}
```

`parse_both` types as `fn(a: Str, b: Str) -> :Err(:InvalidNumStr) | :Ok(I64)`. `try` applies to a
name or call written directly after it. `try (expr)` and `try` before anything else keep R's
meaning, a call of R's `try()`, so no R code changes meaning. A `try` outside a function, or on a
value that is not a result, is a `type-mismatch`.

### Crossing into R

R functions and C code do not know these values. `as.list()` converts a record, tuple or tag to a
list, and `as.record()`, `as.tuple()` and `as.tag()` convert back.

Passing one to an R function that is not the project's own is a `type-mismatch` at the argument. The
exceptions are the functions the runtime lets them through: those that understand them (`format`,
`print`, `identical`, `length`, `[[`, the conversions), those that only ask what a value is
(`is.null`, `class`, `inherits`), and those that store a value without looking inside it (`list`,
`lapply`, `do.call`). A parameter that the callee only forces and perhaps hands back takes one
too, as the `object` of testthat's condition, output and visibility expectations does, so
`expect_error(make_point(-1), "negative")` checks while `expect_equal(make_point(1), p)` is
reported (`@opaque` in the stub, see the stub authoring guide). A value whose type only sometimes is a record, tuple or tag, such as
`Record{x: double} | NULL`, is not reported, because it may be R data when the code runs.

### Records from R

R data enters a typed function only through its parameters, one vector or scalar each, converted
where the call is written (see [Lists and R vectors](#lists-and-r-vectors)). An R list is never a
record, tuple or tag, so passing one where a record is expected is a `type-mismatch`:

```r
alias Trial = Record{time: List<I64>, treated: List<Bool>}
trial_size <- function(trial: Trial) -> I64 List.len(trial$time)
trial <- list(time = c(5L, 3L), treated = c(TRUE, FALSE))
trial_size(trial)
```

```text ry run runtime=ry strict
type-mismatch

  x expected `Record{time: List<I64>, treated: List<Bool>}`, found `list{time: integer[], treated: logical[]}`. An R list never becomes a record: write a record literal, `(time = ..., treated = ...)`
   --[main.R:4:12]
 3 | trial <- list(time = c(5L, 3L), treated = c(TRUE, FALSE))
 4 | trial_size(trial)
   |            ^^^^^

  x strict mode found 1 error, so this code does not run
```

When the value is written as a `list(...)` call, `ry check --fix --unsafe-fixes` rewrites it as the
literal, `list(time = t, treated = d)` as `(time = t, treated = d)`.

Pass the fields as parameters instead, and build any record inside the typed function:

```r ry run runtime=ry strict
trial_size <- function(time: List<I64>, treated: List<Bool>) -> I64 List.len(time)
time <- List.from_r(c(5L, 3L))
treated <- List.from_r(c(TRUE, FALSE))
trial_size(time, treated)
#> 2i64
```

A record or tuple that R code builds with `as.record()` passes to compiled typed code when each
field is a scalar, a string, another such record or tuple, or a ry list, and its shape is then its
type. One with a longer R vector in a field, an `NA` string, a class or attributes makes the call
run in the interpreter.

## Numbers, `Bool` and `Str`

ry has its own scalar types, none of which holds `NA`. They are the types typed ry is written in,
and an R value reaches them only through an explicit conversion (see
[Crossing between R and ry scalars](#crossing-between-r-and-ry-scalars)).

### Number types

| Type | Values |
| --- | --- |
| `I64` | a 64-bit signed integer, every bit pattern a value |
| `F64` | an IEEE 754 double, including `NaN` and the infinities |

An `F64` is stored as R's `double`, so an `F64` fits `double` and is passed to R code as is. An
`I64` does not share R's `integer`, because R reads one 32-bit pattern as `NA` and overflows to `NA`
with a warning: `-2147483648i64` is an ordinary `I64`, and overflow is an error.

The constraint `number` admits both types. An unannotated helper takes ry numbers wherever ry's
arithmetic is defined on them, through the [constraints](#constraints) that join R's numbers and
ry's: `mul <- function(a, b) a * b` multiplies two `F64`s to an `F64`, and
`scale <- function(x) x * 2` makes `List.map(xs, scale)` with `xs: List<I64>` a `List<I64>`. A
helper that divides, such as `function(a, b) a / b` or `function(x) x * 0.5`, takes an `F64` and
not an `I64`, because ry's `/` and fractional literals are defined on `F64` alone, and passing it an
`I64` is a `type-mismatch`.

### Number literals

A suffix names a literal's type exactly: `5i64` or `1.5f64`. A hex literal takes only the `i64`
suffix (`0xFFi64`), because `f` is a hex digit. A `-` written directly before a literal is part of
it, so `-9223372036854775808i64` is an `I64`. Any other suffix is a syntax error.

An unsuffixed literal, such as `5`, `5L` or `1.5`, has R's type unless a ry number type asks for it.
A ry type asks in three places:

- **An expected type.** A typed parameter, an annotated variable, a function's declared result, a
  list item or a record field whose type is known.
- **The other operand.** In arithmetic or a comparison with an operand of a ry number type, the
  literal takes that type, so `n + 1` with `n: I64` is an `I64`.
- **A later use.** `total <- 0` gives `total` a literal type that its first use in the same
  function decides: an operand beside a ry number, an argument to a typed parameter, an annotated
  binding, a value written into a `List`'s item (`lookup[[i]] <- j` with `lookup: List<I64>`)
  or a record's field (`r$x <- j` with `x: I64`), a position into a `List` (`lookup[[j]]`, which
  makes `j` an `I64`), or the value a function declared with a ry number result ends with, which
  may be one branch of an `if` whose other branch is a literal (`if (flag) y else 0`). In
  `for (x in xs) total <- total + x` with `xs: List<F64>`, `total` is an `F64`, and
  `rate <- 2.0; half(rate)` with `half(x: F64)` makes `rate` an `F64`. Arithmetic on the variable
  and number literals of its R type (`x + 1.5` with `x <- 0`) is open too, and so is a variable that branches write with
  literals R gives one type: in a function declared `-> F64`, `x <- 0`, then
  `if (n > 1i64) x <- x + 1.5`, then `x` makes every `x` an `F64`. Branches that write literals of
  different R types (`1L` and `2.5`) keep R's types, inside a value as well: `if (c) :Ok(1L) else
  :Ok(2.5)` is an `:Ok(integer | double)`. An `if` with `else` or a `match` whose
  branches are all number literals of one R type is one such literal, both bound to a variable
  (`m <- if (c) 1 else 2`, then `m + n` with `n: I64`, makes `m` an `I64`) and beside one
  (`score + match (k) { :A => 1, :B => 2 }` with `score <- 0`). Any other read gives the variable R's type,
  as does a function that never decides it. A literal written as a tuple item, a record field
  or a tag payload item is decided the same way: in `pair <- ("a", 1)`, the read
  `Str.concat(s, pair[[1]])` makes `"a"` a `Str`, and with no such use `pair` is a
  `Tuple{character, double}`. In a function declared `-> Result<I64, Str>`, `found <- :Ok(1)`
  then `found` makes the `1` an `I64`.
- **A top-level binding in a typed file.** In a strict file, a file that builds a ry value (a
  record, tuple, tag, `List` or `match`), or a file with an inline annotation that names one of ry's
  own types (a ry number, `Str`, `Bool`, `List`, `Dict`, `Set`, `Result`, a `Record{...}`, a
  `Tuple{...}` or a tag), a literal bound outside any function is a ry value: `count <- 3` is an
  `I64`, `rate <- 2.0` an `F64`, `name <- "ry"` a `Str` and `flag <- TRUE` a `Bool`. A literal
  written with a decimal point or an exponent (but not `1e3L`), or a whole number past the range of
  `I64`, is an `F64`, and arithmetic on literals alone is one literal, so `step <- 1 / 4` is an
  `F64`. The tuple items, record fields and tag payload items of a literal bound there are ry values
  too, also inside a nested literal or a `List`, so `pt <- (x = 3.0, y = 4.0)` is a `Record{x: F64,
  y: F64}` and `pair <- ("a", 1)` a `Tuple{Str, I64}`. A literal inside a call keeps R's type, as
  `f(1)` does: `f((x = 1,))` passes a `Record{x: double}`. Each top-level statement is checked on
  its own, so nothing later in the file decides the type. In any other file a top-level literal has
  R's type, so a file annotated in R's types alone (`f <- function(x: double) -> double`, `p:
  list{name: character}`) runs like its erasure. A record or tuple type counts by its head, so an R
  class named `Tuple` does not, and a `#:` comment never counts, because comment annotations leave
  the code ordinary R that computes what R computes.

A value written with literals alone is decided as one literal, wherever a lone literal would be:

- Arithmetic (`+`, `-`, `*`, `%%` and `/`), so `2 * 3.14159 * r` with `r: F64` is an `F64`, and `/`
  asks for `F64`.
- Logic and comparisons (`!`, `&&`, `||`, and a comparison of number literals), so
  `ok: Bool <- 1.5 < 2` holds, and a literal beside a `Bool` takes that type: `TRUE && done` with
  `done: Bool` is a `Bool`.
- An `if` whose branches are such values, and a `{}` holding one, so `x: F64 <- if (c) 0.1 else 3`
  holds and `if (c) 1 else 2.5` at a top-level binding is an `F64`.

A string or `TRUE`/`FALSE` bound to a variable is decided the same way, as `Str` or `character`
and `Bool` or `logical`. A ry number that a call leaves open is decided by an annotation as well,
so `total: F64 <- List.sum([3])` sums a `List<F64>`. A number, string or `TRUE`/`FALSE` passed
for a ry function's type parameter is open too, so another argument or the call's use decides it:
in `List.walk(words, "", f)` with `f: (Str, Str) -> Str` the `""` is a `Str`, and
`Result.with_default(:Ok(1), 2)` in a function declared `-> I64` is an `I64`.

A tuple item, record field or tag payload item written with a literal in one branch of an `if` or
`match` is decided by the item in the same place of another branch. In the arms
`:Buy(quantity = q, price = p) => (q, p)` and `:Fee(amount = a) => (0, a)`, the `0` is an `F64` when
`q` is an `F64` and R's `double` when `q` is a `double`, so a variable the `match` writes in a loop
has one type on every iteration. A string or `TRUE`/`FALSE` beside a `Str` or `Bool` is decided the
same way, and `(2.5, a)` beside `(q, p)` with `q: I64` is a `type-mismatch` at the `2.5`.

A literal the type cannot hold exactly, such as `2.5` as an `I64`, is a
`type-mismatch` at the literal:

```r
m <- 5i64
m * 2.5
```

```text ry check typing
type-mismatch

  x this literal does not fit `I64`, which cannot hold its value exactly
```

A literal runs as the type the checker gave it. Before `ry run`, the console or `ry load` runs code
on ry's runtime, every unsuffixed literal the checker typed as `I64` is spelled with its suffix, so the arm
`:Err(_) => 0` in a function declared `-> I64` returns `0i64`, and `xs <- [1, 2]` is a `List<I64>`.
An empty list keeps its item type the same way: `[]` and a `List.map` whose items come from its
callback carry the item type the checker gave them, so `List.map(List.range(3, 1), \(v) v * 2)` is
an empty `List<I64>` and its `List.sum` is `0i64`.
A file parsed on ry's runtime, by `source()`, `parse(file = )` or a parse whose `srcfilecopy()` has
`isFile = TRUE` (as `sys.source(keep.source = TRUE)` and testthat make), is checked on its own and
elaborated the same way, and the check prints its annotation warnings, once per session for the
same file and text. The check of a script, of a console command and of a sourced file reads the
files it `source()`s by a literal path, resolved against the working directory as R resolves it,
so strict code may call what they define. It reads them when the code is checked, before it
runs, so a later console command sees what the file defined then, and a script that writes a
file before sourcing it is checked against the file's earlier text. When a name is defined in
several sourced files, the file that finishes running last defines it, and a definition in the
sourcing file itself takes precedence over a sourced one. The check does not follow an absolute
or home path, such as `/lib.R` or `~/lib.R`, `sys.source()` without `keep.source = TRUE`, which
runs the text unelaborated, or a call that evaluates into another environment, such as
`source("lib.R", local = new.env())` or `source("lib.R", local = TRUE)` inside a function,
because nothing it defines is visible globally. `ry check` and the editor do not know the
working directory the code will run in, so they try the sourcing file's own directory first and
then the project root, and follow no path that leaves the project. Strict mode
applies to a sourced file only when the file turns it on itself
(`# typing: strict`), because the project's strict mode belongs to the script that sources it. A
file with no ry syntax and no `# typing:` directive is not analyzed and reads no `ry.toml`, and its
literals keep R's types. Code parsed from text, and `sys.source()` with its
default `keep.source = FALSE`, is not elaborated: there an operator converts a literal
operand, and an annotated binding such as `n: I64 <- 5` converts its literal. An `I64` parameter and an `-> I64` result
convert the same way, so `f(3)` passes `3i64` to `f <- function(n: I64) ...`. A conversion that
cannot be exact is an error, never a rounded value.

### Number arithmetic

Both operands of an arithmetic operator or comparison have the same number type, and the two types
are never converted into each other: `1i64 + 1.5f64` is a `type-mismatch` that tells you to convert
one side.

| Operator | `I64` | `F64` |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `+ - *`, unary `-` | the same type; overflow is an error | the same type |
| `/` | a `type-mismatch` that suggests `%/%` or `Num.to_f64()` | the same type |
| `%/%`, `%%` | floored division and modulo, as in R | as in R |
| `^` | the same type | the same type |
| `== != < > <= >=` | `Bool` | `Bool` |

Integer division by zero is an error. `Num.div_trunc` and `Num.rem` give truncating division, and
`Num.add_checked`, `Num.add_wrap` and `Num.add_saturated` (with their `sub` and `mul` siblings) give
the other overflow behaviors. `Num.to_i64` and `Num.to_f64` convert between the two types.
`Num.to_i64` is an error when the value is not whole or does not fit, and `Num.to_i64_checked`
returns `:Err(:OutOfBounds)` instead.

### `Bool` and `Str`

`Bool` is `TRUE` or `FALSE`, never `NA`. A comparison of ry numbers or strings is a `Bool`, as is
`!`, `&&`, `||`, `&` or `|` on `Bool` operands. `if` and `while` take a `Bool` or an R `logical`.

`Str` is an immutable UTF-8 string, never `NA`. A string literal becomes a `Str` where one is
expected, and the `Str` module holds the functions that work on strings. A `Bool` is stored as R's
`logical` and a `Str` as R's `character`, so both pass to R code as they are.

`f"..."` builds a `Str` from text and interpolated expressions, each written in braces:

```r ry run runtime=ry strict
name <- "ry"
n <- 5i64
f"hello {name}, {n + 1i64}"
#> [1] "hello ry, 6"
```

Doubled braces, `{{` and `}}`, stand for literal braces. R reads `f"..."` as a syntax error, so no R
code changes meaning.

### Crossing between R and ry scalars

`Num.from_r` turns an R `double` into an `F64` and an R `integer` into an `I64`, `Str.from_r` turns
a `character` into a `Str`, and `Bool.from_r` turns a `logical` into a `Bool`. Each stops with an
error on `NA`, on a length other than one, and on attributes. `Num.to_r` goes the other way: an `I64` becomes R's `integer` when it fits in 32
bits and a `double` otherwise.

No R type represents an `I64`, or a `List` whose items are not `F64`, `Bool` or `Str`, so passing
one to an R function is a `type-mismatch` that names the conversion. When such code runs anyway, the
R function stops with the same advice:

```r
n <- 4
inc <- function(x: I64) -> I64 x + 1
sqrt(n)
```

```text ry check typing
type-mismatch

  x `sqrt` is an R function, and this value is a ry `I64`, not an R object. Convert it with `Num.to_r()` first
```

An `F64`, `Bool` or `Str`, and a `List` of one of them, shares the representation of R's `double`,
`logical` or `character` (vector), so it passes to R code unconverted: `sqrt(2.0)` and
`mean([1.5, 2.5])` need no conversion.

An operator never converts between the two sides, so comparing a ry scalar with an R value is a
`type-mismatch` that names the conversion for the R side: `Str.from_r()` for a `character`,
`Bool.from_r()` for a `logical` and `Num.from_r()` for a number.

```r
same <- function(a: Str, b: character) -> Bool a == b
```

```text ry check typing
type-mismatch

  x `==` is not defined between `Str` and `character`. A ry value meets an R value only through a conversion: convert the R side with `Str.from_r()`
```

## Lists

A `List` is ry's typed sequence, and this section
is its typing contract. The `[...]` literal is inline syntax, so with
`[check] inline-annotations = false` each literal is a `ry-syntax` warning, and it is still typed.
The type `List<T>` may be written in `#:` comments either way.

### List literals

`[a, b, c]` types as `List<T>`, where `T` is the type of the first item. Every other item must have
exactly that type: items are never converted into each other, so `[1, "a"]` is a `type-mismatch` at
`"a"`. An unsuffixed number literal is decided by the list it is in, as described under
[number literals](#number-literals): `[1, 2]` is a `List<I64>`, `[1.5, 2]` is a `List<F64>`, and
`[1i64, 2]` is a `List<I64>`. A literal the item type cannot hold exactly, such as `2.5` in
`[1, 2.5]`, is a `type-mismatch`. Tags are the one exception to exact item types: their types join
into a tag union, so `[:Ok(1), :Err("no")]` is a list of `:Ok(double) | :Err(character)`.

An item is a number, a `Bool`, a `Str`, or another ry value: a record, tuple, tag, `Dict`, `Set` or
list. Any other item type is a `type-mismatch`, and so is the literal `NA`. A type does not say
whether a value is missing, so an item computed by R code may still be `NA`, and the constructor
stops with an error on it when the code runs. In an annotation, `List<T>` with any other `T`, such
as `List<double[]>`, is an `annotation` error.

The empty literal `[]` takes its item type from an annotation, as in `#: List<Str>`, or from a use
such as `List.append(xs, 1)`.

### Lists and R vectors

- `List<F64>`, `List<Bool>` and `List<Str>` are stored as R `double`, `logical` and `character`
  vectors, and they fit `double[]`, `logical[]` and `character[]`. A list of ry values fits
  `list[T]`. Other lists, such as `List<I64>`, cross to R only through `List.to_r()`.
- A plain R vector never fits `List<T>`. `List.from_r(x)` converts one: a `double[]` gives
  `List<F64>`, a `character[]` gives `List<Str>`, and a `list[T]` gives `List<T>` when `T` is a ry
  value (the `item` constraint).
- At run time `List.from_r()` stops with an error on an `NA` or an attribute such as a class, and it
  drops names. Setting an attribute on a list, or assigning `NA` into it from R code, turns it into a
  plain R vector.
- At run time `List.from_r(x)` gives a list of the item type the checker gave it, which comes from
  R's vector types. A vector R types as `double` holds integers when R computed it from integers,
  as `abs(-3:3)` does, so where the checked type is `List<F64>` an `integer` or `logical` vector
  converts to `F64` items, and where it is `List<I64>` a `logical` vector converts to `I64` items.
  An empty vector gives an empty list of the checked type:

  ```r ry run runtime=ry strict
  whole <- function() abs(-3:3)
  total <- function(extra: F64) -> F64 {
    List.sum(List.from_r(whole())) + extra
  }
  total(0.5)
  #> [1] 12.5
  ```

  A vector of any other type stops the conversion with an error. That happens when R code gives a
  value its types do not describe, for example after it binds a function to something else:

  ```r
  prices <- function() c(1.5, 2.5)
  count <- function(extra: F64) -> F64 {
    listed <- List.from_r(prices())
    Num.to_f64(List.len(listed)) + extra
  }
  assign("prices", function() c("1.5", "2.5"))
  count(3)
  ```

  ```text ry run runtime=ry
  Error: `List.from_r(prices())` gave a `List<Str>` where the type checker expects a `List<F64>`, the type of the R code it read: convert the vector to that type in R first, for example with `as.numeric()`
  ```

### List operators

An elementwise operator takes two lists with the same item type, or a list and a single value of the
item type. Number types never mix, and an R vector operand is a `type-mismatch` whose message
suggests `List.from_r()`.

| Operator | Items | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `+ - * %% %/% ^`, unary `-` | numbers | `List<T>` |
| `/` | `F64` | `List<T>` |
| `== != < > <= >=` | numbers and `Str` for ordering, any item for equality | `List<Bool>` |
| `& \| !` | `Bool` | `List<Bool>` |

Strings order as R orders `character` values in the session's collation locale, both as items and
as single `Str` values: in the `C` locale that is code point order, so `"B" < "a"` is `TRUE`.

`/` on `I64` items is a `type-mismatch`, as it is on a single `I64`, and it decides undecided
literal items as `F64`, so `[1, 2] / 2` is a `List<F64>`. When the code runs, two lists of different
lengths are an error rather than recycled.

### List indexing

- `xs[[i]]` takes one position and types as `T`.
- `xs[i]` takes positions, a `Bool` mask, or no index, and types as `List<T>`.
- A `character` index, or more than one index, is a `type-mismatch`, because a list has one
  dimension and no names.
- When the code runs, a negative position leaves an item out and a zero selects nothing, as in R. A
  position past the end, an `NA` position, or a mask of a different length is an error, never `NA`.
- A replacement through `[<-` or `[[<-` must be an item of the item type, or for `[<-` a list of
  them with one item per position, which is never recycled.

`for (x in xs)` binds `x` as `T`. The `List` module of the [standard library](/reference/std/list/)
holds the functions that work on lists.

## Function types

A function can be annotated in one of two styles, but not both at once:

- the **expanded** style, with an optional `@forall`, then `@param` lines, then `@return` or
  `@returns`;
- the **compact** style, a single `fn(...)` type with an optional `-> RETURN_TYPE`.

Either way, consecutive `#:` lines form one annotation block for the function.

### Expanded function annotations

The expanded form puts one directive on each line: `@forall` to declare type parameters,
`@param name {TYPE}` for each parameter (or `@param [name] {TYPE}` for an optional one), and
`@return {TYPE}` or `@returns {TYPE}` for the result. A constrained binder is written
`@forall T: numeric`, with the same meaning as the compact `<T: numeric>`.

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
double_count <- function(count: integer, label: character | NULL = NULL) -> integer {
  count + count
}

then_some <- function<T>(condition: logical, value: T) -> T | NULL {
  if (condition) value
}
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: @param count {integer}
#: @param [label] {character | NULL}
#: @return {integer}
double_count <- function(count, label = NULL) { count + count }

#: @forall T
#: @param condition {logical}
#: @param value {T}
#: @return {T | NULL}
then_some <- function(condition, value) {
  if (condition) value
}
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

- Directives come in order: every `@forall` before any `@param`, and every `@param` before `@return`
  or `@returns`.
- Repeated `@forall` lines accumulate in source order, and a duplicate type parameter name is an
  error.
- At most one `@return` or `@returns` may appear. Without one, the return type is
  [elided](#elided-return-types).

### Compact function annotations

A compact annotation is a single function type, in one of these forms:

- `fn(name: TYPE) -> RETURN_TYPE`
- `fn(TYPE) -> RETURN_TYPE`
- `fn(name: TYPE, [optional_name]: TYPE) -> RETURN_TYPE`
- `<T> fn(name: TYPE) -> RETURN_TYPE`
- `<T, U, ...> fn(TYPE) -> RETURN_TYPE`

A leading `<...>` binder introduces [rank-1](#type-parameters-aliases-and-nominal-types) type
parameters for the whole function type. The binder always comes first: `fn<T>(...)` is not the
syntax. An optional parameter must be named, as in `[name]: TYPE`; a bare optional positional form
such as `fn(integer, [character])` is not supported. Omitting the return type
[elides](#elided-return-types) it.

A function may declare one rest parameter to accept a variable number of arguments. It is written
`...: TYPE`, and `fn(...)` is shorthand for `...: Any`. It has no name, because rest arguments are
matched by position, so `...items: TYPE` does not match the function's `...` and is reported, twice
at the same range: as a signature mismatch and as a parameter the function does not define. It can
come after the fixed parameters, as in `fn(prefix: TYPE, ...: TYPE) -> RETURN_TYPE`, or before named
ones, as in `fn(...: TYPE, [option]: TYPE) -> RETURN_TYPE`:

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
join <- function(...: character) -> character paste0(...)

wrap <- function(x: character, ...: character) -> character {
  paste0(x, ": ", paste(...))
}
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: fn(...: character) -> character
join <- function(...) paste0(...)

#: fn(x: character, ...: character) -> character
wrap <- function(x, ...) paste0(x, ": ", paste(...))
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

The position of the rest parameter, counted as the number of parameters declared before it, is part
of the signature. It must match the position of `...` in the function's formals, and it decides which
parameters a positional argument can fill (see [function calls](#function-calls) and
[function type compatibility](#function-type-compatibility)).

### Annotations and the formal list

An annotation declares the *types* of a definition's parameters, not the parameter *list*. R matches
a call's arguments against the formals in the `function(...)` header, so those formals are the call
interface: their names, their order, their defaults, and where `...` sits. An annotation cannot add,
remove, or reorder them. A parameter the annotation does not mention keeps its inferred type, which
makes annotating just one of several parameters a supported partial form.

When the declared shape disagrees with the definition, the definition wins at every call site, and
the disagreement is reported once, at the definition, so a call is never blamed for a mistake in an
annotation. The possible disagreements are:

- a declared parameter name that is not one of the formals (an annotation may not rename a
  parameter, so `fn(count: integer) -> integer` over `function(n) n` is an error);
- more declared parameter types than there are formals left to receive them;
- a declared optional `[name]` over a formal that callers may not omit (one with no default that
  the body [may force](#lazy-arguments-and-required-formals)), because declaring a parameter
  optional tells callers they may omit it;
- a parameter declared as required over a formal that has a default, because R lets callers omit it,
  so the annotation must say `[name]`;
- a rest parameter at a different position in the annotation than in the formals, or present on only
  one side, so a fixed annotation on a variadic function and a variadic annotation on a fixed
  function are both rejected.

The first case and a required declaration over a defaulted formal name the parameter. The others are
reported as a mismatch of the whole signature. In every case, the body is still checked under the
parameter types the annotation does pin down, so hover and navigation keep their facts.

### Elided return types

Both styles allow the return type to be left out: an expanded block without `@return` or `@returns`,
or a compact `fn(...)` without `-> RETURN_TYPE`. An elided return is not the same as a written
`NULL`, and what it means depends on whether there is a function body to infer from:

- On a checked annotation of a `function(...)` literal, the return type is inferred from the body,
  exactly as if there were no annotation, so annotating only the parameters never pins the return.
  `@param u {integer}` on `add_one <- function(u) u + 1L` infers `fn(u: integer) -> integer`. Writing
  the return as `Unknown` has the same effect, because `Unknown` never overrides what the body shows.
- In a nested function type, such as a callback parameter written `@param cb {fn(integer)}`, the
  elided return means `NULL`, which matches R functions called for their side effects.
- In a [trusted coercion](#trusted-coercions), and in an annotation on a value that is not a function
  literal, the return comes from the value's own type, called with the written parameter types.
  With `f <- function(x) x + 1L`, `#: fn(integer)` on `g <- f` types `g` as
  `fn(integer) -> integer`. When the value's return is not known, it is `Unknown`.

A function that really does return `NULL` can say so with `@returns {NULL}` or `-> NULL`. The
explicit form is enforced, so a body that returns anything else is then a type error.

### Inferred function types

An unannotated `function(...)` expression gets its type from the definition itself:

- Every parameter appears as a named parameter under its name in the definition, because R matches
  parameters both by name and by position.
- A parameter with a default is optional at call sites, and so is one the body never forces, as
  [lazy arguments](#lazy-arguments-and-required-formals) describes.
- A default runs lazily in the function's own frame, so it sees the variables the body assigns.
  In `function(n, ma, innov = rnorm(n + q)) { q <- length(ma); innov }`, the `q` in the default is
  the body's local, not base R's `q()`. A name the body never assigns resolves outside the function
  as usual. Such a read is not yet typed against the body's writes, so it reads as `Unknown`.
- A `...` formal becomes a rest parameter with element type `Any`, at the position it holds among the
  formals, so `function(x, ...) …` is `fn(x: T, ...: Any) -> …`. The values that reach `...` are not
  tracked into the body, so forwarding `...` to another call gives `Unknown`.
- Parameter and return types are inferred, and a parameter nothing constrains generalizes when the
  function is bound to a name, so `function(x) x` is `<T> fn(x: T) -> T`.
- A requirement inference could not discharge survives into the exported type: a parameter used as a
  number exports with its constraint, such as `<T: num>`, so calls from other files keep being checked.

`function(count, label = NULL) count` may therefore be called as `f(1L)`, `f(count = 1L)`, or
`f(1L, "x")`.

#### A return chosen by a flag

A body that ends in `if (flag) a else b`, where `flag` is a formal with a literal `TRUE` or `FALSE`
default that the body never reassigns, returns the union of both branches, as any `if` does. A
branch may also end in `return(value)`, and the flag may instead guard an earlier
`if (flag) return(value)` with no `else`, whose return is then the true branch and the rest of the
body the false one, provided those `return()` calls are the body's only early returns. The guard may
be a block that ends in `return(value)`, and the flag may be negated, so `if (!verbose) { ...;
return(TRUE) }` makes the block the false branch. Several such guards of the same flag all
return the same branch, so `if (both) return(a)` early and `if (both) return(b)` later join `a`
and `b` as the true branch. A call that leaves the flag out, or passes a literal, selects one branch,
so it returns that branch's type alone:

```r
p_dist <- function(q, valueOnly = TRUE) {
  prob <- q * 2
  if (valueOnly) prob else list(value = prob, error = prob)
}
# p_dist: <T: arithmetic or number, U> fn(q: T, [valueOnly]: U)
#   -> Promoted<T> | list{value: Promoted<T>, error: Promoted<T>}
gap <- function(x, p) p_dist(x) - p
# gap: <T: arithmetic, U: arithmetic> fn(x: T, p: U) -> double
full <- function(x) p_dist(x, valueOnly = FALSE)$error
# full: <T: arithmetic or number> fn(x: T) -> Promoted<T>
```

Any other flag argument, such as a variable, returns the union. A call that forwards `...` selects
by its visible arguments, so a flag passed through `...` is not seen: in
`wrapper <- function(x, ...) p_dist(x, ...)`, the wrapper returns `double` even when its caller
passes `valueOnly = FALSE`. This is a deliberate limit, because treating every forwarding call as
unselected reports ordinary wrappers that never pass the flag. An annotation has no syntax for
the selection, so a declared signature always returns its declared type.

#### Defaults

Defaults are type-checked, and they interact with the parameter's type in four ways:

- An error inside a default is reported, and the default of an annotated parameter must be
  compatible with the declared type.
- A `NULL` default is checked like any other. `function(title = NULL)` is R's usual way to write an
  optional argument, but when the caller omits it, `title` is `NULL` inside the body, so declaring it
  `character` is a promise the function does not keep. Declare `character | NULL` and narrow with
  `if (is.null(title))`. Marking the parameter `[title]` relaxes only the call.
- An unannotated parameter takes its type from its uses, not from its default, so
  `function(x = 1) x` is `<T> fn([x]: T) -> T`, and passing a character is not a finding.
- A call that omits the argument gets the default's type, because that is the value R puts in the
  frame: with `f <- function(x = 1) x`, `f()` is a `double` and `f("a")` is a `character`. A default
  is therefore checked against an *instantiation* of the declared type rather than against the
  binder, so `#: <T> fn([x]: T) -> T` over `function(x = 1) x` is accepted. A concrete declared type
  is unaffected: `fn(title: character)` still refuses a `NULL` default.
- An inferred parameter type always admits the default's type, because a call that passes the
  default's own value is always fine. When the body settles an unannotated parameter on a type the
  default does not have, the exported type is widened by the default's: with `up` declared
  `fn(x: character) -> character`, `function(s = NULL) up(s)` is
  `fn([s]: character | NULL) -> character`.
- An explicit `NULL` argument matches a `NULL` default, because the callee sees the value it would
  have seen without the argument. So with `search <- function(fun, target = NULL)` passing `target`
  on to arithmetic, `search(fun = f, target = NULL)` checks while `target = "a"` is reported.
- A default that names a function the checker has no type for, such as `timefun = bit::repeat.time`
  in a package it cannot read, makes the parameter's own type open, so calling it with any
  arguments checks.
- A value written to a formal on its `missing()` path types like the same value given as the
  default: it fills in for an omitted argument and does not rewrite a supplied one. So after
  `if (missing(tol)) tol <- 1e-8`, `m * tol` bounds what a caller passes as `tol`, and a call that
  omits `tol` sees `double`. A function written there, as in
  `if (missing(col.fun)) col.fun <- function(n) rep("a", n)`, is a default the same way. A value
  written on any other path is a rewrite, so later uses meet both values.
- Inside `if (missing(x))`, reading `x` is an error unless `x` goes straight on to a parameter a
  project function may leave unread, because R then forces nothing: `if (missing(x)) report(x)`
  checks when `report` tests `missing()` on that parameter. A library function forces what it is
  given, so `formatC(x, width = w)` there is still reported.

An `if` without `else` that writes a formal under a test reading that same formal *normalizes* the
input, as in `if (is.logical(type)) type <- if (type) "q" else "m"` or
`if (is.null(sep)) sep <- ","`. The write does not constrain the formal's own type, so callers may
still pass anything, and after the `if` the body sees the written type rather than a union with the
formal's type:

```r
to_double <- function(x = 1) {
  if (is.character(x)) x <- as.numeric(x)
  x
}
# to_double: <T> fn([x]: T) -> double
```

This is a deliberate approximation. A union with the open formal type would make every later use of
the value accept anything, while the normalized type is what the code after such a test assumes. A
`NULL` write is not a normalization and joins as an ordinary branch write.

### Function literals in call arguments

A function literal passed as a call argument takes its parameter types from the function type the
callee expects at that position. The other arguments are checked first, so the callee's type
parameters are already bound when the literal's body is inferred:

```r
xs <- [1, 2]
pairs <- List.map(xs, \(x) c(x, x))
# error: this function must return a list item (...), but it returns `double[]`
```

`List.map` expects `fn(T) -> U` with `T` bound to `double` by `xs`, so `x` is `double` inside the
body, `c(x, x)` is `double[]`, and the `item` constraint on `U` refuses it. Without the expected
type, `x` would be an open variable and `c(x, x)` could not be typed. The rule has these limits:

- It applies only to a literal written directly as the argument, with no `#:` or inline annotation
  on any of its formals. An annotated formal keeps its annotation, which is then checked against the
  expected type.
- Every formal without a default must receive a type, or none does. In
  `Reduce(function(a, b) a + b, xs)` the accumulator's type is still open when the literal is
  checked, so both formals stay open and are inferred from the body, as for any unannotated
  function. A formal with a default is always left to inference.
- With an overloaded callee, a formal takes a type only when every candidate that accepts the other
  arguments expects the same one. The overload is then chosen as usual, with the literal's inferred
  type among the arguments.
- A formal is an accumulator when its expected type shares a type variable with the expected
  result type, and another argument of the call has exactly that variable as its type: `seen` is
  one in `List.walk(xs, state: S, f: fn(S, T) -> S)`. The result widens the variable, so the type
  the initial state gives it (such as an empty `list()`) is no context, and by the rule above the
  literal takes no types. A variable fixed by a collection is not widened, so in
  `List.update(xs, i, f: fn(T) -> T)` the literal's formal still takes the item type.
- An accumulator whose initial state is a settled ry value takes that value's type, so in
  `List.walk(rows, List.repeat(0f64, 2), \(sums, row) sums + row)` the formal `sums` is a
  `List<F64>`. When the step function returns more tags than the state holds, the state widens to
  the join of both and the step function is checked again under it, so
  `List.walk(xs, :None, \(acc, x) :Some(x))` is `:None | :Some(I64)` and
  `List.walk(xs, (best = :None, n = 0i64), \(acc, x) (best = :Some(x), n = acc$n + 1i64))` is
  `Record{best: :None | :Some(I64), n: I64}`. The initial state is passed as the wider type, and
  the step function is checked at most three times. A step function may also return the state as
  the payload of every tag it returns, so `List.walk_until(xs, :None, \(acc, x) if (x > 2i64)
  :Break(:Some(x)) else :Continue(acc))` is `:None | :Some(I64)`. A state with a tag in it widens
  only with a single candidate callee whose step function returns the state in one of these two
  ways; anywhere else it gives the formal no context.
- A literal with a formal after `...` takes no types, because R binds positional arguments to
  `...` before any later formal.
- A parameter type that is `Any`, `Unknown` or still contains an open type variable gives no
  context.

When the body uses a parameter that took its type this way as an operand its type does not allow,
the finding is reported on that use and names the parameter. Other misuses, such as passing the
parameter to a function that expects another type, keep their usual wording:

```r
words <- ["a", "b"]
bad <- List.map(words, \(w) w * 2)
# error: `w` is used as a numeric value (`integer` or `double`) here, but the call passes this
# function `character` for it
```

A function literal that is the default of a declared parameter takes its parameter types the same
way, from the parameter's declared function type, under the same limits:

```r
#: fn(dsn: character, [connector]: fn(dsn: character) -> character) -> character
connect <- function(dsn, connector = function(dsn) toupper(dsn)) connector(dsn)
```

Here the default's `dsn` is `character`, so strict mode accepts it with no annotation of its own. A
misuse of such a parameter says that the declaration gave the type: `` `v` is used as a numeric
value (`integer` or `double`) here, but the parameter this function is the default of declares
`character` for it ``.

### Named and positional parameters

Parameter names in a function type are part of the call interface. A named parameter may be passed
as a named argument, while an unnamed parameter is positional only. So `fn(count: integer) -> integer`
allows a call with `count = 1L`, while `fn(integer) -> integer` makes a named argument a type error,
but only when no definition stands behind the type. When the annotation sits on a `function(count)`
definition, the formals supply the name, and `f(count = 1L)` stays legal. An optional parameter
follows the same rule, and must be named.

Parameter, field, and type names may contain `.`, matching R's convention for names such as
`na.rm` and `length.out`: `fn(x: double, na.rm: logical) -> double`, `list{na.rm: logical}`, and
`@type My.Type {integer}` are all valid.

### Function type compatibility

A function value is compatible with an expected function type when its parameters accept every call
the expected type may make, and its return type satisfies the expected return type.

Parameters pair by name where the expected type names them, because R matches arguments against the
definition's formal names. So `fn(a: integer, b: character)` accepts a function defined as
`function(b, a)`. An unnamed parameter type pairs with the remaining parameters from left to right,
so `fn(count: integer) -> NULL` and `fn(integer) -> NULL` are compatible with each other.

Arity is a range, not a fixed count. The function may declare more parameters than the interface
passes, as long as the extra ones have defaults. It may not require more than the interface supplies,
and it may not refuse an argument the interface may send. An optional parameter in the expected type
promises callers they can omit it, so the function's formal must have a default.

Parameters are contravariant and the return type is covariant: each expected parameter type must be
compatible with the function's parameter type, and the function's return type must be compatible
with the expected one.

- `fn(integer | NULL) -> integer` is accepted where `fn(integer) -> integer` is expected.
- `fn(integer) -> integer` is rejected where `fn(integer | NULL) -> integer` is expected, because the
  interface may pass `NULL`.
- `fn(a: integer, [b]: integer) -> integer` is accepted where `fn(integer) -> integer` is expected,
  because `b` has a default. This is what lets a standard-library function serve as a callback:
  `lapply(list(mean, sd), function(g) g(1:3))` is `list[double]`, even though `mean` and `sd` declare
  optional formals the callback never passes.
- `fn(a: integer, b: integer) -> integer` is rejected there, because the interface never supplies `b`,
  and `fn() -> integer` is rejected because it cannot receive the argument the interface sends.
- `fn(count: integer, [label]: character) -> integer` does not accept `function(count, label) count`
  in a checked annotation, because `label` has no default. At an argument position this is not yet
  checked, which is a known gap.

A variadic function serves a fixed-arity interface, because its rest parameter requires no
arguments: `mean`, declared as `fn(x: Any, ...: Any)`, is accepted where `fn(integer[]) -> double`
is expected. The other direction is rejected, because a variadic interface may send any number of
arguments and a fixed-arity function cannot receive them. Between two variadic types, the rest
element types are contravariant like any parameter, and both sides must declare the same number of
parameters before `...`. This rejects some safe pairings, but never admits an unsound one.

A union of functions, such as a local chosen by `switch(type, a = up_a, b = up_b)`, is accepted
where one function type is expected when each member is, with its own return, and the union of
those returns satisfies the expected return. Arguments a call forwards to the callback, as in
`lapply(names, chosen, new = NULL)`, reach every member.

A declared function such as `apply` passes its `...` on to its callback, so an untyped parameter
that the body also calls takes `fn(Any, ...: Any) -> Any` when passed there, even from a call that
forwards nothing. After `apply(m, 1, fun)`, the body may still call `fun(m, na.rm = TRUE)`. A
parameter the body only passes on keeps the exact shape: `each <- function(f) lapply(list(1, 2), f)`
is `fn(f: fn(double) -> T) -> list[T]`.

A function whose parameter types are still being inferred is the one exception to exact parameter
types: after `uniroot(f, interval, scontrol = scontrol)` binds `scontrol`'s parameter to `double`,
the same body may call `scontrol(times)` on a `double[]`, because R's numeric functions are
vectorised. An annotated callback keeps its exact type.

`sapply` and `mapply` simplify their results to a vector, a matrix or a list depending on what the
callback returns, so their type follows the callback's return type and the `simplify` flag
(`SIMPLIFY` for `mapply`):

| Call | Type |
| --- | --- |
| callback returns a scalar-like `T`, flag left out or `TRUE` | `T[]` |
| flag is a literal `FALSE` | `list[T]` |
| callback returns anything else, or the flag is not a literal | `Any` |

The result is named (`T[named]`, `list[named: T]`) when the input is named or a character vector,
as R names it. `USE.NAMES = FALSE` drops the names a character input gives, and for `mapply` and
`Map` the names of a named input too:

```r
xs <- c(1, 2, 3)
doubled <- sapply(xs, function(x) x * 2)                    # double[]
kept <- sapply(xs, function(x) x * 2, simplify = FALSE)     # list[double]
pairs <- sapply(xs, function(x) c(x, x))                    # Any: a matrix in R
widths <- sapply(c("a", "bb"), function(s) nchar(s))        # integer[named]
```

A scalar-like return is trusted as length one, as everywhere else, so a callback typed scalar that
returns several elements gives a vector type to what R returns as a matrix. A generic callback is
the exception: its return was inferred before its parameter met an element, so it is trusted only
over an atomic vector, whose elements are scalars. Over a list, `sapply(xs, function(v) v * 2)`
gives `Any`, because `v` may be a vector. A return that depends on the callback's own type
parameters, as `identity`'s `T` does, gives `Any` too. `tapply` gives `Any`,
because its result is an array shaped by the groups of `INDEX`. The callbacks are still checked:
`sapply`'s takes the elements of `X`, as `lapply`'s does, so `sapply(xs, function(v) v + "a")` over
numbers is an error, and so is `sapply(xs, 4)`:

```text
expected a function that takes `double`, found `double`
```

`Map` is `mapply` with `SIMPLIFY = FALSE`, so it gives `list[T]` for a callback that returns `T`, and
`list[Any]` when the return is undetermined.

`ifelse(test, yes, no)` takes the shape of `test` and is either branch's type, so
`ifelse(x > 0, 1L, 2.5)` is `integer[] | double[]` for a vector `x`, and `ifelse(TRUE, 1L, 2L)`, whose
test is made of literals, is `integer`. A bare `NA` fits the other branch, so
`ifelse(x > 0, "positive", NA)` is `character[]`. A branch that is not a plain vector, such as a
factor, whose class `ifelse` drops, gives `Any`, and so does any other test that types as a scalar,
because a comparison on a matrix types as one and the result keeps the matrix's dimensions.

The base functions that take a function argument and resolve it with `match.fun` (`lapply`,
`sapply`, `vapply`, `mapply`, `Map`, `Filter`, `Find`, `Position`, `Reduce`, `do.call`, `outer`,
`apply`, `tapply`, `sweep`, `rapply`, `eapply`, `Vectorize`, `Negate`, `aggregate`, and `match.fun`
itself) also accept the function's name as a string. A string literal that names a known function
checks as that function, so `vapply(words, "toupper", numeric(1))` is an error because `toupper`
returns `character`. The finding sits on the template and names the callback:

```text
`toupper` returns `character`, but this `FUN.VALUE` asks for `double[]`
```

Any other string, such as `"[["` or a computed name, is `Unknown` and
unchecked. A string reaches a function-typed parameter only at these callees: a project function
that calls its `callback` parameter does not accept `"toupper"`.

When a function value is rejected at a parameter, the finding names the one position that failed
rather than printing both whole signatures: either the parameter to which the interface passes a
value the function will not take, or the function's return, which the interface will not take. An
arity mismatch still shows both signatures.

#### Callback forwarding at variadic call sites

R's apply family calls its callback as `FUN(element, ...)`, forwarding the caller's extra arguments,
so a callback with more formals than the interface declares is still correct when the call forwards
the difference. At a call to a variadic function, a function-typed argument that fails the plain
interface check is therefore checked again, as that forwarded call:

- Forwarded named arguments fill the callback's formals of the same name first, each checked against
  its formal's type exactly as a direct call checks it. So a `logical[]` forwarded to a formal whose
  inferred type is `logical` is accepted, as `show(tab, fade = fade)` would be.
- The interface's own parameter types then fill the remaining formals in order, followed by the
  forwarded positional arguments.
- Any formal left unfilled must have a default.
- The callback's return type must satisfy the interface's return type.
- If the second check fails, it binds nothing, and the reported error is the plain interface
  mismatch.

`lapply(words, gsub, pattern = "a", replacement = "o")` is therefore checked as
`gsub(word, pattern = "a", replacement = "o")` and is `list[character]`, and `lapply(words, nchar)`
accepts `nchar`'s optional formals. A forwarded argument of the wrong type fails the check, and the
call is an error.

Inside a wrapper, a call that forwards its own `...` to a function parameter, as in
`function(func, x, ...) func(unique(x), ...)`, demands only the parameters its other arguments fill,
because the forwarded `...` may be empty. So `dedup(basename, paths)` is accepted even though
`basename` takes a single argument. A call that passes nothing but the forwarded `...`, as in
`function(callback) function(...) callback(...)`, gives the parameter the type `fn(...: Any) -> T`,
which any function satisfies, including one with required parameters such as
`function(path) paste0(path, "/cache")` or `function(endpoint, ...) paste(endpoint, ...)`. An annotation `fn(...: Any) -> T` means the same. R
expands an argument whose value is `...` whatever its name, so `func(x, ... = ...)` forwards the
dots exactly as `func(x, ...)` does.

An unannotated parameter passed as the callback of a library function that forwards its own extra
arguments, as in `apply(v, 2, FUN = fun, na.rm = na.rm)`, takes those arguments too, so it is
typed `fn(Any, ...: Any) -> Any` and a later direct call `fun(v, na.rm = na.rm)` is clean.

### Higher-order function types

A function type may appear inside another function type, as in
`fn(transform: fn(integer) -> character) -> character`, and as an expanded `@param` type such as
`@param render_count {fn(integer) -> character}`. A nested function type cannot carry its own
binder, because binders are rank-1.

## Type annotations and assertions

### Checked annotations

`#: TYPE` is a checked annotation: the annotated value must be *compatible* with `TYPE`, so the check
allows widening wherever the rules on this page define it. When the check succeeds, the value is
accepted (through a coercion if one is needed), and the annotated binding or expression has the type
`TYPE` from then on.

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
value: list[integer] <- list(1L, 2L, 3L)
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: list[integer]
value <- list(1L, 2L, 3L)
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

This is valid, because `list{integer, integer, integer}` is compatible with `list[integer]`.

A checked annotation with a nominal type asserts that the value *already* has that type; it does not
create one. `#: Person` on a structural value is a type error, even when the value matches the
representation. Use [`@new`](#nominal-introduction) instead.

### Unknown-only coercions

`#: @if-unknown TYPE` fills a gap in inference. It is allowed only when the inferred type is
`Unknown`, and it then gives the annotated binding or expression the type `TYPE`. When the checker
already knows the type, `@if-unknown` is an error, even if the requested type matches the known one,
so it can never override information the checker has.

Inline, the directive goes between the binding's `:` and its type:

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
frame <- read.csv("a.csv")

gap: @if-unknown integer <- frame$a   # valid: `frame$a` is `Unknown`

value: @if-unknown integer <- 1L      # an error: the type is already known
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
frame <- read.csv("a.csv")

#: @if-unknown integer
gap <- frame$a   # valid: `frame$a` is `Unknown`

#: @if-unknown integer
value <- 1L      # an error: the type is already known
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

### Trusted coercions

`#: @trust TYPE` is the unchecked override: it treats the annotated value as `TYPE` without requiring
the usual compatibility at that site. It has the same effect as coercing the value to `Any` and then
to `TYPE`, and exists as a shorter way to write that.

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
callback <- function(x) x

render_count: @trust fn(count: integer) -> character <- callback
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
callback <- function(x) x

#: @trust fn(count: integer) -> character
render_count <- callback
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

A trusted coercion silences a real error as readily as a false one, so use it only when you know
more than the checker does.

### Nominal introduction

`#: @new NOMINAL_TYPE` is the one way to create a value of a nominal type. The annotated value must
be compatible with the nominal type's representation, and when it is, the annotated binding or
expression has the nominal type from then on.

- `NOMINAL_TYPE` must refer to a nominal type declared with `@type`, either by its bare name, such as
  `Person`, or as a generic application, such as `Person<integer>`. An alias, a structural type, a
  union, a function type, or any other type form is not allowed after `@new`.
- A generic nominal may be written unapplied. `@new Person` on a `Person<T>` infers the type
  arguments from the representation check, so a value `list(value = 1L)` becomes a
  `Person<integer>`.
- When the value already has the nominal type, `@new` is allowed and does nothing further.
- The representation is an expected type for the value's literals, so `@Tree(:Leaf(1))` with
  `type Tree = :Leaf(I64) | :Node(Tree, Tree)` makes the `1` an `I64`.
- The parentheses of `@Name(...)` are the value's own, so they follow the record and tuple literal
  rules: `@Point(x = 1, y = 2)` is a record, `@Pair(1L, "a")` a tuple, and `@UserId(id = 1L,)` a
  one-field record. `@UserId(id = 1L)` is an R assignment wrapped in the constructor, and when the
  representation is a one-field record with that field, the `type-mismatch` suggests the trailing
  comma.
- `@new` is an annotation form, not a type expression, so it cannot appear inside a compact type or
  an expanded function annotation.

Inline, `@Person(value)` introduces the nominal type on any expression, and `x: @new Person <- value`
on a binding:

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
type Person<T> = list{ value: T }

value <- @Person<integer>(list(value = 1L))
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: @type Person<T> {list{ value: T }}

#: @new Person<integer>
value <- list(value = 1L)
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

## Operators

### Operators over union operands

Control-flow joins and mixed containers produce union-typed operands, so every operator below accepts
a union member by member. A union operand is accepted when every member is accepted; one unacceptable
member rejects the whole operand, and the diagnostic shows the full union. The result is the join of
the per-member results, which for a binary operator means the join over every pair of left and right
members.

- `(integer | double) + integer` is `integer | double`.
- `(integer | double) > 0L` is `logical`.
- `(integer | character) + 1L` is a type error, because the `character` member is not numeric.

- `(integer | NULL) + 1L` is `integer`. R's arithmetic and comparison give an empty vector for a
  `NULL` operand rather than failing, so a `NULL` member is accepted and contributes nothing to the
  result, and `(character | NULL) == "a"` is `logical`. A class member dispatches its declared
  operator method the same way, so `(factor | NULL) == "a"` goes through `==.factor`. An operand that is only `NULL`, as in
  `NULL + 1L` or `NULL == 1L`, gives `Unknown`.
- `rec$a` on `list{a: integer} | list{a: character}` is `integer | character`. A member that lacks
  the field changes the result, as [indexing](#indexing) describes.
- A `for` loop over `integer[] | character[]` binds its variable as `integer | character`.

Two arithmetic exceptions keep correlated paths from being reported. A list-shaped member (a list,
record, or tuple without an arithmetic method) beside a numeric member is skipped, because a test
elsewhere in the body usually rules that path out, as in mgcv's `offset` that is a number or a
`list` of terms. A list or `NULL` operand beside an `Unknown` or `Any` operand is not reported
either, because the other operand may be a class whose method accepts it, as ggplot2's `+` does.

#### Conditions

`if`, `while`, and the operands of `&&` and `||` all take a *scalar condition*, and R decides what a
scalar condition admits:

- `logical` is the ordinary case.
- `integer` and `double` are accepted, and coerce as R coerces them: zero is false, and anything else
  is true. That makes `if (length(x))`, `if (nrow(df))`, and `while (n)` ordinary R.
- `complex` and `raw` are type errors, as R refuses them outright.
- A `character` condition of `if` or `while` is accepted unless it is a string literal. R reads it
  with `as.logical`, which accepts the spellings of `TRUE` and `FALSE` (such as `"T"` or `"true"`),
  and the value of `Sys.getenv("FLAG", unset = "TRUE")` presumably is one. Only a literal shows its
  text, so `if ("yes")` is reported and `if ("TRUE")` is not. The same holds for a `character`
  member of a union condition, such as `character | logical`. An operand of `&&` or `||` that is
  `character` is a type error, because R rejects any string there.
- A vector is accepted, because a vector type carries no length, and the vector a test produces,
  such as `x >= 1` on a `double[]`, has length one at run time far more often than not. Only a
  `c()` call with two or more arguments is known to be longer than one, so `if (c(TRUE, FALSE))` is
  a type error, as it is in R. A `matrix` is accepted the same way, as in `if (!m[i, j])`, and a
  named vector is accepted exactly when the unnamed vector of its element is.
- A condition whose type is still undetermined becomes `logical`, so `function(flag) if (flag) 1L`
  infers `flag: logical`. A value already required to be numeric keeps that requirement, so
  `function(n) if (n %% 2L) "odd" else "even"` is `<T: numeric> fn(n: T) -> character`. An
  unannotated formal with a default is the exception: an option flag such
  as `function(x, which = FALSE) if (which) ...` is called with `1` as often as with `TRUE`, so the
  condition leaves its type to its other uses. The same holds for `!` on such a formal.
- A union is accepted when every member other than `NULL` is. The `NULL` member is the "not
  supplied" path of an optional value, which code tests only once it holds one, so
  `if (config_flag(pkg))` on a `logical | NULL` is not reported. An undetermined member, such as a
  parameter rebound on one path (`T | logical`), becomes `logical` as a lone one would. A bare `NULL`
  condition is still a type error, as `if (NULL)` is in R.

### Indexing

`[[` extracts a single element, and `[` is R's general subsetting operator. `[` with an out-of-range
position or a missing name gives `NA` at run time, which is a property of the value, not of its type.
`[[` with a missing name gives `NULL` on a list, which the rules below record as `| NULL`, and fails
on a named vector, so there it gives the element type.

`$name` behaves like `[["name"]]` on lists, on records, and on abstract nominals, and a backtick-quoted
name follows the same rule. `$` does not work on atomic vectors, because R rejects it on every atomic
vector, named ones included: `c(foo = 1L)$foo` is a type error, while `c(foo = 1L)[["foo"]]` is
`integer`: R stops with "subscript out of bounds" when the name is absent from an atomic vector,
where a list would give `NULL`.

On a nominal whose class declares a `$` method, in a stub or in the project (`$.request` for
`@type request`), `x$name` types as that method's result instead, because R dispatches the read to
the method before it looks at the representation. The same holds for `[.class` and `[[.class`.

When the package's `R/` files, or the file being checked, define any `$` method at all, `$` on an
atomic vector is `Unknown` rather than an error, because a classed atomic vector (an igraph vertex sequence, say) can carry that method under
a class the checker does not track.

Every element of `NULL` is `NULL` in R, so `NULL$name`, `NULL[["name"]]`, and `NULL[1]` are `NULL`.
A list that may be absent is therefore read directly: on `list{retries: integer} | NULL`,
`config$retries` is `integer | NULL`.

On a union, a field may be missing from some of the members. When the members that lack it are
empty lists, the read is that field's type unioned with `NULL`, which is what R answers for the
empty list. Code that builds a list field by field therefore checks:

```r
args <- list()
if (escape) args$escape <- TRUE
args$escape        # logical | NULL
```

When a non-empty member lacks the field, the read is `Unknown`, for the reason a single record-like
list reads a missing name as `Unknown`. A field that *no* member has is still an error when it is a
near miss of a field some member has, and the "did you mean" suggestion draws on every member's
fields.

#### `[[` on vectors

- A scalar-like `T` returns `T`.
- An array-like `T[]` returns `T`.
- A map-like `T[named]` returns `T` for a name index too, because R stops with "subscript out of
  bounds" on an absent name rather than returning `NULL`.

#### `[[` on lists

- A list-like `list[T]` returns `T`. `$name` on it returns `T | NULL`, because such a list may still
  carry names at run time, and R answers `NULL` for a name it lacks.
- A dict-like `list[named: T]` returns `T | NULL` for a name index, and `T` for a positional or
  computed one.
- A tuple-like list returns the element at a literal position. A position that does not exist is an
  error, and a computed position returns the union of the item types.
- A record-like list returns the field at a literal name or literal position, and a computed index
  returns the union of the field types, which is how a call to a function looked up in a list gets
  its type. A position that does not exist is an error.
- A literal name a record-like list lacks reads as `Unknown`, because R lists are open: a list
  literal is often only the start of a value other code adds fields to. A name that is a near miss
  of a field, such as `opts$verbsoe` for `verbose`, is still an error with a "did you mean"
  suggestion. `$` also matches a unique prefix of a field name, as R does, so `opts$verb` reads
  `verbose`.

#### `[` on vectors

The result of `[` on a vector depends on the shape of the index:

- A scalar-like `integer`, `double`, or `character` index gives the scalar element type. That is not
  always exact, since `x[-1]` drops one element and returns the rest, but a scalar coerces into every
  vector position, so the claim can never cause a false error later.
- An array-like or map-like numeric or character index, such as `x[c(1L, 3L)]`, keeps the subject's
  shape.
- A `logical` index of any shape is a mask, as in `x[x > 0]`, and keeps the subject's shape.
- `NULL` selects nothing and gives the array-like vector of the element type.
- An index whose shape is undetermined, such as an unannotated parameter or an `Unknown`, counts as
  scalar-like and is left unconstrained.
- A `complex` or `raw` index is a type error, as is a list, a function, or any other index that is not
  a vector.

Keeping the subject's shape means `E[]` on a scalar-like `E` or an array-like `E[]`, and `E[named]` on
`E[named]`, because `[` keeps names. A character index is allowed on every vector shape, not only a
map-like one, because R returns `NA` rather than failing when the subject has no names, and most
operations erase names.

- `c(1L, 2L, 3L)[2L]` is `integer`.
- `c(1L, 2L, 3L)[c(1L, 3L)]` is `integer[]`.
- `c(a = 1L, b = 2L)[c("a", "b")]` is `integer[named]`.
- `x[list(1)]` is a type error.

#### `[` on lists

`[` slices a list, so a fixed shape does not survive into the result:

- A list-like `list[T]` returns `list[T]`.
- A dict-like `list[named: T]` returns `list[named: T]`.
- A tuple-like list returns `list[T]`, where `T` is the union of the item types, so
  `list(1L, "foo")[1L]` is `list[integer | character]`.
- A record-like list returns `list[named: T]`, where `T` is the union of the field types.
- Slicing the empty list gives `list[NULL]`.

#### Indexing a value whose shape is unknown

`$`, `[[`, and `[` on a value whose shape the checker has not determined, such as an unannotated
parameter, give `Unknown` and leave the value unconstrained. So `function(node) node$value`,
`function(x) x[[1L]]`, and `function(x) x[1L]` report nothing, and neither does indexing with several
indices, as in `function(m, i, j) m[i, j]`. Ordinary R reads fields and slices off values whose shape
nobody wrote down, so refusing here would report correct code. Like access on an abstract nominal, such
an access is an unsupported construct under [strict mode](#strict-mode).

Indexing a `matrix` follows R's `drop` rule. A matrix does not record its element type, so the
elements are `Any`. When both indices are empty or vectors, as in `m[1:2, 1:2]` or `m[, ]`, the result
is a `matrix`; a scalar index drops that dimension, so `m[i, j]` and `m[i, ]` are `Any`; and
`drop = FALSE` keeps the `matrix` whatever the indices are. A single index, `m[i]`, reads elements and
is `Any`.

Any other indexing with several indices, which is data frame selection, or with a named argument, is
not modelled. It gives `Unknown` without a report, so `c(1L, 2L)[1L, 2L]` passes, and strict mode
reports it as an undetermined type. An empty index is
the subject itself, so `x[]` on `list{integer, integer}` is `list{integer, integer}`.

### Arithmetic operators

The arithmetic operators are defined for numeric operands:

- `integer` and `double`;
- `logical`, which R promotes to `integer` before arithmetic, so `TRUE + TRUE` is `2L`;
- `complex`, which makes the result `complex`, so `1i + 2` and `1i / 2L` are `complex`;
- an unannotated value, which is then required to be numeric (see below);
- a class that declares an [operator method](#operator-methods-on-a-class).

The result is scalar-like when both operands are scalar-like, and array-like otherwise, so a map-like
operand gives an array-like result. Unary `-` keeps a scalar-like or array-like operand's shape. The
result's atomic type depends on the operator:

- `+`, `-`, `*`, `%%`, and `%/%` give `integer` when both operands are `integer` (or `logical`), and
  `double` otherwise.
- `/`, `^`, and `**` (R's parser alias for `^`) always give `double`.
- Unary `-` keeps its operand's atomic type. A class whose arithmetic takes two of its values
  negates to itself, because R dispatches unary `-` through the same methods, so `-(m %*% m)` is a
  `matrix` and `-elapsed` on a `difftime` is a `difftime`. `-day` on a `Date` is a type error, as R
  stops on it.
- R rejects `-NULL`, and so does the checker. A value the body computes that may be `NULL` negates
  as its other members, because code like `if (length(k)) res <- res[-k]` negates only once the
  value exists. A parameter or an annotated variable keeps its `NULL`, so `-x` on a declared
  `x: double | NULL` is reported.

For example, `double * integer[]` is `double[]`, `integer[named] + integer` is `integer[]`,
`2L ^ 3L` is `double`, and `-c(foo = 1L, bar = 2L)` is `integer[]`. Every `%op%` operator other than
`%%` and `%/%` is covered under [operator methods on a class](#operator-methods-on-a-class).

#### Unannotated values in arithmetic

An unannotated value used as an arithmetic operand is *required to be a number* rather than
rejected: it must end up as an R number (`integer` or `double`, in any vector shape), a class with
arithmetic, or a ry number where ry's arithmetic is defined on it. The operator and the other
operand pick the [constraint](#constraints) and the result:

| Operands | Constraint | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `x` beside a whole number literal (`x + 1L`) | `arithmetic or number` | `T` |
| `x` beside a whole `double` literal (`x * 2`) | `arithmetic or number` | `Promoted<T>` |
| `x` beside a fractional literal (`x * 0.5`), or under `/` | `arithmetic or F64` | `Promoted<T>` |
| two of one type, under `+`, `*`, `%%` or `%/%` | `num` | `T` |
| two of one type, under `-` | `arithmetic or number` | `Subtracted<T>` |
| two of one type, under `/` | `numeric or F64` | `Promoted<T>` |
| two of one type, under `^` | `num` | `Promoted<T>` |
| beside any other value | `arithmetic` | R's result |
| `x` beside a number literal, under `<`, `>`, `<=` or `>=` (`x > 0`) | `comparable or number` | `Compared<T>` |
| `x` beside a string literal, under `<`, `>`, `<=` or `>=` | `comparable or Str` | `Compared<T>` |
| `x` beside a number literal, under `==` or `!=` (`x == 0`) | `equatable or number` | `Compared<T>` |
| `x` beside a string literal, under `==` or `!=` (`x == "a"`) | `equatable or Str` | `Compared<T>` |
| a value compared with itself, or with another open value, under `==` or `!=` | `eq` | `Compared<T>` |
| a value compared with itself, or with another open value, under `<`, `>`, `<=` or `>=` | `comparable or ordered` | `Compared<T>` |

A whole literal is one with no fractional part, so `2.0` counts as whole and `2.5` does not. A
class such as `Date` adds a number but not another value of its own, so two operands of one type
take `num` under `+`. A difference `a - b` is the exception, because R subtracts two dates or two
times: it asks only `arithmetic` on the R side, and the difference of two dates is a `difftime`,
so its result is `Subtracted<T>`: a `double`, the representation every arithmetic class shares,
unless the class has arithmetic between two of its values (two `difftime`s subtract to a
`difftime`). Beside a number, a class with arithmetic keeps its class: `Sys.Date() - 0.5` is a
`Date`.

A family result keeps both outcomes while it is bound, returned, passed to a function, or used as
another operand. Anywhere only an R value fits, such as a subscript (`(x * 2)[1]`), a `for`
sequence, `c()`, an overloaded base function (`abs(x - y)`), or an operand beside another open
value, `Promoted<T>` and `Subtracted<T>` are `double`, `Compared<T>` is `logical`, and the operand takes its
constraint's R half. A value tested as a condition (`if (a %% 2L)`, `!a`, `a && b`) takes R's half
the same way, because a condition reads R's truth of a number, which a ry number lacks.

- `function(x) x + 1L` is `<T: arithmetic or number> fn(x: T) -> T`, and calling it with `"oops"`
  is a type error. Calling it with an `I64` is an `I64`.
- `function(x) x / 2` is `<T: arithmetic or F64> fn(x: T) -> Promoted<T>`: an `F64` for an `F64`,
  a `double` for an R number, and a type error for an `I64`.
- `function(a, b) a + b` is `<T: num> fn(a: T, b: T) -> T`, and calling it with two dates is a
  type error. Calling it with two `I64`s is an `I64`.
- `function(a, b) a - b` is `<T: arithmetic or number> fn(a: T, b: T) -> Subtracted<T>`, and gives
  a `double` for two dates and an `I64` for two `I64`s.
- `function(x) x > 0` is `<T: comparable or number> fn(x: T) -> Compared<T>`, a `Bool` for an
  `I64` and a `logical` for a `double` or a `character`. Calling it with a `Str` is a type error,
  because ry compares a `Str` only with a `Str`.

- `function(a, b) a < b` is `<T: comparable or ordered, U: comparable or ordered> fn(a: T, b: U)
  -> Compared<T>`. The two operands stay separate types, because R compares an `integer` with a
  `character` and a function may return either operand unchanged: `cmp(1L, "s")` is a `logical`,
  and `keep <- function(x, lo) { if (any(x < lo)) warning("low"); x }` returns a `Date` for
  `keep(Sys.Date(), "2020-01-01")`. ry compares a ry value only with a value of its own type, so a
  call that passes one checks the other operand against it, as a parameter of that type would:
  two `Str`s give a `Bool`, `cmp(1i64, 2)` takes the literal as an `I64`, and `cmp(1i64, "s")` and
  `cmp(1i64, 2.5)` are type errors. The check follows the pair through a function that forwards
  it (`function(p, q) cmp(p, q)`) or returns a function comparing it (`function(q) function(a) a <
  q`), and through a function passed where a function type is declared, although the signature
  does not show it, and through a function with a declared return type. A literal default adapts
  as a passed literal does, so `function(a, lo = 0) a < lo` takes an `I64`, while `lo = 2.5` or
  `lo = sqrt(2)` keeps its `double` and refuses one. An operand compared with an R value admits
  only R values, so `function(a, b: double) a < b` refuses an `I64` for `a`. Two records are a
  type error.

A ry `List` meets a comparison's constraint through its items, as it compares item by item, and a
ry nominal through its representation. A declared binder widens over R's types, so
`Bool.is_eq(1L, "one")` is a `Bool`, while `Bool.is_eq(1i64, "one")` is a type error. A
`complex` value passes `function(x) x == 0` but not `function(x) x > 0`, because R tests it for
equality and has no order for it. Two gaps remain: the result of two different open operands
follows the left one alone, so `cmp(1L, 1:3)` is a `logical` rather than a `logical[]`, and an
`I64` beside an R `integer` scalar (`cmp(1i64, x)` for `x: integer`) is a type error, although the
runtime compares them.

A callback whose family result must have its operand's type, as a fold's accumulator must, takes
a type the family keeps. A comparison settles the accumulator as R's `logical` when it fits, else as
`Bool`. Arithmetic leaves it open, and the fold's call gives `Promoted<T>` of it, so
`Reduce(function(acc, x) acc * 2, 1:3, 1L)` is the `double` R's arithmetic makes of the `integer`
init, and so is a callback that returns the accumulator unchanged in one branch
(`if (x > 1L) acc * 2 else acc`). `List.walk(xs, k, function(acc, x) acc * 2)` with `k: I64` is an
`I64`, and an init of a vector or a class gives the fold its own type:
`Reduce(function(acc, x) acc * 2, list(1:3, 4:6), c(1L, 2L, 3L))` is a `double[]`, and
`Reduce(function(acc, x) acc - 0.5, 1:3, Sys.Date())` a `Date`. A fold's result is its init or its
callback's, so `Reduce(function(a, b) a < b, 1:3, 0L)` is `logical | integer`. An init that a
function's parameter gives stays generic:

```r
doubled <- function(ys, init) List.walk(ys, init, function(acc, y) acc * 2)
# doubled: <T: item, U: arithmetic or number> fn(ys: List<T>, init: U) -> Promoted<U>
```

So `doubled([1i64], 1i64)` is an `I64`, `doubled([1i64], 1L)` a `double`, and
`doubled([1i64], "a")` a type error. The parameter itself keeps its type, so a function that also
returns `init` returns `U`. Any function with a callback parameter `fn(A, T) -> A` folds the same
way: its result reads each `A` as `Promoted<A>`, so for
`give <- function<A>(f: fn(A, integer) -> A, a: A) -> fn(A, integer) -> A f`,
