`give(function(acc, x) acc * 2, 1L)` is `fn(double, integer) -> double`, while a result that does
not name `A` keeps its type. R's fold returns the init itself for an empty `xs`, which the type does
not track: `Reduce(function(acc, x) acc * 2, integer(), 1L)` types as `double` and runs as `1L`.

When the other operand is a vector, the unannotated operand stays open and the result is a `double`
vector, so `f <- function(x) x + c(1L, 2L)` is `<T: arithmetic> fn(x: T) -> double[]` and accepts
`f(TRUE)` and `f(c(1, 2))` alike. When the other operand is a union, the unannotated operand is pinned
to a scalar `double`, which matches how R treats bare numbers. An operand whose shape is still unknown
counts as scalar-like here and in comparisons, by the same claim that
[`[` on vectors](#-on-vectors-1) makes. A generic vector written `T[]` is the exception, and its
operator results really are vectors.

A union operand is checked member by member. A union of numeric types, such as `double | integer`,
follows the rules above. A union that also holds an unannotated value or a class with arithmetic
methods, such as `double | matrix` from `if (is.null(previous)) 1 else x %*% x`, requires its
unannotated members to be numeric, and the result is `Unknown`, because R picks the result type per
member at run time. A member that is neither, such as the `character` in `double | character`, is a
type error.

A `logical` argument satisfies the numeric requirement, because R promotes it, so
`cost <- function(price, rate) price * rate` accepts `cost(TRUE, 0.5)` and `cost(1L, 0.5)`. Mixed
arguments widen in R's order, `logical` to `integer` to `double`, so both calls are `double`. A
union of numbers widens to its widest member, so `cost(1L, if (fine) 0.5 else 1L)` is `double` too.
A `matrix` absorbs any number, logical or numeric vector the same way, because R's arithmetic gives a
matrix: with `weigh <- function(a, H) a * H`, both `weigh(2, m)` and `weigh(m, 2)` are `matrix`. A
matrix's element type is not tracked, so a character matrix is absorbed too.

A `logical` argument binds a numeric type parameter to `integer`, the type R's arithmetic reads it
as, so with `bump <- function(x) x + 1L`, `bump(TRUE)` is `integer`, as in R. A function that
returns its `logical` argument unchanged, such as `keep <- function(x) { x + 0L; x }`, is then
reported to return `integer` where R returns the `logical`. That claim is safe, because a `logical`
fits wherever an `integer` is expected, while the opposite claim would type R's `2L` as a flag.

An argument that is itself still generic, such as the parameter `H` in
`outer <- function(H) weigh(2, H)`, is constrained to be numeric rather than pinned to the literal's
`double`, so `outer` is `<T: numeric> fn(H: T) -> double` and accepts a vector or a matrix. The result
keeps the literal's `double`, which is exact for a scalar argument and approximate for a vector or
matrix one. A declared numeric type parameter is accepted the same way.

### Operator methods on a class

When an operand of an operator is a nominal type, the operator first dispatches to the class's
declared operator method, before any numeric rule applies, just as R dispatches `d + 30L` on a `Date`
through `+.Date`.

The lookup follows R's order: first the operator-specific method, such as `+.Date`; then the
operator's S3 group generic, `Arith.Date` for arithmetic and `Compare.Date` for comparison; and
finally `Ops.Date`. Either operand's class may supply the method, the left one first, so `30L + d`
behaves like `d + 30L`. The method name's suffix is the nominal type's name, not R's full class
vector, so a class declared as `@type ggplot` takes `+.ggplot`, even though R registers the method
as `+.gg`.

A method is declared under the name R gives it, in a stub or an annotation. A stub sees only the
types stubs define, so the methods of a type your R code defines, such as `@type Money {double}`, are
annotated R functions beside it (`` `+.Money` <- function(e1, e2) ... `` under
`#: fn(e1: Money, e2: Money) -> Money`). Declaring each method keeps the result
precise for each pairing of operands: subtracting two `Date` values gives a `difftime`, and offsetting
one by a count gives a `Date`. A class that declares an operator but has no candidate that accepts
the operands at hand is a `type-mismatch` error rather than a fall-through to the numeric rules, just
as in R. A class that declares nothing does fall through to the numeric rules, so arithmetic on an
abstract nominal is still a type error. An unannotated operand beside a class operand does not take
the class from the first candidate, because R accepts a plain vector there too. It is only required
to be numeric, so in `function(omega, v) omega + v %*% t(v)`, `omega` is a `T: numeric`, and a call
with two plain vectors is accepted.

A union operand beside a class dispatches member by member, and the result is the union of the
members' results. So the accumulator `total <- if (first) 0 else m %*% m; total + m %*% m` takes
`Arith.matrix` for both `double` and `matrix` and is a `matrix`, while a `character` member is
reported as `` `+` is not defined between `character` and `matrix` ``. A member with no class takes
the ordinary operator rules, so `x >= 0` on `matrix | double` is `logical`. When some member is
rejected that way, or no member is a class, the union is checked by the ordinary operator rules as a
whole.

Your own classes count. A method declared anywhere the global scope reaches, which includes a
package's `R/` sources and a script's own top level, makes its class support that operator exactly as
a shipped stub does. That is also how the class satisfies the arithmetic requirement: passing a
`Money` to `function(x) x + 1L` is accepted when the project defines `+.Money`, and refused when it
defines no arithmetic method. Passing two to `function(a, b) a + b` needs a method whose first two
operands both take a `Money`, as `function(e1: Money, e2: Money)` does.

R also dispatches on a class that `class(x) <- "string"` sets on a plain list or an environment
(ggplot2's ggproto objects are classed environments), and no type tracks that attribute. So when the
project defines an arithmetic or `Ops` method for a class that no type declares, or registers one
through S4's `setMethod("+", ...)` or S7's `method(\`+\`, ...)`, arithmetic with a
list or environment operand is left to that method: `result + "text"` on such a list, and the rest
of a chain `result + a + b`, give `Unknown` and report nothing. Without such a method,
`list(a = 1) + 1` is a type error, as it is in R.

`c()` dispatches the same way. A class that declares a `c.Class` method keeps its class through
concatenation, so `c(d1, d2)` on two `Date` values is a `Date`. The stubs keep `Date` and `POSIXct`
through `[`, `min`, `max`, `range`, `sort`, `rev`, `unique`, `head` and `tail`, as R's methods do.
A `for` loop over dates gives its variable the type `double`, because R strips the class there.
`Reduce(f, dates)` is a type error, although R runs it, because `Reduce` takes a `list[T] | T[]`
and a `Date` vector has no element type to write. A nominal with no such method loses
its class but keeps its data, as R's default `c()` does, so two values of `@type Money {double}`
combine to `double[]`. An abstract nominal has no representation, so combining it gives `Unknown`.

`a %op% b` is the call `` `%op%`(a, b) ``. A `%…%` operator that the standard-library stubs declare,
or that the project defines as a top-level function, is checked and typed as that call, so
`"a" %in% valid` is `logical`, `m %*% m` is a `matrix`, and with
`` `%plus%` <- function(a, b) a + b `` annotated `fn(a: double, b: double) -> double`,
`1 %plus% "a"` is reported at `"a"`. A project operator that captures an operand's code with
`substitute()` is non-standard evaluation, as magrittr's `%>%` is, so it gives `Unknown` and its
operands are not judged. An operator of a package without stubs, or one defined inside a function,
gives `Unknown` too. Using any `%…%` operator still counts as a read of its name, so a project's own
operator is never reported as unused.

### Comparison operators

`<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, `==`, and `!=` compare two `logical`, `integer`, `double`, or `character`
operands, mixed freely. R coerces both operands to a common type before comparing, so
`Sys.getenv("RSTUDIO") == 1` and `1L < "a"` are legal R and are not reported. A `complex` operand
compares under `==` and `!=` only, so `1i == 0` is a `logical` and `1i < 2` is a type error. `raw`
operands are not supported. A map-like vector takes part through its
compatibility with an array-like vector. A `NULL` operand, or a `NULL` member of a union, is
accepted, because R answers an empty `logical` vector instead of failing, as
[union operands](#operators-over-union-operands) describe. A list compared with an atomic value
is accepted, because R compares it element by element when each element is a length-one atomic,
so `parts[parts != "null"]` on a parsed list and `lapply(xs, anyDuplicated) > 0` are not reported.
Two lists never compare, so `list(1L) == list(1L)` is a type error, as it is in R. ry's records,
tuples and tags compare only for equality, structurally, as
[their equality](#equality-of-records-tuples-and-tags) describes.

An unannotated operand is left unconstrained, whatever the other operand is, so
`function(x) x > 0` is `<T> fn(x: T) -> logical` and `function(a, b) a < b` is
`<T, U> fn(a: T, b: U) -> logical`. A comparison against a number is often how R code tests a
string or a flag, so it is not evidence that the operand is numeric. The cost is that a caller's
mistake can pass unreported: with `f <- function(x) x > 0`, `f(function() 1)` is accepted, although
R fails the comparison. Comparing a value whose type is known, as in `1 > function() 1`, is still a
type error.

The result is always `logical`: scalar-like when both operands are scalar-like, and array-like
otherwise. So `1L == 1.5` and `1L < "a"` are `logical`, and `c(TRUE, FALSE) > 0` is `logical[]`.

### Unary `!`

Logical negation coerces its operand exactly as a [scalar condition](#conditions) does, so `!0` is
`TRUE` and `!5` is `FALSE`. Its result is always logical:

- `!logical`, `!integer`, and `!double` are `logical`.
- `!logical[]`, `!integer[]`, and `!double[]` are `logical[]`.
- A map-like operand gives `logical[]`, because negation does not preserve map-likeness.
- A `matrix` or `array` negates to itself, so `!as.matrix(flags)` is a `matrix`, and so does a
  class with a `!` or `Ops` method, which R dispatches `!` to. A `-` or `Arith` method is not
  enough, so `!Sys.Date()` is a type error, as it is in R.
- `!NULL` is `logical[]`, the empty vector R returns, so a value that may be `NULL` negates without
  error: `!x` on `logical | NULL` is `logical[]`.
- An `Any` or `Unknown` operand gives `Unknown`.
- An operand whose type is still undetermined is constrained to `logical`, and the result is
  `logical`, except for an unannotated formal with a default, which stays open as it does in a
  [condition](#conditions).
- A union negates when each member does, and an undetermined member counts as one that does. An
  unannotated parameter shaped by an `is.null()` test has such a type, so after
  `if (is.null(read)) read <- !write` the `!write` is not an error.
- Any other operand is a type error.

### Range operator `:`

`from:to` builds a numeric sequence. Each endpoint may be an `integer`, a `double`, or a `logical`,
which R takes as a number, or a union of those. A vector endpoint is accepted too, because R uses its
first element and a vector type carries no length; only a `c()` call with two or more arguments is
known to be too long, so `1:c(1L, 2L)` is a type error, and so is a non-numeric endpoint. The
result is `integer[]` when both endpoints are `integer` or `logical`, and `double[]` when either
may be `double`, so `1:TRUE` is `integer[]` and `1:n` on `n` of type `integer | double` is
`double[]`. A whole-number `double` literal such as `1` or `10`
counts as `integer` here, matching what R does at run time, so `1:10` is `integer[]` and `1.5:3L` is
`double[]`.

R rejects a `NULL` endpoint, and so does the checker, except for an endpoint read through a `$`
field of a value the body computed (`b$off[i]`, with `b` returned by a call). Such a field is
typically filled under a flag, as in mgcv's `off`, and read only by callers that set the flag, so
its `NULL` member is dropped. A local, a value indexed directly, and a field of a parameter keep
their `NULL`, so `cfg$off:3L` on a parameter `cfg: list{off: integer | NULL}` is reported. The
`NULL` of an unannotated parameter's default and of a variable the body computes is the
placeholder that [function calls](#function-calls) drop, so it is dropped here too:
`rows[1:.after]` on `.after = NULL` is clean when the code sets `.after` before it builds the
sequence.

Base R's `seq(from, to)` without `by` or `length.out` returns `from:to`, so it follows the same
rule: `seq(1, 10)` is `integer[]`, and so is `seq(n)` with one argument, while `seq(0.5, 3)` and
`seq(1, 10, by = 2)` are `double[]`. A project that defines its own `seq` gets its own typing.

An unannotated operand, as in `1:n`, is required to be a scalar `integer` or `double`, and the
result is `double[]`, because the endpoint may turn out to be a `double`. Like any
[inferred scalar](#argument-compatibility), that requirement records a use, not a length, so a
caller may pass a numeric vector such as the `integer[]` of `which()`, whose first element R takes.
A `c()` call of two or more arguments is reported at the call, because it is known to be too long.

### Combine `c(...)`

`c(...)` builds a vector from arguments of any shape:

- With no arguments, `c()` is `NULL`, as in R.
- `NULL` arguments are dropped, as in R, so `c(x, NULL)` is `c(x)` and `c(NULL)` is `NULL`.
- A union-typed argument takes part member by member. Its `NULL` members are dropped first, and every
  remaining member joins the coercion like a separate argument. So with `acc` of type
  `double[] | NULL`, `c(acc, 1.0)` is `double[]`, and `c(if (a) list(1), if (a) list(2))` is
  `list[double]`.
- When any argument is a list, `c` concatenates into a list-like `list[T]` whose element type is the
  join of every argument's elements, with an atomic argument contributing its own type:
  `c(list(1L), "a")` is `list[integer | character]`. An empty list adds no element, so
  `c(list(), f)` is `list[fn]` and `c(list(), list())` is the empty list. The atomic rules below
  apply only when no argument is a list.
- An argument whose element type is not known statically, such as `Any`, `Unknown`, an unannotated
  parameter, or a union or vector that holds one (`T | NULL`, `T[]`), is tolerated, and the whole
  result is `Unknown`, because a concrete element type could
  be widened by a later argument. So `function(x) c(x, 1L)` is `<T> fn(x: T) -> Unknown`. Under
  strict mode, an unannotated parameter makes the `c()` call the origin, an `Unknown` argument was
  already reported where it arose, and an `Any` argument is not reported.
- Mixed atomic arguments coerce to the widest type in R's order,
  `logical < integer < double < complex < character`.
- `c()` never reports an argument, because R's `c()` accepts every value. An argument the order
  does not cover, such as a function or a `raw` beside another atomic type, makes the result
  `Unknown`, so `c(as.raw(1L), 2L)` and `c(function() 1, 2)` are `Unknown`.
- When every argument is named, the result is a map-like `T[named]`; otherwise it is an array-like
  `T[]`.

For example, `c(TRUE, 1L)` and `c(1L, NA)` are `integer[]`, `c(1L, "a")` is `character[]`, and
`c(foo = 1L, bar = 2L)` is `integer[named]`.

### Assignment operator `<-`

`name <- expr` writes the type of `expr` into the variable slot for `name` in the current scope,
creating the slot on the first write (see [value names](#value-names) for the slot model). The
assignment expression itself has the type of `expr`, so `y <- (x <- 1L)` gives both `x` and `y` the
type `integer`. When the assignment carries an annotation, `expr` is checked by the annotation rules.

- A string in place of the name binds that name: `"x" <- 1` is `x <- 1`, and so is `` `x` <- 1 ``.
  For a string target, the range of any finding is the literal, quotes included.
- A target that is not a name, such as a computed value or a number, is reported as a
  `syntax-error`, because R refuses it at run time. A target headed by `!` is exempt, because `!`
  binds tighter than `<-`, so rlang's `expr(!!name <- value)` builds an assignment instead of
  performing one.
- A later assignment in the same scope writes the same variable. On a straight-line path the new
  write replaces the old type, and writes that arrive from different paths are joined (see
  [control-flow joins](#control-flow-joins)). So after `x <- 1L; x <- "foo"`, `x` is `character`, and
  after `x <- 1L; if (flag) x <- "foo"`, it is `integer | character`.
- `x <<- v` writes the nearest enclosing frame that defines `x`, or the global environment. When
  an enclosing function defines `x`, the rest of the writing function reads `x` as `v`, and that
  function joins `v` with its own writes, because a closure may run at any time after it is created. A write whose type is still
  undetermined, such as a callback's result, joins as a union member instead of binding the other
  writes, so a reset `cache <<- TRUE` beside `cache <<- fun()` leaves `fun` generic. A write to a
  top-level variable is read back in the rest of the writing body: after `cache <- 1L`, the body
  `cache <<- "a"; cache` reads `cache` as `character`. A write on one path joins with the
  top-level binding, so `if (flag) cache <<- "a"; cache` reads `integer | character`, except
  that a top-level `NULL` is a placeholder the write fills and does not join. Other items still
  read the top-level binding, and so does the enclosing body of a nested function that writes it.
- A closure may run many times, so on entry an enclosing variable that may still be `NULL` holds
  either that or what the closure's own first `<<-` writes. The lazily filled cache
  `if (is.null(cached)) cached <<- dir("css"); cached` therefore reads as `character[]`. A read
  before that first write already fixes the type, so a first write of another type is an error:
  after `y <- if (is.null(x)) 0 else x + 1`, the write `x <<- "a"` is reported.
- zeallot's `c(a, b) %<-% value` gives each name the type of its position. For a tuple or record
  value that is the item's type, so after `c(lo, hi) %<-% list(1, 2)` both are `double`. For a
  `list[T]` it is `T`, and for a vector it is the element type. A nested `c(...)` destructures its
  position the same way, `.` skips a position, and `...rest` takes the remaining items. When a tuple
  or record has a different number of items than the pattern has names and the pattern has no
  `...rest`, the assignment is an error, because zeallot stops at run time.

#### Recursion

A function can call itself, because its own name is visible inside its body.
`fact <- function(k) if (k <= 1L) 1L else k * fact(k - 1L)` is `fn(k: integer) -> integer`, and a
call that violates the recursively inferred signature is an error. The recursive uses share one
instantiation, so there is no polymorphic recursion.

Two local functions that call each other are beyond this, because names are bound one at a time. The
forward reference still resolves, since a closure sees later writes to its frame, but it stays
`Unknown`.

At a package's top level, a self-recursive definition and a group of mutually recursive definitions
are both re-derived until their types stop changing, starting from `Unknown`. Simple recursion
converges to its precise type: a top-level `fact` exports `fn(n: integer) -> integer`, and the pair
`is_even` and `is_odd` exports `<T: numeric> fn(n: T) -> logical`.

A self-reference whose type would grow without bound cannot converge without recursive types. A tree
fold is one: its parameter would need `T = double | list[T]`, so such a group settles at `Unknown`. A
cycle can also converge with an `Unknown` inside it, and a pure self-call such as
`f <- function() f()` settles at `fn() -> Unknown`. Either way, strict mode reports it, and an
explicit annotation on the binding closes the cycle exactly.

### Boolean operators `&&` and `||`

Both operands are [scalar conditions](#conditions), and the result is a scalar `logical`. As in a
condition, a vector operand is accepted unless it is a `c()` call with two or more arguments, so
`v && TRUE` on a `logical[]` passes, while `c(TRUE, FALSE) && TRUE` and `TRUE || c(FALSE, TRUE)` are
type errors.

### Elementwise operators `&` and `|`

Each operand is a `logical`, `integer`, or `double` scalar or vector, and the result is `logical`.
It is a scalar when both operands are scalars, and `logical[]` otherwise, so `a > 0 & b > 0` on two
`double[]` parameters is `logical[]`. An operand whose type is `Any` makes the result `Any`, and
one whose type is `Unknown` makes it `Unknown`, which strict mode reports where the operand's type
was lost. An operand with no type yet, such as an unannotated parameter, could be a scalar or a
vector, so the operator gives `Unknown` and strict mode reports it; annotate the parameter.

### Formula operator `~`

A formula, one-sided (`~ x`) or two-sided (`y ~ x + z`), is the nominal type `formula`. Its operands
name model variables, which are usually columns of a data frame rather than bindings in scope, so
they are not checked: `y ~ undefined_name + "text"` reports nothing. Annotate a formula variable or
parameter as `formula`. `==` and `!=` compare two formulas by their text, as R deparses them first,
so `formula(model) == ~1` is `logical`.

## Calls

### Function calls

A call evaluates to its callee's return type. When the callee expression or its return type is
`Unknown`, the call is `Unknown`. When the callee is a union whose members are all function types,
the call must be valid against every member, since the value could be any of them, and it evaluates
to the union of the members' return types. No member's argument bindings leak into another's.
Calling a function looked up in a list, as in `handlers[[name]](...)`, has this shape, and so does
a variable that holds such a lookup, as `handler <- handlers[[name]]` does.

A union of functions that take different parameters but is not a lookup usually comes from code
that picks a function by an argument value, as rlang's `compat("warn")` returns a one-message shim
while `compat("is_interactive")` returns a function of no arguments. A call through such a value
presumes a member that accepts it, so it is reported only when no member does. When some members
accept the call and others do not, the call is `Unknown`.

A callee whose union joins functions with plain values, such as `(fn(id: T) -> character) | character`,
comes from a function whose result kind depends on an argument, as `prefixer(namespace)` below
returns its inner function exactly when `id` is missing. The call presumes the function: it is
checked against the function members only and is `Unknown`. A union of functions and `NULL` alone
is different and is reported, as [called names](#called-names) describes.

```r
prefixer <- function(namespace, id) {
  f <- function(id) paste(namespace, id, sep = "-")
  if (missing(id)) f else f(id)
}
scoped <- function(namespace, name) prefixer(namespace)(name)
```

An argument that names a variable the body assigns without an annotation drops its `NULL` member
when the parameter's type admits no `NULL`. The `NULL` there comes from a `switch` without a default,
an `if` without `else`, or a helper that returns `NULL` on some path, and the code presumably does
not reach the call on that path. A `switch()` without a default written directly as the argument
is treated the same way, as in Matrix's `new(switch(shape, g = "dgeMatrix", t = "dtrMatrix"))`. So `type <- switch(type, logical = "a logical %s", integer =
"an integer %s")` followed by `sprintf(type, kind)` is clean. A parameter the body does not
reassign, and a variable whose assignment is annotated, keep their `NULL`, so passing a
`name: character | NULL` parameter on to a `character` parameter is still reported. The `NULL` of
an unannotated parameter is a placeholder of the same kind, whether it comes from a `NULL` default
or from an `is.null()` test: in `function(H = NULL, Z) { ...; if (quadratic) scale_by(Z, H) }`, where
`quadratic` was set from an `is.null(H)` test, `H` passes to a numeric parameter without its `NULL`.

Length works the same way. A variable the body computes as `character | character[]`, because a
helper returns a vector on some path, passes to a parameter that takes one `character` as that one
string, so `pattern <- format_message("^%s=", name)` followed by `sub(pattern, "", spec)` is clean.
Only such a union qualifies: a variable known to hold a vector, as `flags <- c(TRUE, FALSE)` does,
is still reported at a `logical` parameter, and so is a declared vector. An element `x[i]` of such a
variable, passed as an argument, drops the variable's placeholder `NULL` too, since an element can
be `NULL` only through the vector. As an endpoint of `:` it keeps the `NULL`, because a test such as
`length(range) < 3` admits the empty vector.

A call of an unannotated helper, written directly as the argument, makes the same claim about
length but keeps its `NULL`, because a test can narrow only a variable. With `format_maybe`
returning its `character[]` argument when no values follow and `sprintf(fmt, ...)` otherwise,
`gsub(format_maybe("$%s", name), "value", x)` is clean. An unannotated function passed by name
as a callback drops the `NULL` an `if` without `else` adds to its return, so
`vapply(rows, render_row, character(1L), type = "row")` checks when `render_row` returns a string
for every `type` the code passes.

A call is a type error when a required argument is missing, when it passes too many arguments to a
callee without a rest parameter, or when an argument is incompatible with its parameter's type. The
[named and positional parameter rules](#named-and-positional-parameters) apply throughout. A missing
named argument is reported by name, as in "this call leaves out `y`, which the function requires",
and too many arguments are counted as the positional arguments against the
positional slots that named arguments left open.

#### Matching arguments to parameters

Arguments are matched in R's order, and names compare case-sensitively:

1. Every argument given by name claims the parameter of exactly that name, before any positional
   argument is placed.
2. A remaining named argument claims a parameter declared before the rest parameter whose name it
   is a prefix of, when exactly one unclaimed parameter has that prefix. So
   `list.files(".", full = TRUE)` sets `full.names`. Parameters after the rest parameter match only
   exactly, as in R.
3. The positional arguments then fill what is left: first the fixed positional parameters, then the
   unclaimed named parameters declared before the rest parameter, in declaration order. When the
   function is not variadic, they fill all the unclaimed named parameters.
4. The rest parameter absorbs whatever remains, positional or named.

So in `vapply(xs, character(1), FUN = f)`, the named `FUN` is claimed first, and `character(1)`
reaches `FUN.VALUE`, exactly as R matches it. In `lapply(1:2, fmt, x = data)` the lowercase `x`
does not match `lapply`'s `X`, so it goes to the rest parameter and is forwarded to `fmt`.

A rest parameter follows R's rules for the formals around the dots:

- It adds no required arguments, so `paste()` is legal.
- Positional arguments fill the parameters declared before it first, so `wrap("a", "b")` on
  `fn(x: character, ...: character)` gives `x = "a"` and sends `"b"` to the rest.
- It then absorbs any number of remaining positional arguments, each checked against its element
  type.
- A parameter declared after it can only be matched by name, so `sum(1, 2, na.rm = TRUE)` on
  `fn(...: integer[] | logical[], [na.rm]: logical)` sends `1` and `2` to the rest and `na.rm` by
  name.
- It also absorbs a named argument that matches no declared parameter, as in
  `read.csv(file, colClasses = "character")`.
- A named argument that duplicates a parameter already given is still an error, because R rejects a
  formal matched twice.

When an argument is the enclosing function's bare `...`, as in a wrapper
`function(x, ...) helper(x, ...)`, it forwards an unknown number of arguments, possibly none. Such a
call skips both arity checks, and the `...` argument itself matches no parameter. The call's other
arguments are still checked as usual.

rlang's splice `!!!x` passes each element of `x` as an argument, so it forwards an unknown number
of arguments the same way. Each element is checked against the rest parameter's element type: for a
`list[T]` or a vector that is `T`, and for a tuple or record every item. So `join(!!!parts)` on
`fn(...: character)` with `parts: list[double]` reports `` expected `character`, found `double` ``
at the splice. The argument types as that element, never `Unknown`.

#### Argument compatibility

Arguments are checked for compatibility, not exact equality:

- The ordinary coercions apply at parameter positions, including a scalar-like `T` into an
  array-like `T[]`, and `T` or `NULL` into `T | NULL`.
- The promotion order from [vector coercions](#vector-coercions) applies, so `mean(1L)`,
  `sd(c(1L, 2L))`, and `sum(x > threshold)` are not errors.
- A type parameter that several parameters share, directly as `T` or as the items of `list[T]` or
  `List<T>`, takes the join of what the arguments pass, whatever their order. Tags join to their
  union, so with `light: :Red | :Green`, `Bool.is_eq(:Red, light)` checks just as
  `Bool.is_eq(light, :Red)` does, and `pick(:Red, :Green)` on `<T> fn(a: T, b: T) -> T` is
  `:Red | :Green`. Numbers join in R's promotion order, so `Num.add(1L, 2.5)` and
  `List.concat(list(1L), list(2.5))` are `double` and `list[double]`. Any other mix is a type
  error, as in `Bool.is_eq(1L, "one")`, and every argument, a callback's parameters included, is
  then checked against the joined type, so `fn(f: fn(T) -> logical, a: T, b: T)` rejects a
  callback that accepts only `:Red` when `a` and `b` are `:Red` and `:Green`.
- A whole-number `double` literal counts as `integer` at a parameter, as it does at the endpoints of
  `:`, so for `fn(n: integer)`, `f(10)` is as valid as `f(10L)`. A fractional literal such as `2.5`
  is still rejected, and so is a `double` variable that happens to hold a whole number. `-3` is unary
  minus applied to a literal, not a literal, so `f(-3)` is rejected too.
- An argument whose type is `Unknown` is accepted at any parameter, because whatever made the value
  `Unknown` was already diagnosed where it happened.
- A `character` argument is accepted at a parameter of function type, because R's functional
  helpers resolve a function's name with `match.fun`, which makes `lapply(calls, "[[", "call")` and
  `apply(m, 1, "mean")` idiomatic. At the base helpers that call `match.fun`, such as `lapply`,
  `sapply`, `vapply`, `Map`, `Filter`, `Reduce`, `do.call`, and `apply`, a string literal that names a
  known function is checked as that function, so `lapply(list(1, 2), "sqrt")` is `list[double]`.
  Any other string is accepted unchecked. A function that takes a ry value, such as `List.map`,
  calls its callback directly, so a string there is an error: `List.map([1, 2], "toupper")` is
  reported, with the hint to pass `toupper` itself. `match.fun` also looks a bare name up as a function, past
  a binding that holds something else, so after a mocking placeholder such as `nchar <- NULL`,
  `vapply(words, nchar, integer(1))` calls the library's `nchar`, and beside a local list named
  `prod`, `apply(m, 1, prod)` calls base's `prod`. A local that holds a string is the function's
  name, and is checked as that name. A name the library does not
  export is still checked as the value it holds.
- At a parameter of type `T | NULL` with `T` still open, which is what a callee that tests
  `is.null(x)` infers, `T` binds to the argument's non-`NULL` part. Passing a value there says that
  the callee tolerates `NULL`, not that the caller's value may be `NULL`, so with
  `check <- function(x, allow_null = FALSE) if (allow_null && is.null(x)) ...`, a caller that runs
  `check(thing)` and then reads `thing$field` keeps `thing` open.
- A scalar parameter type that ry inferred from an unannotated body records how the body uses the
  value, not a length, because a vector type carries none. Such a parameter accepts a vector of the
  same atomic type, so `rlength <- function(x) if (all(!x)) length(x) else min(which(x)) - 1L`
  accepts `flags > 0`. An inferred `logical` parameter also accepts an `integer` or a `double`,
  scalar or vector, because that claim comes from a condition or `!`, which coerce numbers, so
  `skip <- function(line.id) if (!line.id) 0L else 1L` accepts `skip(0L)`. An inferred `integer`
  slot also accepts a `double`, because R's integer-taking functions truncate one: a callback
  shape that a first call `col.fun(n = length(levels))` pinned to `integer` accepts
  `col.fun(n = length(levels) + 1)`. An annotated signature
  keeps its scalars: under `#: fn(flag: logical) -> double`, `pick(c(1, 0, 2) > 0)` is a type
  error. A type parameter bound by an earlier argument still widens as usual, and an inferred
  `character` parameter still rejects a number. The same holds for an element that the apply family
  passes to an inferred callback, so `lapply(0:3, not)` over `not <- function(value) if (value)
  FALSE else TRUE` is `list[logical]`, and `lapply(c("a", "b"), not)` is still reported.
- An inferred `logical` parameter that the body reads only as the whole condition of an `if` or a
  `while` accepts a `character` argument the way that condition does: a string literal only when it
  spells `TRUE` or `FALSE`, and any non-literal string. So `not(Sys.getenv("CHECK", unset =
  "TRUE"))` and `not("TRUE")` are accepted, and `not("yes")` is reported. A parameter that `!`,
  `&&` or `||` reads gets no such leniency, because R rejects any string there, so `neg("TRUE")`
  over `neg <- function(x) !x` is reported.
- At a base helper that calls `match.fun`, a bare unannotated formal that the body also reads as a
  value (as an operand, a condition, or an assigned value) is not inferred as a function there,
  because `match.fun` takes a function or its name and looks a bare name up as a function. So
  `spread <- function(x, var = 1) { co <- apply(x, 2, var); if (var < 10^-3) stop(); co }` calls
  base `var` and compares the number, and `lapply(chunks, op, n)` beside `op == "tail"` accepts a
  string `op`. A formal used only as a function, as in `each <- function(f) lapply(list(1, 2), f)`,
  still infers as `fn(double) -> T`.
- An argument bound to a formal that the callee reads only through `substitute()`, or rlang's
  `enquo()`, `enquo0()`, `enexpr()` or `ensym()`, is code the callee captures, not a value, so it is
  not typed in the caller's frame. An exclusion by name, `-"value"`, passed to a library function no
  stub types is captured code too, because R rejects it as a value, and so is code that reads a
  variable holding its own name (`.x <- ".x"` before `scales::math_format(2^.x)`), a placeholder
  such code splices into an expression. In the data-mask arguments of a verb a stub marks `@masked`,
  tidyselect reads `-"value"` as a column exclusion, so `dplyr::select(data, -"value")` and
  `tidyr::pivot_longer(data, cols = -"row")` report nothing. Base `with(data, -"value")` evaluates
  the negation and is reported.
  withr's `wrap <- function(f, pre) { pre <- substitute(pre); ... }` splices `pre` into a function
  that takes `f`'s formals, so `wrap(sink_to, { if (append) "a" else "w" })` reads `sink_to`'s
  `append`, not base's `append`. A callee that also evaluates the formal checks the argument as usual,
  and so does one that passes the formal on to another function, which may evaluate it.

#### The native pipe

R's parser rewrites `x |> f(y)` into `f(x, y)` before anything runs, so the pipe types exactly like
that call. The piped value becomes the first positional argument, and every call rule applies to it:
arity, argument compatibility, and overload selection. A chain composes from left to right, and a
type error in the piped value is reported on the left-hand expression.

The `_` placeholder follows R's rule: it is legal only as the entire value of exactly one named
argument, which then receives the piped value instead of the first positional slot. So
`x |> lm(y ~ z, data = _)` is `lm(y ~ z, data = x)`, and `2 |> f(tag = _)` supplies only `tag`.

A pipe that R itself would reject is not guessed at. That covers a right-hand side that is not a
call, a positional or repeated `_`, and a `_` nested inside a subexpression. Such a pipe types as a
silent `Unknown`, and its reads are not reported.

### Overload sets

A standard-library stub may declare several signatures for one name, forming an ordered overload
set ([authoring stubs](/development/authoring-stubs#overloads-and-generics) describes how). A call
to such a name is resolved separately at each call site:

- Candidates are tried in declaration order, and the call commits to the first one whose parameters
  accept the arguments. That candidate's return type is the call's type, so `sum(1L, 2L)` is
  `integer` and `sum(1.5, 2.5)` is `double`. Nothing a failed candidate bound leaks into the next
  candidate or into the result.
- A candidate that leaves the caller's undetermined types as they were beats one that constrains
  them, whatever the declaration order, so a wrapper's parameter usually stays open on the set's
  `Any` fallback. Strict mode reports a call that resolves to such a fallback. When only one
  candidate fits, it is selected and whatever it determines stands.
- A wrapper over a numeric set is the exception. When the `Any` fallback is the only candidate that
  leaves a parameter open, and every other candidate narrows it to a number (`logical`, `integer`,
  `double` or `complex`, scalar or vector), the call needs a number whichever candidate a caller
  picks, so the parameter takes the `arithmetic` bound. `function(x) abs(x)` infers
  `<T: arithmetic> fn(x: T) -> T`, and calling it with a string or `NULL` is an error, as `abs` is
  in R. A class with an arithmetic method (`Ops.<class>`, `Arith.<class>` or an operator method)
  satisfies the bound, as it does for an operator, and so does a data frame. A set that also
  accepts other values imposes nothing: `max` takes `character`, `cumsum` takes `NULL` and `sweep`
  takes any `matrix`, so `function(x) max(x)` stays `<T> fn(x: T) -> Any`.
- When several candidates fit only by narrowing the caller's undetermined types, the call keeps that
  narrowing only if they all narrow the same way. Otherwise the type becomes `Unknown`, and so does
  the call: `function(xs) lapply(xs, f)` infers `fn(xs: Unknown) -> Unknown`, because `lapply`'s
  candidates for a named list and for a vector return different shapes. One disagreement is
  settled instead: when one candidate narrows to a scalar such as `character` and the others to
  that atom's vector, which accepts the scalar, the scalar wins. `grepl` also takes a vector
  `pattern`, yet `function(p, x) grepl(p, x)` infers `p: character`.
- The exception is a set that preserves its argument. `Filter`, `head` and `unique` declare one
  candidate per shape, and each returns exactly the shape it accepted. When every fitting candidate
  other than an all-`Any` catch-all returns what it narrowed the same undetermined type to, the call
  returns that type unchanged and leaves it undetermined. So `function(xs) Filter(f, xs)` is
  `<T> fn(xs: T) -> T`, and passing it a `data.frame` gives back a `data.frame`. The same holds for a
  declared `<T>`, so that signature can be written back as an annotation. A record passed through
  such a function keeps its record type although `Filter` or `head` may drop fields; that misses an
  error rather than reporting a false one.
- A function literal among the arguments does not belong to the caller, so narrowing its
  parameters is no guess: that is how `lapply(xs, function(v) v + 1L)` gives `v` a type. When the
  parameter is a function type with a `...: Any` rest, such as plumber's `handler: fn(...: Any) ->
  Any`, the literal's formals past the declared ones are `Any`, so `function(req) req$postBody` reads
  `req` without a strict finding.
- An argument whose type is not known statically fits every candidate, so the call does not guess
  among candidates whose results differ, and it is `Unknown`. So
  `function(w) sort(unique(unlist(w)))` is `<T> fn(w: T) -> Unknown` rather than taking `sort`'s
  `factor` candidate. A catch-all candidate, one whose results are all `Any`, is left out of that
  comparison when a more specific candidate fits and every unknown argument lands on a parameter
  that accepts any value there, because then the known arguments chose the candidate. So with `n`
  unknown, `rep(0, n)` is `double[]`, since `n` is `rep`'s `times: Any`, while `rep(x, 2)` with `x`
  unknown stays `Unknown`, since `x` is what `rep`'s vector candidate and its fallback differ on.
  The same holds beside arguments whose types the call narrows: `vapply(seq_along(x), g, template)`
  with `template` unknown is `Unknown`, whatever `g`'s parameter narrows to. An `if` whose branch
  ends in such an undecided call is `Unknown` as a whole, rather than the join of the other branch
  with nothing.
- The [whole-number literal rule](#argument-compatibility) does not influence which candidate is
  selected. Candidates are first tried against the arguments' true types, so `sum(1, 2)` selects the
  `double` candidate, matching what R computes. Only if no candidate accepts them is the set tried
  again with literals allowed as integers.
- When no candidate accepts the arguments, the call is a type error. Normally the error names the
  overloaded callee and how many signatures were tried, and gives the first candidate's failure as a
  hint. One candidate's own finding is reported instead, at its own argument range, when every
  candidate rejects the call for the same reason, or when the candidates that got furthest into the
  argument list all reject the same argument for the same reason, including all wanting a function
  where the argument is not one.
- Passing an overloaded name as a value, or hovering over it, sees the *last* declaration, which by
  convention is the most general. Go-to-definition jumps to the *first* declaration.
- The exception is an overloaded name passed by name to `lapply`, `sapply`, `vapply` or `Reduce`.
  There the set is resolved once per element type, as R calls it once per element, so
  `lapply(list(units = c(1L, 2L)), sum)` is `list[named: integer]` and `sapply(list(a = 1L), sum)`
  is `integer[named]`. Each field of a record or frame is its own element type, and an element no
  candidate accepts is an error. A class whose first argument declares a `sum.<class>` or
  `Summary.<class>` method is dispatched to that method instead of the set, as R dispatches it.
- `Reduce` with a function named directly, or chosen by an `if` between two names, folds that
  function over the elements instead of matching it against `fn(U, T) -> U`. R hands each return
  back as the next accumulator, so the accumulator's type is the join of the start (`init`, or the
  element type) and every return. With `same <- function(x, y) if (identical(x, y)) x`,
  `Reduce(same, list(1, 2))` is `double | NULL`. A step the function rejects is an error at that
  argument, so `Reduce(add, list(1, 2), "start")` with a numeric `add` is reported at `"start"`. A
  function that closes over the enclosing function's own type variables, `right =`, `accumulate =`
  and `simplify =` keep the stub's signature.
- A function whose body ends in `Reduce(f, x, init = init)` over its own formals folds the same way
  at its call sites. This covers a purrr-style wrapper whose `f` is a local
  `function(a, b) .f(a, b, ...)`. With `reduce <- function(.x, .f, ..., .init)` written that way,
  `reduce(sizes, .init = list(0), function(output, input) c(output, list(input)))` folds the callback
  from `list(0)` instead of pinning the accumulator to the start's type, and
  `reduce(list(1L, 2L), function(total, item) total + item, .init = "zero")` is reported at
  `"zero"`. A wrapper with an early `return`, or a call that also passes arguments on through the
  wrapper's `...`, keeps its ordinary signature.

Only a declaration file can overload a name, because a name with several signatures has no single
most general type and must be resolved by search rather than inferred. A `#:` annotation on your own
function declares exactly one signature; to make one name accept several shapes, give the parameter
a [union type](#union-types), or split the shapes into separate functions.

A local or package binding that shadows a stub name switches its overload set off, and the binding is
used everywhere, calls included. A project override stub may declare overload sets, because a
`.Rtypes` file is a declaration file wherever it lives.

### Vectorised math

Elementwise base functions, such as `sqrt`, `exp`, `log`, `round`, `nchar`, `toupper` and `is.na`,
declare one signature whose result describes a single element, and the call's result takes the
shape of the argument:

```r
xs <- c(1.5, 2, 3)
roots <- sqrt(xs)                      # double[]
one <- sqrt(4)                         # double
kept <- sqrt(c(a = 1, b = 4))          # double[named]
grown <- exp(-matrix(1:4, 2))          # matrix
widths <- nchar(c("a", "bb"))          # integer[]
#: fn(x: double[]) -> double[]
scaled <- function(x) sqrt(x) / 2      # checks
```

A `matrix` records no element type, so it lifts only a `double` result: `is.finite(m)` stays
`logical`, which a condition accepts. A union argument lifts member by member, so
`log(if (flag) 2 else xs)` is `double | double[]`. `sapply(columns, toupper)` over elements whose
shape is unknown is no vector of strings, since each element may be a whole column that `sapply`
stacks into a matrix, so the call keeps `sapply`'s general result. An
argument whose shape is not yet known keeps the scalar result, so an unannotated
`wrap <- function(x) sqrt(x)` infers `<T> fn(x: T) -> double`; annotate the wrapper to give it a
vector result. `array` has no type of its own, and `array()` is `Any`.

## Control flow

### `if` expressions

#### `if` without `else`

An `if` without `else` takes a [scalar condition](#conditions). If the branch has type `T`, the whole
expression is `T | NULL`, because the missing branch contributes `NULL`. Union normalization applies,
so a `NULL` body stays `NULL`, a nullable body stays a single `T | NULL`, and an `Unknown` body stays
`Unknown`.

#### `if ... else`

An `if ... else` takes a [scalar condition](#conditions) and joins the types of its two branches. The
join does not merge or coerce branch types beyond unification; it records the alternatives:

- Branches that unify share that type. `if (flag) 1L else 2L` is `integer`.
- A `NULL` branch joins by union without constraining the other branch, so `if (flag) NULL else 2L`
  is `integer | NULL`.
- Branches with genuinely different types give their union: `if (flag) 1L else "foo"` is
  `integer | character`, not a type error.
- Two ry values of the same shape join part by part, because ry's values are immutable and so
  covariant: `if (c) [:A] else [:B]` is `List<:A | :B>`, `if (c) (t = :A, n = 1i64) else (t = :B,
  n = 2i64)` is `Record{n: I64, t: :A | :B}`, and `if (c) :Some([:A]) else :Some([:B])` is
  `:Some(List<:A | :B>)`. A loop's accumulator joins the same way, so `acc <- [:A]` followed by
  `for (x in xs) acc <- List.append(acc, :B)` leaves `acc` a `List<:A | :B>`. Only tags widen: a
  part that differs in anything else, such as `[1i64]` beside `[1.5f64]`, leaves the union of
  both values (`List<I64> | List<F64>`).
- A branch whose type is still undetermined is never pinned by the other branch.
  `function(flag, x) if (flag) x else "s"` is `<T> fn(flag: logical, [x]: T) -> T | character`, not
  `fn(flag: logical, x: character)`, because unifying would make a caller wrong because of a line
  that is not wrong. A variable that only carries a bound such as `numeric` is not pinned by a
  branch that is not a plain vector either, so in
  `if (is.null(L)) rho + rho0 else L %*% rho + rho0` the result is `T | matrix`, not a `rho` that
  must be a matrix. The same holds for a variable nested inside a branch, so
  `if (identical(dp, FALSE)) list(character(), character()) else list(dp, dp)` is
  `list{character[], character[]} | list{T, T}`.
- A branch whose variable the body has already constrained may unify with the other branch, because
  that adds nothing the program did not already require. That is how the recursive `fact` above
  converges to `fn(n: integer) -> integer`.
- Two branches that are open in variables of their own, such as two parameters, give their union,
  because a caller chooses the two values separately.
  `function(value, fallback) if (is.null(value)) fallback else value` is
  `<T, U> fn(value: T | NULL, [fallback]: U) -> U | T`, so `x %||% list()` and `default %||% FALSE`
  are both fine. The parameters of a function passed as a call argument are the exception: the
  callee supplies them, so its signature decides how they relate, and two open branches over them
  are tied. In `Reduce(function(best, x) if (x > best) x else best, xs, 0)` the callback returns
  the accumulator's type. A callback that returns the union of two such open types still fits a
  callee that wants one of them, and the other ties to it, so with `bind_pair` returning either of
  its arguments, `Reduce(function(x, y) bind_pair(x, y), parts)` is accepted.
- An `Unknown` branch makes the whole conditional `Unknown`, so `if (flag) 1L else df$count` is
  `Unknown`. The exception is a branch whose `Unknown` comes from a recursive call the checker is
  still working out: it joins as the other branch, which is how `fact` converges.
- An `if` whose value is discarded, such as a statement in the middle of a block, does not join its
  branches at all, so a branch that only has a side effect never ties its types to the other
  branch's.

#### Diverging branches

A branch *diverges* when it never falls through to the code after the `if`: `return(...)`,
`stop(...)`, `break`, or `next`, a block ending in one of those, or an `if ... else` whose branches
both diverge. The `else` of an `exists("name")` test counts as diverging when `name` is a function
that a stub of an attached package types and the project does not define, because that fallback
exists only for older versions: `if (exists("emptyenv")) emptyenv() else NULL` is `environment`.
A probe for a name no stub types keeps both branches. A diverging branch contributes neither its value nor the state of its variables, so
`x <- if (c) return(NULL) else 5` gives `x` the type `double`, and a variable written inside a
diverging branch does not join into the state after the `if`.

A call that never returns has no bottom type of its own. A `stop(...)` call types as `Any`, which
fits every context and which strict mode does not report, so `function() stop("no")` is
`fn() -> Any`. `stop(...)` is recognized by its bare name, as `local` and `return` are, so
rebinding `stop` is not modelled.

### Guard narrowing

A condition that applies a type-guard predicate to a plain local variable refines that variable's
type along each edge of the `if`. Inside each branch, the variable keeps the refined type until a
write replaces it, and the refinements merge back at the join exactly as branch writes do. A
branch the guard does not refine holds the value from before the test, so `if (!is.null(x)) ...` on
an untyped `x` leaves `x` untyped after the `if`, not `NULL`. With `x`
a local variable or parameter, or an assignment to a local written inside the test
(`is.integer(limit <- check_limit(limit))`, which narrows `limit`), the recognized guards are:

- `is.null(x)` makes `x` a `NULL` in the true branch, and removes `NULL` from its union in the false
  branch.
- `is.character(x)`, `is.logical(x)`, `is.integer(x)`, `is.double(x)`, `is.function(x)`, and
  `is.list(x)` keep only the members of that family in the true branch, and remove them in the false
  branch. `is.numeric(x)` does the same for the `integer` and `double` family, and `isTRUE(x)` and
  `isFALSE(x)` narrow like `is.logical(x)`. That is deliberately loose in the false branch, which
  also drops the logical member: code like car's `if (isFALSE(fit)) 0 else fit$m` returns `FALSE`
  only to signal failure, so the other branch reads the non-logical members.
- `is.null(x$field)` removes from `x`'s union, in the false branch, each member that cannot carry
  the field: `NULL`, the empty `list()` placeholder, and a record with no field the name partially
  matches. The true branch keeps every member, because the field itself may hold `NULL`. So
  `url <- list()`, a loop that fills `url`, and then `if (is.null(url$scheme)) stop(...)` leave
  `url` as the filled record. The false branch also drops `NULL` from the field itself, in each
  record that names it exactly, so after `if (is.null(cfg$limit)) return("")` a later
  `cfg$limit` reads `character` where the field is declared `character | NULL`, until the code
  writes `cfg` or that field, directly or from a closure with `<<-`. A nominal type's field
  narrows the same way while the value keeps its type: after `if (is.null(node$left)) stop()` on
  a `Tree`, `node$left` reads `Tree` and `node` is still a `Tree`.
- `length(x)` as a condition, or `length(x)` compared with a numeric literal, removes `NULL` from `x`'s union
  in whichever branch a length of zero cannot reach. So after
  `if (length(range) != 2) return(0L)`, `range` is no longer `NULL`, and `if (length(range))` or
  `if (0 < length(range))` drops it in the true branch. `length(range) < 3` admits zero and refines
  nothing. `length(x) == 1` also reads each vector member of the union as its scalar on the true
  edge (the false edge of `!= 1`), so a `character | character[]` value passes to a `character`
  parameter inside `if (length(new_row) == 1)`.
- `!cond` swaps the two branches.

Combined with a [diverging branch](#diverging-branches), the surviving edge's refinement persists
after the `if`, which is the idiomatic early-exit guard:

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
f <- function(x: integer | NULL) -> integer {
  if (is.null(x)) {
    return(0L)
  }
  x + 1L   # x : integer here
}
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: fn(x: integer | NULL) -> integer
f <- function(x) {
  if (is.null(x)) {
    return(0L)
  }
  x + 1L   # x : integer here
}
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

The details:

- A family test covers both the scalar and the vector of that atomic type, so `is.character` is true
  for `character` and `character[]`. `is.list` covers every list shape, and `is.function` covers
  function types.
- Narrowing filters union members. A member whose family cannot be decided statically, such as an
  unannotated value or an abstract nominal, is kept on both edges.
- `inherits(x, "simpleError")` with a class R never gives an unclassed value drops `x`'s atomic,
  `NULL` and function members on the true edge, since only a value with a class attribute passes.
  So after `res <- tryCatch(download.file(url, path), error = function(e) e)`,
  `if (inherits(res, "simpleError")) stop(res$message)` reads the condition's field. A class such
  as `"integer"`, `"numeric"` or `"list"` narrows nothing, because R reports it for unclassed
  values too. The false edge keeps every member, since an object may lack the class, except for
  `"error"` and `"condition"`, which drop a `condition` member there: in
  `tryCatch(f(), error = function(e) e)` the handler's value is a condition, so
  `if (!inherits(res, "error")) res + 1` reads the expression's value. Every condition has the
  one type `condition`, so a `warning = function(w) w` handler's value is dropped by an `"error"`
  test too, and `res + 1` after it is not reported although R fails there.
- An unannotated parameter whose runtime type the body inspects is `Unknown`, because code that
  inspects a type uses the value as a different type on each branch, so no one type describes it.
  The inspections are `typeof()`, `class()`, `mode()`, `storage.mode()`, `inherits()`,
  `is.object()`, `oldClass()`, the `is.*` family tests, such as `is.character()`, and rlang's
  type predicates, such as `is_string()`, `is_function()`, `is_formula()`, `is_bare_list()` and
  `is_scalar_integer()`. These count by name, whatever the name refers to, since packages vendor
  rlang's standalone files and define the predicates themselves. So
  `function(x) if (is.character(x)) x else "other"` is `fn(x: Unknown) -> Unknown`. `is.null()`,
  `is.na()`, and `is.finite()` test a value rather than its type, so they keep the parameter typed,
  and an annotated parameter still narrows.
- `is.null(x)` on an `Any` or `Unknown` variable refines the true edge to `NULL`, because the runtime
  guarantees it. A family guard does not refine `Any` or `Unknown`, because inventing a concrete shape
  there would cause reports on standard-library functions that declare a scalar result for a value of
  any length.
- `is.null(x)` on a value with no constraints yet, such as an unused unannotated parameter, *shapes*
  it: the test asserts that `NULL` is possible, so the variable becomes `T | NULL` for a fresh `T`, and
  the edges narrow as for an ordinary union. That is how the `value`/`fallback` function under
  [`if ... else`](#if--else) types without annotation. Testing a parameter for `NULL` and then
  passing it unguarded to a parameter that excludes `NULL` is therefore a genuine finding, although
  arithmetic and comparison on it are accepted, as [union operands](#operators-over-union-operands)
  describe. The shaping never fires on a variable that already
  carries a constraint, or on a declared rigid type parameter.
- When a guard cannot fire, such as `is.null(x)` on a union with no `NULL` member, nothing is refined.
  Dead branches get no special typing.
- Only a read of a variable, or one field of it, narrows: a parameter, a function local, or a
  top-level variable assigned by an earlier statement. `is.null(f(x))`, `is.null(x$a$b)` and
  `is.null(x[["a"]])` do not narrow. When such a value reaches a place that rejects only its `NULL`
  member, the mismatch says to assign it to a variable and test that:

  ```text
  expected `character`, found `character | NULL`. A test such as `!is.null()` narrows only a
  variable or one of its fields, so to rule out `NULL` here, assign this value to a variable and
  test that
  ```
- A refinement does not outlive the top-level statement it was made in. Inside a function body, or
  one braced top-level block, `if (is.null(x)) stop(...)` narrows `x` for the rest of the body,
  because the guard and the later reads are one statement. Written as bare top-level statements, the
  guard and the read after it are two statements, and the read is not narrowed.
- A read from inside a closure narrows when the guard is in the same statement, just as a local
  does. The guard's own subject must not itself be a deferred read: that body runs later, so the test
  proves nothing about the value it will see then.
- A closure may run after the rest of its frame, so it reads an enclosing variable as it stands
  where the closure is defined, joined with every later write the closure can still see when it
  runs, in the frame or in another closure. After
  `if (is.null(x)) return(""); get <- function() x; x <- NULL`, the call `get()` is
  `character | NULL`, and the same holds for a narrowed field (`get <- function() cfg$limit`
  followed by `cfg$limit <- NULL`). When the closure runs decides which writes it sees:

  | The closure | Sees writes up to |
  | --- | --- |
  | called inline, `(function() x)()` | the call |
  | passed to a function that calls it before returning (`lapply`, `vapply` and the rest of the apply family, `do.call`, `tryCatch`) | the call |
  | bound with `get <- function() x` and only called | its last call |
  | called inside a loop | the end of that loop |
  | read in any other way: returned, stored, passed to another function | the end of the frame |

  So `out <- get(); x <- NULL; out` keeps `out` a `character`, and a write on the other branch of an
  `if` is never seen. A later R literal with the same default type as the read changes nothing, a
  literal with another default joins (`x <- 1L; get <- function() x; x <- 2.5` makes `get()`
  `integer | double`), and the closure's own `<<-` writes are joined as described under
  [the assignment operator](#assignment-operator--).
- Guards compose across `&&` and `||`. The right operand of `&&` runs only on the left's true edge,
  so it is checked under that refinement, and the whole condition refines its true edge by both
  tests: `!is.null(x) && length(x) > 0L` reads a non-`NULL` `x`. `||` does the same on the false
  edge. The other edge of a compound condition is not refined. A family test on an untyped local
  makes the value unchecked on the true edge rather than constraining it, so after `y <- a`,
  `is.numeric(y) && y + 1 > 0` leaves `a` open, where `y + 1 > 0` alone would require it to be
  numeric.
- `is.na(x)` is not a type guard, because being `NA` is a property of a value, not of a type.
- A test that two values differ in being `NULL` ties them together for the rest of the body. After
  `if (is.null(A) != is.null(b)) stop(...)`, a later `!is.null(A)` also rules `NULL` out of `b`.
  The same holds for `xor(is.null(A), is.null(b))` and for the spelled-out
  `is.null(A) && !is.null(b) || !is.null(A) && is.null(b)`. A write to either variable, even on one
  branch, ends the tie.
- A one-argument call to a function that is not a type test, such as `is_loaded(package)`, is
  remembered on its true edge. A callee whose only `NULL` return is a statement
  `if (!pred(x)) return(NULL)` in its body, on a formal it never rewrites and with no other return
  that may be `NULL`, returns without `NULL` when the caller's argument is a variable on which
  `pred` was already found true.
  With `ns_env <- function(package) { if (!is_loaded(package)) return(NULL); asNamespace(package) }`,
  `if (!is_loaded(package)) stop("not loaded")` followed by `parent.env(ns_env(package))` checks,
  while the same call without the test is reported. A write to the variable after the test, even
  on one branch or in a loop, forgets it.
- A callee whose body ends in a chain of such tests of one formal, with `NULL` only as the last
  `else`, answers `NULL` for an object of no class it handles. With
  `chain_count <- function(x) if (is.chain(x)) 1 else if (is.chains(x)) length(x) else NULL`, a call
  whose argument may carry a class (any type but an atomic vector, `NULL` or a function) returns
  without `NULL`, so `1:chain_count(x)` checks for an unannotated `x`, while
  `1:chain_count("a")` is reported.
- `identical(f, g)` with `f` a local and `g` a function narrows `f` to `g`'s type on the true edge,
  and a generic `g` is instantiated afresh at each call, so after `format_name <- identity` as a default, `if (identical(format_name, identity))
  format_name(name + 1)` calls `identity` with a number although an earlier call passed a string.
- `identical(x$field, "literal")`, with a string, logical or numeric literal, rules `NULL` out of `x` on the true
  edge, because a `NULL` `x` has no field equal to the literal. So with `info` a record or `NULL`,
  `if (identical(info$type, "venv")) use(info$path)` passes `info$path` as the record's field.
- A local written under a test of another local remembers both values. After
  `if (case == "a") x <- "s"` with `x` a number before it, a later test of `case` that implies
  `case == "a"` sees `x` as the string, and one that rules it out sees the number:
  `if (case == "b") x + 1` checks. The tests compare the local with literals through `==`, `!=` and
  `%in%`, a bare local (read as `== TRUE`), and `!` of these. R coerces before it compares (`"1" == 1` holds),
  so only literals of one kind are told apart by their values, and a string with a backslash is told
  apart from none. A write to either local ends it, and so does a write in a loop or through `<<-`.
  A test reads within the tests of the same local that enclose it, so inside
  `if (case %in% c("a", "b", "c"))`, the writes under `if (case %in% c("a", "b"))` and
  `if (case == "c")` together leave no path that skips both: a local each makes a number is a
  number after them.

#### Lazy arguments and required formals

R evaluates an argument only when the body forces it, and omitting an argument is an error only at
that moment. A formal without a default is therefore required at call sites only when every path
through the body forces it, so `function(x, unused) x` may be called as `f(1)`, and
`function(x, y) if (is.null(x)) y else x` may be called as `f(1)`, because the branch that reads `y`
is not taken. This relaxes only an inferred
signature. An annotation is honored as written, so under `#: fn(a: integer, b: character) -> integer`,
`function(a, b) a` still requires `b`, and to let callers omit it the annotation says `[b]`. A read forces the formal, with these
exceptions and additions:

- A read on only some paths does not force the formal. The branches of `if`, `switch()`, and `match`
  force only what all of them force, and an `if` without `else` or a `switch()` without an unnamed
  default forces nothing. The right operand of `&&`, `||`, or `%||%` and the body of a loop may not
  run, so they force nothing either, except that the branch an `if (a && b)` takes has run `b`.
- A path that leaves through `return()` forces only what it read before returning, so in
  `function(flag, value) { if (flag) return(NULL); value }`, `value` is optional. `stop()` is not a
  return: a path that stops never completes the call, so it does not relax what the other paths
  force.
- Passing the formal on under its own name to a project function, as in `inner(x, finally = finally)`,
  does not force it, because the callee decides whether it is needed. A call to a library function
  still forces it, except for a base R parameter that has no default and that the function tests
  with `missing()`: `Reduce(f, x, init = .init)` and `base::tryCatch(expr, finally = finally)` leave
  the formal unforced. A library function declared only as `Any`, such as graphics' `rect`, forces
  nothing either, because no declaration says what it reads.
- Passing the formal bare, under any name or position, to a project function's parameter that the
  callee may leave unread does not force it either. Such a parameter is optional without a default,
  or tested with `missing()` in the callee's body. In R the callee sees the argument as missing, and
  a declared default does not fill it in: in `table_of <- function(x, format) pick_format(format)`,
  `format` is optional when `pick_format <- function(format = NULL)` tests `missing(format)`, and
  required when `pick_format` never tests it, because reading it would stop. A caller that omits such
  an optional formal sees the type of the callee's default. When the callee writes the value on its
  `missing()` path, that is the value R reaches, so `inverse <- function(m) pseudoinverse(m)` over
  `pseudoinverse <- function(m, tol) fast_svd(m, tol)` returns `tol` as `double`. A declared default
  describes only a call that fails. As for a formal's own `missing()` test, the order of the test and
  the reads is not checked.
- Passing the formal bare to a function read from a field or a list element, as in
  `dist$quantile(p, parms)` or `handlers[[1]](p, parms)`, does not force it, because the checker
  cannot see what that function reads.
- Using the formal bare as one position of a matrix-style index, as in `x$data[i, j]` or
  `x$data[i, j] <- value`, does not force it, because `[` reads a missing argument as an empty
  index and selects the whole dimension. A one-position `x[i]` and `x[[i]]` still force it, since
  `x[i]` types as one element and `[[` has no empty index.
- Passing the formal positionally to a function that the enclosing body defines further down,
  as in `format_advice <- function(x) format_advice_cli(x)` before `format_advice_cli <- function(x)
  "advice"` in the same `local()`, does not force it either. The sibling runs only after its
  definition, and the checker does not look at what it reads, so the formal stays optional even
  when the sibling reads it, and a call that omits it is not reported.
- A body that dispatches with `UseMethod()` forces none of its formals, because it hands them to a
  method unevaluated, and the method decides what it needs. So
  `describe <- function(x, ...) UseMethod("describe")` may be called as `describe()`. Forwarding a
  formal into the formal the generic dispatches on (its first, or the one named by `UseMethod`'s
  second argument) is different: `UseMethod` evaluates a supplied argument there to read its class, so after
  `f <- function(fit) persistence(fit)`, the call `f()` is reported as leaving out `fit`.
- A read guarded by `missing(name)` does not make the formal required; see below.
- An argument of `substitute()` or `quote()` is not evaluated, so a formal named only there is not
  forced. The same holds for rlang's `enquo()`, `enquos()`, `enexpr()`, `enexprs()`, `ensym()`,
  `ensyms()`, and `maybe_missing()`, so `quo <- function(expr) enquo(expr)` may be called as
  `quo()`.
- A formal assigned before any read, as in `function(x) { x <- 1; x }`, is never forced. The order
  is evaluation order, so `n <- n / 2` forces `n`, and a `for` variable is assigned by the loop.
- A write on some paths covers those paths, so a later read forces the formal only on the paths
  that did not write it. `function(x, flag) { if (flag) x <- 1; x }` may be called as `f(flag =
  TRUE)`, and so may a formal written in a `while` or `for` body, the right operand of `&&` or `||`,
  or some arms of a `switch()`, because a call that takes the writing path never needs the
  argument. A path that forced the formal before writing it still forced it, so
  `if (flag) x <- x + 1; x` requires `x`, and a write inside `local()` binds a new frame and covers
  nothing.
- `x <<- 1` writes an enclosing frame, so it never assigns the formal `x`.
- A function that writes a name with `<<-` reads that name as the binding it keeps updating, such
  as a Reference Class field, never as the shipped function of the same name. In `t <<- t[-drop]`,
  `t` is `Unknown` rather than base `t`, so indexing it reports nothing, and strict mode reports the
  read. A project binding of that name still types the read.
- A read inside a nested function forces the formal only where the nested function certainly
  runs, and only on every path through its body: when it is called inline, as in
  `(function() x)()`, when it is bound to a local and called through that local, and when the
  outer function returns it, because a factory's closure is made to be called. A function passed
  to a call forces nothing, because `lapply()` over an empty vector never calls it, so
  `function(items, x) lapply(items, function(item) c(x, item))` may be called without `x`. A
  nested function's own parameter or assignment of the name shadows the formal.
- A read inside another formal's default forces the formal only when that default runs, which is
  when the call omits the formal that owns it and the body forces that formal. So in
  `g <- function(x, y = x) y`, `x` is required exactly when `y` is left out: `g(y = 1)` and `g(2)`
  are accepted, and `g()` is reported as leaving out `x`, as R fails with "argument "x" is
  missing". A formal with its own default, or one the body tests with `missing()`, stays optional.

#### `missing()` on a defaultless formal

A formal that the body tests with `missing(name)` (or rlang's `is_missing(name)`) is optional at call sites, which is how R writes an
optional argument without a default: `function(name, punct) if (missing(punct)) … else …punct…` may
be called without `punct`. The test also narrows whether the formal was supplied, like a type guard:

- On the true edge, reading `name` is an error, because R fails that read with "argument is missing,
  with no default". Writing it is legal and supplies it, as in `if (missing(punct)) punct <- "!"`.
- On the false edge the formal is supplied, and reads are ordinary.
- A diverging true edge, such as `if (missing(x)) stop(...)`, leaves the rest of the body on the
  supplied edge, and `!missing(name)` swaps the edges.
- After the branches rejoin, the formal counts as unsupplied only when it is unsupplied on both
  edges, so only definite failures are reported.
- A formal with a default is never narrowed, because reading it while unsupplied evaluates the
  default.
- As in R, `missing()` applies only to the immediate function's own formals.

### Blocks

A block evaluates to the type of its last expression, so an empty block is `NULL`. A trailing `;`
adds no expression in R's grammar, so `{ 1L; }` is `integer`.

### `return`

`return(x)` exits the enclosing function with `x`, and `return()` exits with `NULL`. It is a
control-flow construct recognized by its bare name, as `local` is, rather than a call.

- A function's return type is the union of every `return` value in its body with the body's trailing
  value, so `function() { if (c) return("foo"); 5 }` is `fn() -> character | double`.
- A body that always exits, because it ends in a `return`, or in a `repeat` that no `break` leaves,
  contributes no trailing value, so `function() return(TRUE)` is `fn() -> logical`, not
  `fn() -> logical | NULL`. A body that ends in `stop()` contributes none either, so
  `function(x) { if (x) return(1L); stop("no") }` is `fn(x: logical) -> integer`.
- An inferred return type reads a bare `return(NA)` as a missing value of the other returns' type
  when they all share one atomic type, so a function that returns `NA` on one path and a string on
  the others is `-> character`. Otherwise the `NA` joins as `logical`, as in
  `fn(x: logical) -> list{double} | logical`. A declared return type still checks `return(NA)` as
  `logical`, so declare `-> character` with `return(NA_character_)`.
- The `return` expression itself yields no value where it stands, so it types as `NULL` locally and is
  not a strict-mode origin, just like `break` and `next`.
- The returned expression is checked like any other expression.
- A `return` inside a loop exits the whole function, so it abandons the loop the way `break` does.
- A `return` at the top level is an R runtime error. Its value is still checked, and joins no
  function's return type.

### `switch`

`switch(subject, a = ..., b = ..., default)` selects one branch by the subject's value at run time.
That selection cannot be modelled statically, but the call is still checked in full:

- The subject and every branch are type-checked.
- The call's type is the union of the branch value types. `NULL` joins the union unless there is a
  default branch, because an unmatched `switch` returns an invisible `NULL`. A default branch is any
  branch without a name, including the first.
- A named branch with no value falls through to the next branch, as in R, and contributes no type of
  its own.
- A branch label may be `NULL`, as R's grammar allows: `switch(typeof(x), NULL = 1, 2)` labels the
  case for the string `"NULL"`.
- With a subject `typeof(x)` on a local variable, each named branch runs only for values whose type
  its labels (together with the empty labels falling through to it) name, so `x` is narrowed inside
  the branch. A union keeps the members a label can match. An unannotated parameter tested this way
  is `Unknown`, as [guard narrowing](#guard-narrowing) describes, so `logical = if (x) ...` does not
  pin the type the other branches and callers see:
  `function(x) switch(typeof(x), logical = if (x) "T" else "F", "other")` is
  `fn(x: Unknown) -> character`.
- Exactly one branch runs, so assignments inside them fork and join exactly like the arms of an
  [`if`](#if-expressions), and a branch that cannot fall through contributes no state. A name
  introduced only inside the branches is not defined on the path where nothing matches, which R
  reports as `object 'r' not found`.
- `switch` is recognized by its bare name, so a local binding named `switch` makes the call an
  ordinary one again.

### `for`

`for (name in value) body` needs an iterable source, and binds `name` to its element type:

- A vector of any shape iterates with its scalar element type.
- A list-like `list[T]` and a dict-like `list[named: T]` iterate with `T`.
- A tuple-like or record-like list iterates with the union of its item types, so
  `for (item in list(a = 1L, b = "two")) ...` binds `item` as `integer | character`. The empty list
  iterates with `NULL`, the union of zero item types.
- A union of iterables iterates member by member, and a `NULL` or empty-list member adds no item
  type, because it runs the body zero times: `for (f in if (a) list(g)) f()` binds `f` as `g`'s
  type, not `NULL`.
- `NULL` is iterable and runs zero times, which is legal R. It binds the variable as `NULL`.
- `Any` iterates with `Any` items, and `Unknown` with `Unknown` items.
- An abstract nominal iterates with `Any` items, because its element shape is not visible.
- Iterating does not constrain a value whose shape is undetermined, such as an unannotated
  parameter, because R iterates both vectors and lists. The loop variable becomes `Unknown`.
- Any other source, such as a function, is an error, reported on the source expression.

The source is evaluated once, before the first iteration, and the loop does not change its type. The
loop variable is re-initialized from the source on every iteration, so an assignment to it in the
body does not carry over into the next iteration.

After the loop, R keeps the variable's last value, or sets it to `NULL` when the source was empty.
A read there is therefore the element type joined with `NULL`, and with the variable's type at the
end of the body when the body assigns it. A `break` leaves the loop with the state it saw and a
`next` starts the next iteration with it, so a write that one cuts off from the end of the body still
counts. A nested loop over the same name writes the outer loop's variable, so the outer variable's
type after the loop includes the inner loop's elements. A `from:to` range always has at least one element, so after
`for (i in 1:n) {}` the variable `i` is `integer`, while after `for (value in x) {}` with
`x: double[]` it is `double | NULL`. A loop at the top level of a file leaves its variable bound
like a top-level assignment, so a later statement or item reads the same type. When the item also
binds the name in another way, the name exports as `Unknown`.

### foreach loops

`foreach(g = groups) %do% body` from the foreach package is a loop too. Each named argument of
`foreach()` that does not start with a dot is an iterator, and the body sees it bound to the element
type of its value, by the rules of `for` above. An iterator object such as `icount()` binds an
`Unknown` iterator. `foreach(i = xs) %:% when(i > 0) %:% foreach(j = ys) %do% body` nests the
