  class), or has its own `initialize` method, which may take any argument.
- `x@slot` reads an S4 slot and types as `Unknown`, and is a strict-mode origin, except on an `Any`
  subject, whose slots are `Any` like its `$` fields. The subject
  expression is still inferred and counts as a read for naming, the unused check, references, and
  rename. A slot write `x@slot <- v` is an ordinary
  [replacement-form assignment](#replacement-form-assignment) of its base variable.
- `R6Class(...)`, `$new(...)`, and R6 fields and methods are not modeled and are `Unknown`; `self`,
  `private`, and `super` are covered under [names bound at run time](#names-bound-at-run-time).
  In strict mode, an R6 value is an origin where it is first bound, as in
  `account <- Account$new()`.

### Declaring a checked type for your own classes

A class is a [nominal type](#nominal-types) with a representation. Wrapping the constructor is
enough to get slot types, constructor arity, and field access checked on an S4 or R6 class:

<Tabs syncKey="annotation-style">
<TabItem label="Inline">

```r
type Point = list{x: double, y: double}

setClass("Point", representation(x = "numeric", y = "numeric"))

make_point <- function(x: double, y: double) -> Point {
  @Point(new("Point", x = x, y = y))
}

norm2 <- function(p: Point) -> double sqrt(p$x^2 + p$y^2)

norm2("nope")   # type-mismatch: the argument is not a `Point`
make_point(1)   # type-mismatch: a required argument is missing
```

</TabItem>
<TabItem label="Comments">

```r
#: @type Point {list{x: double, y: double}}

setClass("Point", representation(x = "numeric", y = "numeric"))

#: fn(x: double, y: double) -> Point
make_point <- function(x, y) {
  #: @new Point
  new("Point", x = x, y = y)
}

#: fn(p: Point) -> double
norm2 <- function(p) sqrt(p$x^2 + p$y^2)

norm2("nope")   # type-mismatch: the argument is not a `Point`
make_point(1)   # type-mismatch: a required argument is missing
```

</TabItem>
</Tabs>

The `setClass` call stays opaque, and the annotation is what the checker reads. Operators on the
class work the same way: declare `Arith.Point`, and `p1 + p2` is checked. Method dispatch, on the
other hand, needs the argument's class at the call site, and inside an unannotated
`function(x) speak(x)` that class is not known.

## Strict mode

Strict mode is an opt-in check, switched on with `[check] strict` and off by default. It changes
nothing about inference and adds no typing rules. It reports the places where the checker could not
determine a type, together with the [parameters nothing constrains](#untyped-parameters), and it
escalates unresolved references to errors. It also holds each function written in ry types to
[running natively](#native-functions).

Strict mode reports an `Unknown` once, where it enters: at the construct, read or call the checker
could not type. A value computed from it, such as the result of a function that returns another
function's `Unknown` result, is not reported again. A call to a function that no stub declares is
such an entry point, whether the function comes from base R, as `R.home()` and `.Call(...)` do, or
from another package. `#: @if-unknown TYPE` on the binding that receives the result accepts it, and
a [stub](/development/authoring-stubs) line declaring the function removes the finding everywhere.

A value typed `Any` by an annotation or a single declaration is an intentional opt-out and never
produces a strict finding. A call that falls back to an overload set's `Any` candidate is the
exception, because the fallback marks a call the declarations could not describe.

Strict mode also reports each read that the
[attached-package tolerance](#package-imports-namespace-and-description) silenced, and points at the
[stub](/development/authoring-stubs) that would make it checkable. Without that, a clean run after
`library(unknownpkg)` would look the same as a run that checked nothing.

### Unresolved references escalate to errors

An unresolved reference carries the `unresolved` code, and comes in three kinds:

- a bare name the resolver cannot find in the package, its imports, or the builtins;
- an unknown package namespace in `pkg::name`;
- a name that a known namespace does not export, read as `pkg::name`.

Outside strict mode these are warnings; under strict mode they are errors, whether strict mode comes
from the configuration or from the per-file directive. Turning strict mode on can therefore raise the
severity of findings without changing their count, which matters when a `--min-severity error` gate
reads them.

Two `unresolved` findings are errors in every mode, because they stop the package from loading: a
`NAMESPACE` `importFrom` naming something the namespace does not export, and an `export()` naming
something that is not in the package's namespace. That namespace holds every top-level binding of a
package file, including each target of a chained assignment such as `memoise <- memoize <-
function(f) f`, and every imported name, so a package may re-export what it imports: a name from an
`importFrom`, such as `importFrom(magrittr, "%>%")` with `export("%>%")`, or from an `import(pkg)`
whose namespace exports it. An `import(pkg)` of a namespace without stubs may supply any name, so it
silences the check.

### Per-file directive

A plain top-level comment sets one file's typing mode, overriding the configured `[check]` switches
in both directions:

```r
# typing: strict
```

- `off` silences the file's type errors and strict diagnostics, even when the configuration checks
  types. `on` opts a single file into type checking, with strict mode off. `strict` also switches on
  strict mode for the file.
- The comment must hold nothing but the directive: `# typing: off  # reason` is reported as an
  unknown directive.
- A directive must be a comment between top-level expressions. Inside any expression, such as a
  function body or an `if` block, it is ignored.
- The older `#: @strict` form is still supported: `#: @strict` means `# typing: strict`, and
  `#: @strict off` means `# typing: on`.
- If a file has several directives, the last one wins. A `typing:` comment with any other value is
  reported as an error.
- The directive changes only which diagnostics are published for the file. Inference and every other
  check are untouched, so hover and the other editor features keep working under `off`.

### A package file's top level

Under strict mode, the top level of a library file may hold only definitions, so that loading the
package binds names and registers classes and does nothing else. A library file is a file in the
package's `R/` directory. Every other file is an entry: a script that `ry run` starts, a test file
or helper under `tests/`, and each console command. Entries may run anything at their top level.

A top-level statement of a library file is a definition when it is one of these:

- `name <- value` or `name = value` whose value is a function, a class definition form, or a
  constant (below), including a chain such as `a <- b <- function(x) x`. zeallot's
  `c(a, b) %<-% value` counts the same way.
- A call to a class definition form: `setClass`, `setClassUnion`, `setGeneric`, `setMethod`,
  `setReplaceMethod`, `setValidity`, `setOldClass`, `setAs`, `setRefClass`, or `R6Class`, bare or
  namespace-qualified. Their arguments are not inspected.
- A call that imports a package (`library`, `require`, `requireNamespace`, `loadNamespace`,
  `attachNamespace`) or only declares something (`globalVariables`, `suppressForeignCheck`,
  `delayedAssign`, `makeActiveBinding`, `.S3method`, `registerS3method`).
- A `type`, `alias` or `opaque` definition, or an expression with no effect, such as `NULL`.
- An `if` whose condition has no effect, or is an import call such as
  `requireNamespace("x", quietly = TRUE)` (negated or not), and whose branches are definitions, or a
  `{` block of definitions.

A constant's initializer must have no effect. Literals, names, `pkg::name`, function literals,
formulas, and the built-in operators have none. A call has none when its function is declared pure
and its arguments have none; the arguments of `quote`, `expression`, `alist` and `substitute` are not
evaluated, so they are not inspected. A call of a function declared pure only for pure callbacks,
such as `lapply`, also needs the function it is passed to be pure: a function literal whose body has
no effect, or the name of a pure function.

```r
squares <- vapply(1:3, function(i) i * i, numeric(1))
widths <- lapply(list("a", "bb"), nchar)
printed <- lapply(1:3, print)
```

```text ry check strict
x strict mode: `lapply` calls `print` here, which is not declared pure, so it runs every time the
  package loads. A file in `R/` may only define things at its top level: move the call into a
  function, `.onLoad`, or the script `ry run` starts
 --[R/main.R:3:24]
```

Inside `local()`, assignments and loops touch only the local environment and are allowed. A name
bound there to a function literal may be called when the call has no effect: neither the defaults it
evaluates nor the body, read with the bindings in place at the call, so rebinding a name the body
calls after the function is defined counts. A call to a name bound inside that `local()` to anything
else, including through a loop, a destructuring or a replacement such as `body(f) <- ...`, is an
effect. A replacement inside `local()` of a name bound outside it, such as
`local({ defaults$a <- 2; defaults })`, is allowed when the project binds that name once, at the top
level, to a literal or to `c()` or `list()` of literals. R then replaces in a local copy, which leaves
the outer value alone. Any other outer value might be an environment, which the replacement would
change, so it is an effect.

Anything else is an effect, which is a `strict` error reported on the expression that causes it.
Only the first effect of a statement is reported. The effects are:

- a call to a function that is not declared pure, including a `%op%` operator;
- a call whose function is computed, such as `handlers[[1]]()`;
- a `for`, `while` or `repeat` loop outside `local()`;
- `<<-`, which assigns outside the package namespace;
- a replacement such as `names(x) <- value` or `x$field <- value`, which changes a value after its
  definition;
- an assignment nested inside an expression, such as `f(x <- 1)`;
- `?`, which looks up help.

#### Purity

A function is pure when calling it has no effect beyond returning a value, whatever its arguments.
Purity is declared, and the declaration is trusted: ry does not check the body. The one exception is
a function bound inside `local()`, whose body ry reads, because no other file can call it.
A project function is declared pure with a `#: @pure` comment line above its definition or an inline
`@pure` before `function` (or `\(x)`), and a stub declares one with `name : @pure fn(...)`, or with
`name : @pure(FUN) fn(...)` when it is pure only for a pure function passed as `FUN`. `@pure` above
anything but a top-level binding declares nothing and carries an `annotation` warning. With
inline annotations turned off, an inline `@pure` declares nothing and carries the `inline-annotation`
warning. The
standard library's stubs mark base R's pure functions, such as `paste0`, `seq_len` and `sum`, and
`c`, `list`, `switch`, `expression` and `alist` are pure without a stub.

A project definition of a name shadows the stub's declaration, so a package's own `paste0` is pure
only if it is marked. A bare call to a stub-declared function takes its purity from the declaration
the name resolves to, the same one its type comes from: when two packages declare the same name, the
later one wins, and its marker alone decides. `pkg::name` asks the named package's declaration. A function defined inside a condition, such as `if (cond) f <- function() 1`,
is never pure, because which definition exists depends on the condition.

Purity belongs to each definition alone. Adding `cat()` to the body of a function marked `@pure`
re-checks only that function, and removing the marker re-checks only the statements that call it.

#### Lazy constants

`ry load` binds a library file's functions lazily, and under strict mode its constants too: a constant
whose name has exactly one top-level writer becomes a promise that evaluates its initializer on first
use. Every package name the initializer mentions must also be written exactly once, by a statement
earlier in collation order, and otherwise the constant runs eagerly in order. A `<<-` anywhere in a
package function counts as a writer of its target that comes after every top-level statement,
because the namespace locks once loading finishes and such a write can only land while `.onLoad`, or
something it calls, runs. Because the initializer
has no effect and what it reads cannot change after it, evaluating it later gives the value a load
would have computed. A reload binds the constant again whenever anything its initializer reaches changed, such
as the body of a pure function it calls.

### Untyped parameters

A parameter without an annotation is reported when nothing constrains it, because it then accepts
any value unchecked. That is the case when its inferred type is `Unknown`, or is still a fresh type
variable of its own function that neither another parameter nor the return type mentions:

```r
describe <- function(x) cat("a value\n")
```

```text ry check typing strict
x strict mode: parameter `x` has no type. How the function uses it does not determine one, so any
  value is accepted unchecked. Annotate it, as in `x: TYPE`
```

A type variable that the signature mentions twice is genuine polymorphism, so `function(x) x`
(`<T> fn(x: T) -> T`) and `function(flag, a, b) if (flag) a else b` are not reported. Neither is a
constrained variable such as `<T: numeric>`, a variable tied to an enclosing function, such as the
inner `x` in `function(f) function(x) f(x)`. A lambda passed as an argument is not judged at all,
because the call gives its parameters their types only after the lambda is inferred. An R6 method,
a function inside a `list(...)` passed to `R6Class`, is the exception: `R6Class` gives it no types,
so it is judged like any other definition. A `...` formal is
never reported, and a default does not count as a type, because an unannotated parameter takes its
type from its uses. Annotate a parameter that really accepts anything as `Any`, which strict mode
treats as a deliberate choice.

The parameter finding is about the declaration, so it can stand beside an origin in the body that it
feeds, as in `function(node) node$value`, where annotating `node` would fix both.

Returns and bindings are not reported separately. An undetermined return or binding always traces
back to one of the origins below, a type error, or a reference to a project binding whose own
definition is reported, so reporting the declaration too would repeat that finding.

### Where an undetermined type is reported

An undetermined value is reported once, where it first became undetermined, not at every later
expression that carries it. There are three such origins: a construct the type system does not
describe, a reference to a binding whose type is not known, and a recursive definition that could not
be typed. A declaration whose return type is `Unknown` produces no finding at its call sites, and an
`Unknown` nested inside a larger type is not reported either.

A reference to a binding defined in this project is not an origin; the binding's own definition is
reported instead, so one undetermined value does not produce a finding in every file that reads it.
An unresolved bare name is not an origin either, because naming already reports it. An unresolved
`pkg::name` is reported both ways. A value under any `#:` annotation (checked, `@trust`,
`@if-unknown`, or `@new`) is not an origin, because the annotation gives it a type, and neither is
anything inside that value: `#: double[]` above `x <- as.numeric(df$age)` covers `df$age`. A function
literal or a braced block inside the value keeps its own origins, because its statements can be
annotated where they are.

The one exception is a checked annotation over a value whose own type is `Unknown`. Such an
annotation checks nothing, because `Unknown` satisfies every type, so it is reported in place of the
origins it covers, and `@if-unknown` is the way to accept the value unchecked:

```r
total <- function(df: data.frame) -> double {
  #: double[]
  amount <- as.numeric(df$amount)   # covered: `as.numeric` returns `double[]`

  #: double[]
  prices <- df$price                # strict: the annotation checks nothing

  #: @if-unknown double[]
  costs <- df$cost                  # accepted unchecked
  sum(amount) + sum(prices) + sum(costs)
}
```

### Native functions

In strict mode, a function whose signature is written entirely in ry types is a *native function*.
It is compiled to machine code, and its body may hold only ry values. `ry check` reports each
construct that would send it to the interpreter instead, under one of two codes:

| Code | Severity | What it marks |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `native` | error | an R value used outside a conversion, which breaks the rule |
| `interpreted` | warning | legitimate ry that the native compiler does not compile yet |

A native function with neither finding runs natively. This is a `native` error:

```r
label_width <- function(label: Str) -> I64 {
  width <- nchar(label)
  Num.from_r(width)
}
print(label_width("native"))
```

```text ry check typing strict
native

  x `nchar` is an R function, and a native function holds only ry values, so its result cannot be
    used here. Convert the result where it returns: `Num.from_r(nchar(label))`
```

**Which types are ry types.** The ry types are `I64`, `F64`, `Bool` and `Str`, `List<T>`, `Set<T>`
and `Dict<K, V>`, records, tuples, tags and tag unions, nominal types over those, type parameters,
function types whose parameters and result are ry types, and the table types `DataFrame`,
`LazyFrame`, `GroupBy`, `LazyGroupBy` and `Expr`. `NULL` alone is neither. Every other type is an R
type: `double`, `integer`, `character`, `logical`, R vectors such as `double[]`, R lists,
`data.frame`, classes, `Any` and `Unknown`.

**Which functions are judged.** A function literal is a native function when every parameter is
annotated with a ry type, the declared result is a ry type, `NULL` or left out, and at least one of
them is a ry type. A signature written in a `#:` comment, or on the name the function is assigned
to, is judged by the same rules and compiles exactly like the inline form
([Annotations at run time](#annotations-at-run-time)), and so is a signature that is only the
name of a type alias, which the preflight writes out as the function type it names.

A type parameter bounded by an R type, as in `<T: numeric>`, counts as an R type.

A function with an R type anywhere in its signature is a *boundary function*. It is where R values
and ry values meet, it runs in the interpreter when it has to, and it is not judged. So an R type in
the signature is how a function opts out: `function(path: character) -> F64` may read a file with
any R code and hand a ry value back. A function with an unannotated parameter is not judged either.
A function literal without annotations is judged as part of the native function whose body holds
it, and not at all anywhere else.

**What a native function may do with R.** An *R function* is any function that is not a ry
[standard library](/reference/standard-library) function, a
[table function](/reference/dataframes) that gives a table, a group or an expression, another
native function of the project, or a local function. A boundary function and an unannotated
project function count as R functions when a native function calls them. A native function calls
an R function only in these positions:

1. directly as the argument of `Num.from_r`, `Str.from_r`, `Bool.from_r` or `List.from_r`, which
   turns the result into a ry value;
2. as a statement whose value nothing reads, which is not the last expression of the function;
3. as `stop(...)`, whose arguments may hold anything.

Each argument of such a call is one of these:

- a ry value that shares R's representation: an `F64`, `Bool` or `Str`, or a list of them;
- `Num.to_r(x)` or `List.to_r(x)` written directly as the argument;
- an R literal, such as `42L`, `NA`, `NA_real_` or `2i`, which R evaluates itself;
- a variable from outside the function that the function never assigns, such as a global. R reads
  it when the call runs, so the call sees the binding as it is then;
- a call of a base R function, or a `%op%` operator of base R such as `%in%`, whose own arguments
  are only such literals, variables and calls, as in `Expr.col("day") >= as.Date(start)`. R
  evaluates it lazily, when the outer call reads the argument, as it would without ry. A function
  that looks at the frame it is called from cannot stand there: `get`, `assign`, `environment`,
  `parent.frame`, `sys.call`, `match.call`, `do.call`, `with`, `subset`, `stop`, `warning` and the
  like. Neither can another package's function, which may look at that frame as `glue::glue` does
  to find the variables it interpolates, nor an index such as `month.name[1L]`.

The arguments of a table function follow the same rule, and so do the operands of an operator that
builds an `Expr`, as in `Expr.col("units") >= Num.to_r(least)` or `Expr.col("price") > limit`; they
may also be table values and nested table calls
([Tables in typed code](#tables-in-typed-code)). This function is native:

```r
simulate <- function(draws: I64, label: Str) -> F64 {
  set.seed(42L)
  noise <- List.from_r(stats::rnorm(Num.to_r(draws)))
  if (List.is_empty(noise)) stop("no draws for ", toupper(label))
  cat(label, List.sum(noise), "\n")
  List.sum(noise) / Num.to_f64(draws)
}
print(simulate(4, "noise"))
```

```text ry check typing strict exact
1 file checked, no problems
```

An operator applied to ry values is native. So is an operator with an `Expr` operand, which builds
a bigger expression, and its other operand may be an R literal: `Expr.col("units") > 3L`.

A native function reads a global that holds an `F64`, an `I64`, a `Bool`, a `Str`, a `List` of
one of them, or a table value. The compiled code reads the binding each time it runs, so a call
after the global is rebound sees the new value:

```r
rate: F64 <- 0.25
taxed <- function(amount: F64) -> F64 amount * (1 + rate)
print(taxed(100))
rate <- 0.5
print(taxed(100))
```

```text ry check typing strict exact
1 file checked, no problems
```

The second call prints `150`. A global that R code rebinds to a value of another type, as
`assign("rate", "high")` does without a finding, stops the compiled code with an error that names
the global and both types. With `ry run --aot`, a function that reads a global the script has not
bound yet compiles at its first call instead of before the script starts.

#### What is reported

Each function is judged on its own body, so a function that calls a blocked native function gets no
finding for the call. An R value is reported once, where it first appears, and not again at each
expression it flows into.

**An R call whose value is used.** The first example shows it. The fix names the conversion that
fits the R function's result type, when the checker knows it, and `ry check --fix` wraps the call
in it. It makes no change when the call's value is assigned to a variable that a later conversion
reads, as `width` in the first example, or that an R call reads as a number or a list, which would
then need `Num.to_r` or `List.to_r`. There the conversion moves by hand.

**An R call inside the arguments of another R call.** R evaluates an argument only when the function
reads it, so native code cannot run the two calls in a fixed order. A conversion around the inner
call does not change that, and neither does a table function on the outside, so
`cat(Str.from_r(format(amount)))` is reported the same way:

```r
report <- function(label: Str, amount: F64) -> NULL {
  cat(label, format(amount, nsmall = 2), "\n")
  NULL
}
report("total", 12.5)
```

```text ry check typing strict
native

  x this calls R's `format` inside the arguments of R's `cat`. R evaluates an argument only when the
    function reads it, so native code cannot run the two in a fixed order. Compute the inner call
    into a variable first, as in `value <- Str.from_r(format(...))`, and pass the variable
```

`ry check --fix --unsafe-fixes` makes that rewrite. It is unsafe because the outer function may
read the argument late, more than once or not at all, and computed first the inner call runs once,
before the statement. It computes the inner call, converted, into a new variable on
a line of its own just before the statement, above any `#:` lines of the statement, and passes the
variable. The variable is `value`, or `value2` and on when the function already uses that name:

```r
report <- function(label: Str, amount: F64) -> NULL {
  value <- Str.from_r(format(amount, nsmall = 2))
  cat(label, value, "\n")
  NULL
}
report("total", 12.5)
```

```text ry check typing strict exact
1 file checked, no problems
```

The rewrite runs the inner call before the statement, which is the one order native code can run.
So `--fix` moves a call only where the statement runs it exactly once: never out of a branch, a
loop, the condition of a `while`, the right side of `&&` or `||`, a function literal without braces,
or the arguments of an R function that may evaluate them late, more than once or not at all, such as
`tryCatch`, `switch`, `ifelse`, `quote` or a `with...` or `suppress...` function. The condition of
an `if` runs first and once, so a call there moves before the `if`.

**A variable that holds an R value, outside an R call's arguments.** A native function reads its
own parameters and variables. A global that holds an R value may stand directly as an argument of
an R call, which reads it, but anywhere else it is converted once, outside, and passed in:

```r
#: character
unit <- Sys.getenv("UNIT")
label <- function(amount: F64) -> Str {
  Str.concat(Num.to_str(amount), Str.from_r(unit))
}
print(label(2.5))
```

```text ry check typing strict
native

  x `unit` comes from outside the function and holds an R value, and a native function holds only ry
    values. Convert it once where it is made, as in `Str.from_r(...)`, and pass the ry value in as a
    parameter
```

**A boundary function called for its value.** It may return any R value its body builds, so its
result converts like an R call's:

```r
read_rate <- function(path: character) -> F64 {
  Num.from_r(as.numeric(readLines(path)))
}
taxed <- function(amount: F64) -> F64 amount * (1 + read_rate("rate.txt"))
print(taxed(100))
```

```text ry check typing strict
native

  x `read_rate` has an R type in its signature, so here it counts as an R function, and a native
    function holds only ry values, so its result cannot be used here. Convert the result where it
    returns: `Num.from_r(read_rate("rate.txt"))`
```

**A column read.** `table$column` and `table[["column"]]` give an R vector, so they stand directly
inside a conversion. The table functions that give an R value do too: `DataFrame.height`,
`DataFrame.width`, `DataFrame.columns`, `DataFrame.schema`, `DataFrame.key`, `DataFrame.set_key`,
`DataFrame.write_csv`, `DataFrame.write_parquet` and `LazyFrame.explain`.

```r
total <- function(sales: DataFrame{units: integer[], price: double[]}) -> F64 {
  units <- sales$units
  List.sum(List.from_r(sales$price))
}
```

```text ry check typing strict
native

  x reading a column gives an R vector, and a native function holds only ry values. Convert it where
    it is read: `List.from_r(sales$units)`
```

`ry check --fix` wraps a column read in `List.from_r` where it is read, under the same condition as
an R call's result.

**An R value written as a literal or an operator.** `a:b` builds an R vector, and `NA` and complex
literals have no ry type, so each is a `native` error outside the arguments of an R call. Write
`List.range(a, b)`, and use a `Result` or a tag for a missing value. `Num.to_r` and `List.to_r` give an R value too, so they stand directly
inside the arguments of an R call.

**A construct that is not compiled natively yet.** The program is legitimate ry and runs with the
same results, but the function that holds the construct runs in the interpreter. The finding is an
`interpreted` warning, and each message gives the way to write the step that compiles:

```r
count_up <- function(limit: I64) -> I64 {
  total <- 0
  step <- function(amount: I64) -> NULL {
    total <<- total + amount
    NULL
  }
  step(limit)
  total
}
print(count_up(2))
```

```text ry check typing strict
interpreted

  ! this function runs in the interpreter, because `<<-` is not compiled natively yet: a native
    function changes only its own variables. Return the new value and assign it in the caller
```

| Construct | What to write instead |
| --- | --- |
| a function with `...` | name each parameter, or take one `List` |
| a function literal used as a value other than an argument, a function that a function returns, a call of a variable that holds a function | define the function inside the native function, or pass it straight to the function that calls it |
| a recursive call that passes a function literal or local function that calls a function parameter, as in `nest(n - 1, \(k) f(k) + 1)` | a loop, or pass the function on as it is and what the wrapper adds as a parameter of its own |
| an R function passed by name, as in `List.map(xs, sqrt)` | the standard library function (`Num.sqrt`), or a function literal |
| a standard library function without native code, such as `List.for_each_try` | a loop or another standard library function |
| parameter types in a `#:` comment or on the assigned name, as in `f: fn(x: I64) -> I64 <- function(x) x` | write the types on the parameters |
| a call of a standard library function with named arguments (`Num.is_approx_eq` takes them) | pass every argument by position |
| a call of a function parameter with named arguments | pass every argument by position |
| a call that leaves out a parameter whose default is more than constants, operators, ry literals, the standard library's constants and calls, and parameters or local variables the body does not set (the warning is on the default) | compute the value in the body |
| `missing(x)` | a default, such as a tag that marks the parameter missing |
| a call of a base R function that looks at the frame it is called from, such as `assign`, `get`, `parent.frame` or `sys.call` | a function with an R type in its signature |
| a standard library function called with a `ry::` prefix | the name without the prefix |
| an `I64`, a `List<I64>`, a `Dict` or a `Set` passed to an R function as it is | `Num.to_r(x)`, `List.to_r(xs)`, `Dict.keys` and `Dict.values`, or `Set.to_list` |
| `<<-`, and an assignment to anything but a variable or `xs[[i]]` | return the new value; build a new record with `(..record, field = value)` |
| a local function that a branch or a loop defines again after an earlier definition, or one called after the branch or loop that defines it | define it once at the top |
| a name read before the function sets it, as in `acc <- acc + i` in a loop that sets `acc` nowhere else, or after branches that set it to values of different types | set it before the branch or loop, with one type |
| a variable, a parameter or a loop variable that holds values of different types, and branches that set a name to different types | a new name for each value |
| the value of an `if` without `else` | an `else` branch that gives a value of the same type |
| `xs[i]` with a position, which takes a part of a list | `xs[[i]]` for one item, `List.sublist` for a part |
| a column read by its position, as in `table[[2]]` | the column's name: `table[["price"]]` |
| a table value inside a record, a tuple, a tag or a list | a variable of its own for the table value |
| an operator applied directly to a table expression that a local function, a function whose result type is an alias of an open or generic type, an `if` or a `match` gives | assign the expression to a variable, and apply the operator to the variable |
| a `Dict` whose keys are not `I64`, `F64` or `Str`, or whose values hold something other than numbers, strings, `Bool`s, lists, records, tuples and tags | convert the keys to `I64` or `Str`, and keep such values in a `List` whose positions the dictionary stores |
| a `Set` whose elements are not `I64` or `Str` | convert the elements to `I64` or `Str`, or keep them in a `List` |
| a `match` guard that assigns a variable, a pattern with `NA`, `NaN` or a complex number, and a pattern other than a name or `_` on a value that is not a number, a string, a logical, a `List`, a tag, a record or a tuple | compute the value before the `match`, and bind the value to a name |
| a read of a global that holds a record, a tuple, a tag, a `Dict` or a `Set`, or of a variable of an enclosing boundary function | pass the value in as a parameter |
| a function whose paths disagree about showing its value: one ends in an assignment, a loop or another expression whose value R returns invisibly, and another ends in a visible value or a `return` | end every path the same way, for example with the value itself |

The rule covers the function's body. How R code calls a native function is not checked, so these
calls from the interpreter run in the interpreter with no finding:

- a call that passes a function, or an argument that runs R code to compute (anything but a
  constant, a variable, arithmetic of them, and list, record, tuple and tag literals of them);
- a call that passes a variable to a native function that calls R, because that R code could change
  the variable before R would read the argument;
- the first call of a native function that calls into a namespace R has not loaded yet, such as
  `tools::file_ext`, or that converts a value whose type the checker does not know, with
  `Num.from_r` or with a `List.from_r` such as `List.from_r(get("prices"))`: the interpreter runs
  that call once, which loads the namespace or gives the type, and later calls run natively;
- a call in which such a conversion gives a type other than the one it gave before. The native code
  stops there, and the interpreter runs the call again from the start, with each R call the native
  code already made giving the value it gave then instead of running a second time, so what those
  calls printed or assigned happens once. From then on the conversion's type is not known before
  it runs, so every call of the function runs in the interpreter;
- a call of a function that returns a `Dict` or a `Set`.

A name that a branch, a `match` arm or a loop body sets is read natively after it, and so is a
name a loop body reads before a later statement of the body sets it, as `last` in
`for (x in xs) { if (x > 1) gap <- x - last; last <- x }`, unless the read is inside a local
function or a function literal, which runs in the interpreter. A call that reaches the read on a path
that left the name unset stops there, and the interpreter runs the call
again from the start, replaying its R calls as above, where R reads the name from outside the
function or fails with `object 'found' not found`. This holds for native functions called from
native code too.

A native function called from another native function has none of the other limits.
`RY_TYPED_LOG=1` prints each such call with its reason.

### Diagnostics

Strict diagnostics carry the code `strict`, so they can be filtered separately from type errors. Each
origin is reported once, at the exact range of the originating expression. A binding and a bare
expression are worded differently, because a binding can be annotated and a bare expression cannot.
An R value in a [native function](#native-functions) carries the code `native`. All three are
errors, and `ry run` refuses to start a program that has one. A construct that sends a native
function to the interpreter carries the code `interpreted` and is a warning, so the program runs.

## Syntax errors

A file with syntax errors is still analyzed, under one rule: a broken region reports its syntax error
and nothing else, strict mode included, because the checker draws no conclusions from source it
could not read.

- Every well-formed statement in the file is analyzed normally. Definitions keep their exports,
  references resolve, and a genuine type error outside the broken region still surfaces.
- A broken statement contributes nothing: no names, no reads, and no diagnostics beyond the syntax
  error covering it.
- An unterminated argument or parameter list ends at the next statement, so the mistake stays on the
  line that made it. A list that runs onto the next line is ordinary R, and a fragment there such as
  `beta)` really is a forgotten separator and is reported as one. But a line that assigns is the next
  statement, because adopting it would report a "missing separator" on that line and every line after
  it, and cost each adopted line its own definitions.
- A broken assignment whose name side is intact keeps its definition. The value becomes a hole that
  types as `Unknown`, so its dependents neither lose resolution nor see a wrong type while the value
  is being edited. The hole is not a strict-mode origin, because the syntax error already marks it.
- A checked annotation on such a broken definition binds its declared type without checking it, so
  the definition keeps its contract for callers until the value parses again, and is then checked
  against the annotation as usual.

## Limitations

Most gaps are silent, because a value the checker cannot type is `Unknown` and its checks are
skipped. [Strict mode](#strict-mode) reports most of the places they occur.

Not checked:

- **Base data frame columns from anything but `data.frame()`.** Everything read out of a bare
  `data.frame` (from `read.csv`, `merge` and the like) is `Unknown`, so `df$whatever` passes even if
  the column does not exist. A `data.frame{...}` and a [`DataFrame`](#dataframes) type their columns.
- **Matrix dimensions.** Matrix arithmetic is checked, but a non-conformable product is not.
  Indexing with several indices, as in `m[i, j]`, or with `drop = FALSE` gives `Unknown`.
- **S4 and R6 objects** are `Unknown`, and calls to an S3 generic that dispatches through
  `UseMethod()` return `Any`. Operator methods such as `+.Date` and methods called directly are
  checked.
- **Packages without a stub.** Their names are `Unknown`, and attaching one switches off
  unresolved-name reports for the whole project.
- **`$` on an atomic vector in a package that defines a `$` method**, because a classed vector may
  carry that method.
- **Some paths a test elsewhere in the body rules out.** A list member beside a number in
  arithmetic, a list or `NULL` beside an operand of unknown type, the `NULL` of a computed list
  field used as a `:` endpoint, and the logical member in the false branch of `isTRUE()` or
  `isFALSE()` are not reported, because correct R usually guards them in ways the checker cannot
  follow.
- **A placeholder local**, a variable whose only visible value is `NULL` or a bare `NA`, filled
  later by `assign()` or `<<-`, where it is passed as an argument or tested as a condition.
- **A project `%op%` operator that quotes an operand** with `substitute()`, as magrittr's `%>%`
  does, and any `%op%` operator defined inside a function.

Known bugs, where correct code is reported:

- **A bare `NA` under a declared return type.** A function declared `-> character` that returns
  `NA` on one path is reported, because `NA` is `logical`. Return `NA_character_` instead.
- **A trailing comma for rlang's dynamic dots.** dplyr's verbs accept `mutate(d, y = 1, )`, but the
  trailing comma is reported as it is for `c(1, 2, )`.
- **Package-qualified calls to a package ry does not know.** `pkg::fun()` reports an unknown
  namespace when ry has no stub or export list for `pkg`, even if a file calls `library(pkg)`.
- **A vector of statically unknown length at a scalar parameter.** `p[file.exists(p)]` returned
  through a function and passed to a stub's scalar `character` parameter is reported, although the
  program guarantees one element. No type records the length.
- **A later top-level write in a package.** A closure in a package reads a top-level name as its
  definition, so a later top-level `x$a <- v` is not seen there. Reads at top level and in a
  script see the write.
